# Psalms 37-72

# PSALM XXXVII

**Verse 1. ***{Fret not thyself because of evildoers, &c.}* The argument of this Psalm, is a beaten argument; a vindication of God’s providence concerning the prosperity of the wicked, and the afflictions of the godly in this world. It is an argument that has been handled by many philosophers of old; by Seneca, a Latin philosopher, *Cur bonis male sit*, &c. among the rest, to the utmost of what could be said by a heathen, singularly well: and since that, by many Christians. There is not any subject indeed, of either more consequence, or profundity: and could all scruples and doubts be well resolved, to the satisfaction of common capacities, as well as of others; it would make a great alteration in the world, and lives of men; vice would not be so predominant, and virtue more in request. Yet among Christians, thoroughly and really possessed with the principles of faith, as of the immortality of the soul, a resurrection of the body, a day of judgment, and the like; the matter is of no difficult resolution: if any so principled, are much scandalized, it is not so much because they cannot be satisfied, as because they will not take the pains, or allow themselves time to consider of it seriously. Many also cannot be satisfied, not through any defect, either of information, or understanding, but of a godly mind and life. Lewdness and profaneness, when once they possess the heart, they either put out the light of the understanding, or at least hinder the fruit and operation of it. But we are not here to treat of providence in general, but only to consider what David says of it, what against it, and what for it. He handles the point in another whole Psalm, besides this: the 73. *Truly God is good to Israel*, &c. if that Psalm be David’s, as I think it is, though by some ascribed unto Asaph, because of his name there before it. And again, but more obscurely, in the 49. Psalm, *Hear this all ye people,* &c. and more briefly, Psalm 90.5,6,7. *O Lord how great are thy works,* &c. Divers chapters in Job are of this subject; as 24.27. and some others: it is one of the chiefest subjects of that book. In Jeremy also, and Habakkuk; the 12. of the one, and the first chapter of the other, there is somewhat. All whom I mention the more willingly, because they go much one way; both of expostulation and answer. The main of the answer is, which we therefore are the more particularly to weigh and examine, first; that though the prosperity of the wicked be great, yet their overthrow is as sudden and terrible: and more generally, that their prosperity is but a seeming prosperity; not really and truly what it seems. Why so? Because their life is but a blast, their pleasures soon over and forgotten: for their very brevity, not considerable. But is it not so with the temporal prosperity of the religious and godly too? Is not their time, generally, as short; and their pleasures, or worldly goods, as passing and transitory? Those very places, where they speak of the prosperity of the wicked, particularly, do not tell us so; yet there be other places of Scripture, many, that do; as fully, and in as express terms, as where they speak of those before; whereof see before upon Psal. 1.3. *And he shall be,* &c. Here, in few words, the matter might have been answered and cleared: that the righteous and religious, how they fare in this life, is not to them considerable, because they do not expect their reward in this life: their happiness therefore, not to be measured, by what they enjoy in this world. *And what is a man profited if he shall gain the whole world, and yet lose his soul?* Matth. 16.26. What need more, to men that are rational, and know how to put a difference between a moment of time, and eternity? Surely Plato the heathen philosopher had it not from Christ, and much less Christ, though many ages after, from Plato: yet reason and common sense prompted this notion unto Plato; and his very expression comes very near to that of Christ’s; Ἀλλὰ μέντοι ἀντί γε τῆς σῆς ψυχῆς οὐδ᾽ ἂν τὴν πάντων Ἑλλήνων καὶ Βαρβάρων, &c. He speaks unto noble Alcibiades, and says, I am confident you would not for all Greece and all Barbary, (that is in their language, for all the whole world,) exchange your soul, or, think them a sufficient recompense for your soul.

And as for those words of Christ, wherewith he begins this sentence, τί γὰρ ὠφελεῖται ἄνθρωπος; *What is a man profited?* Plato has them also, upon this very occasion, in the sixth, as I remember, of his Politics, οὐδὲν ἡμῖν ὄφελος, &c. And why should we wonder that Plato, so learned and judicious a philosopher, should say it, when Homer, a poet, says no less? But that in him ψυχῆς must be translated *life,* and not *soul.* Οὐ γάρ ἐμοι ψυχῆς ἀντάξιον, &c. Iliad. 10. Though there be, who even in Christ his words would have ψυχὴ to be translated *life,* not *soul.* Which if it were granted, yet they themselves show well, that by good and necessary collection, the same sense may be made of it. For if a short temporary life be worth so much, how much more eternal life? But I cannot be of their opinion; were it but for Christ his words, much to the same effect, Matth. 10.28. *Fear not them which kill the body, but are not able to kill the soul: but rather,* &c. where I think what by *soul* is to be understood, no question at all can be made.

If then this, or anything equivalent (as there is choice enough in the New Testament) to this, had been answered, an infidel, or a Jew perchance (not a Christian) might have required further satisfaction, concerning the immortality of the soul, &c. but this granted; of God’s providence, in reference to ungodly men’s worldly prosperity, there would have been no further question. But that time was not yet come. The church was yet in its infancy; and the full revelation of the mysteries of heaven and eternity, was reserved for another time. This was a mystery of God’s dispensation: and by what degrees that light was dispensed by God unto his people, learned men have enquired, and showed that Daniel was the first, who clearly spoke of the resurrection; and that as the sufferings, and persecutions of the church under Antiochus, and others, increased; so did that light spread more and more, and the kingdom of heaven every day more talked of among the Jews, than before. Therefore is Christ said, *to have brought life and immortality to light through the Gospel,* 2 Tim. 1.10. and Matth. 11.11. that *he that is least in the Kingdom of Heaven, is greater than John the Baptist.* Not but that of old, long before that time, there was somewhat intimated in the Law and Prophets; and talked of among the godly Jews to that purpose; but not anything clearly, nor with any certainty: of the wicked especially, that they also should rise again, and give an account of their lives, and accordingly be rewarded; this was a thing not known then, but the contrary rather believed; as at this day among the Jews.

However, that such a one as David, so godly, and so beloved, had a far greater knowledge of these things, than ordinary Jews; and in some respect, than ordinary Christians, perchance; I make no question. But what he knew or believed, is one thing; and what he thought fit for common knowledge, another. However some places there be, that seem to me, though there be that do not apprehend them so, very clear; as Psal. 17.14,15. *From men which are thy hand, O Lord: from men of the world, which have their portion in this life, and whose belly thou dost fill,* &c. *As for me, I will behold thy face in righteousness: I shall be satisfied when I awake with thy likeness.* See more there. And again Psal. 49.6. *They that trust in their wealth, and boast themselves in the multitude of their riches,* &c. 14. *Like sheep they are laid in the grave; death shall feed on them,* &c. 17. *But God will redeem my soul from the power of the grave: for he shall receive me.* These places seem to me very full and satisfactory, in this point, of vindicating of God’s providence against all objections.

But there be other places, I must confess, in number more, by far, not in the Psalms only, but in Job, and elsewhere, where the question is otherwise resolved, and the goods and blessings of this life absolutely annexed and asserted to godliness; to which, as I conceive, especially, the Apostle (whose main work elsewhere, is to prepare true Christians for crosses and tribulations in this world) alluding, says this, *Godliness hath the promises, both of this life, and of that which is to come,* 1 Tim. 4.8. Now the resolution or decision of the question upon that ground of temporal happiness annexed to godliness, without any express mention of another life, is that which perplexes the matter more, (*in fine*) than if nothing had been said at all. I will easily grant, there might be some difference between the times under the Law; and those, since the Gospel, and that improvement of knowledge, which has been by Christ: that the goods of this world, wealth, long life, &c. were then dispensed with more equality, to the just and unjust, according to the merits of either. But that there were any times, when those things, that either in this, and the 112. Psalm, or in Job, Chap. 20,21. and 27. are pronounced at large concerning the condition of the just and unjust upon earth (yea though we allow a poetical or rhetorical amplification) had a literal fulfilling, there is neither authority of any Scripture history, nor records of ancient times that are come to our hands, nor any experience of our times, that can induce us to believe.

That it so falls out sometimes, we grant, and know; but so often to the contrary too, as how to make a general rule of it, or to ground a satisfactory answer to this objection against providence, upon it; we do not see how it can be done. Of some of these general asseverations that may seem most strange, we shall particularly consider of, as they shall offer themselves to us. In the meantime, what in general to say more of them, I know not but this: God having passed his promise, (as in the Law often) that it should be well with them that feared him, and kept his commandments: and denounced heavy judgments against them that should not; which, according to the style and condition of those days, though literally by the most (and often so approved by the event) understood, had a further reach and meaning: those holy men that wrote since of the same subject, being fully persuaded (and moved to by the Holy Ghost) of the truth of God’s promises, that it should be well with the godly, &c. and either not fully understanding themselves, or having no warrant from God to declare unto others the particulars, wherein the happiness of the one, or unhappiness of the other, chiefly consisted: they thought good to set out both the one and the other, in the ordinary forms and expressions, (according to the vulgar apprehensions of those times) with much confidence (as themselves were fully persuaded) reiterated asseverations and amplifications, to work upon others the more powerfully, whom it so much concerned to be fully persuaded of this truth, that the godly shall be blessed, and do well; and evil should be the portion of the unrighteous and profane.

Such a conflict between full persuasion, (grounded upon God’s Word) on the one side, and common experience on the other, we may see in the very expressions of Ecclesiastes, where he says, *Though a sinner do evil an hundred times, and his days be prolonged; yet surely I know that it will be well with them that fear God; which fear before him. But it shall not be well with the wicked, neither shall he prolong his days, which are as a shadow, because he feareth not before God,* Eccles. 7.12,13. It was a strange device of Plato’s (a man once in request among heathens, as much as ever man was, both he, and his sayings) and for the sublimity of it, not unworthy to be related in this place; who after a long dispute, to prove that there is more blessedness in the practice of righteousness and virtue, than any could be in the contrary: comes at last to this conclusion, That whether it were so or no, yet he would not have it lawful (yea liable to a great penalty) for any man, poet or other, to say or write, that there was more pleasure to be found in wickedness, than in virtue: and that all men of authority, lawmakers, and others, should be bound, upon all occasions, to avouch it for a truth, that good men were happy men, and wicked men unhappy: adding, That if it were ever lawful, or laudable, for a man upon any occasion to lie (which elsewhere he shows and maintains, that it is not:) it was upon this occasion.

And whereas it is there objected, that well men might write, or speak so; but it could not be so easy to persuade: he takes upon him to maintain, that if the matter were carried as it might be, it was feasible enough to persuade men, that it is so indeed. And in very deed, his reasons and allegations to that purpose, are very pregnant and pertinent; and Aristotle, one of his disciples, that wrote since him, seems to confirm it, speaking in a place of the power that lawmakers have, to persuade men even what they would themselves, though of itself never so false or prodigious. In which words, it is very likely, that he had some respect to this his master’s discourse. All this you shall find in the second book of his Laws; besides what he has elsewhere to that purpose. I do not allege this (God forbid) to make any application of it: I have said before, and I am fully persuaded, that neither David, nor any others I have named, ever wrote concerning this matter, but what they verily believed, and were assured of. I mention it to this purpose only, to show, that although all that have been written either by David or others, upon this subject, will not (as I apprehend it, at least) hold, in the literal and obvious sense: yet such expressions might do good, nevertheless to this end, that they whose thoughts cannot easily ascend above, and beyond this present life, might believe this truth (a truth of so much consequence, and such necessity by all men to be believed) as they are able to apprehend it; though they apprehend it not to the fullness, or exactness of truth. And so much of this matter in general, shall serve. What else we shall say of it, shall be, as particulars shall offer themselves.

**V. 2. ***{For they shall soon be cut down like the grass}* These words may be taken either of the sudden fall and destruction of the wicked, in their greatest height and prosperity; as Psal. 73.18,19. or more generally, of the instability, and transitoriness of all human felicity, compared to grass, as here, Esay 40.6. and Psal. 90.6. See before, upon Psal. 1.3. *And he shall be,* &c. But if so, then how is the matter answered, if both of the just and unjust man’s prosperity the same thing may be said, and both come to one end? Of this we have spoken before upon the former verse: to this purpose; that such answers are grounded upon a difference implied, though for certain reasons there mentioned, not expressed: which is, that whereas the wicked has this world only to trust to; the happiness of the righteous is to last for ever. But how for ever? That is, successively, continued in their posterity. So indeed we find it expressed in some places, as in this very Psalm verse 28. *For the Lord loveth judgment, and forsaketh not his Saints; they are preserved for ever:* (what is meant by this *for ever,* appears by the opposite member) *but the seed of the wicked shall be cut off;* and it does so fall out sometimes. But eternal life is the thing (as has been said) that must make the words generally true; which though not expressed, often, (how, and where has been showed) yet even in this Psalm is obscurely intimated. For by these words so often repeated, *to dwell in the land; to inherit the land,* or, *the earth,* ver. 3,9,11,22,29,34. yea *for evermore,* ver. 18,27. is properly and literally intended not every land, but the land of Canaan, which was a type of the kingdom of heaven. Whence it is, that in the New Testament we are said to *inherit eternal life,* or, *the Kingdom of Heaven;* as also, *to enter it;* words borrowed from the usual expressions of the Old Testament, concerning the land of Canaan. See more upon the 36. verse.

**V. 3. ***{Trust in the Lord}* To trust in God, and do according to his will, are sure tokens that his providence will never fail us.

*{so shalt thou dwell in the land}* The original Hebr. goes by imperatives, *dwell in the land,* &c. Whereupon some would have it to be meant, Desert not your own station, Keep your place, &c. But I take it to be a promise, (and so the future more proper) rather than a command. It is often repeated. See upon the former verse.

*{and verily thou shalt be fed}* In the Heb. ורעה אמונה properly, *and feed truth* (as some render it) or, *be fed with truth.* Study to improve your faith and confidence in God: Strengthen yourself with faith, or truth; as the Chaldee Paraphrast expresses it. So Jer. 3.15. *And I will give you Pastors according to mine heart, which shall feed you with knowledge and understanding.* The words will bear this also; and the sense is good. But that which we have here in our English, I conceive (as most others) more proper to the place. Some render it, *proventuque fideliter respondente frueris;* that is, *and thou shalt have a good increase:* to wit, of your seed, and labor, whatever it be. Whatever you put your hand unto, shall prosper. This too, the words (if any like it better) will bear well enough.

**V. 4. ***{and he shall give thee the desires of thine heart}* See before upon Psal. 21.2. *his heart’s desire.*

**V. 5. ***{Commit thy way unto the Lord}* Heb. *roll thy way upon the Lord,* Psal. 55.22. Prov. 16.3. Mat. 6.25. 1 Pet. 5.7. Be not led by your own wisdom, but obey God, and he will finish his work in you.

**V. 6. ***{as the light}* As the hope of the daylight causes us not to be offended with the darkness of the night: so ought we patiently to trust that God will clear our cause, and restore us to our right.

*{the noonday}* Job 11.17.

**V. 7. ***{Rest in the Lord}* Heb. *Be silent to the Lord,* Exod. 14.13,14. 2 Chr. 20.17.

*{who prospereth in his way}* When God suffers the wicked to prosper, it seems to the flesh that he favors their doings, Job 21.7, &c. Psal. 50.21.

**V. 8. ***{fret not thyself in any wise to do evil}* In the Hebr. אל תתחר אך להרע. *Ne succenseas* (or, *movearis*) *veruntamen ad malefaciendum.* Others, *saltem ad malefaciendum.* Others, *utique ad peccandum.* ὥστε πονηρεύεσθαι: as the Septuagint. From whence this double sense does arise. First, Be not angry (or fret not) in any wise to do evil. Anger is the cause of many evils; and he that gives way to anger, is not master of himself, and his actions. Therefore is anger absolutely forbidden; *Let all anger be put away,* Eph. 4.31. *For the wrath of man* (ὀργὴ ἀνδρός; it is the same word in the original) *worketh not the righteousness of God.* But on the other side, moderate anger upon a just occasion, is not thought unlawful. Altogether to be without passions, some think as much against reason, as nature. Seasonable passion may be the occasion of good; as well as unseasonable, of evil. Where no passion is, zeal (for the most part) is but cold. *But when he had looked round about on them with anger:* it is spoken of Christ, Mar. 3.5. The Apostle therefore Ephes. 4.26. resolving this negative here, into an affirmative, expresses it; *Be angry, and sin not.* He might have this place in his mind also. However, the words he takes from Psal. 4.4. *Stand in awe,* &c. as has been showed there.

**V. 9. ***{For all evildoers shall be cut off}* Psal. 55.23.

**V. 10. ***{For yet a little while and the wicked shall not be}* He corrects the impatience of our nature, which cannot abide till the fullness of God’s time come.

**V. 11. ***{But the meek shall inherit the earth}* Mat. 5.5.

**V. 12. ***{The wicked plotteth against the just}* The godly are assured that the power and craft of the wicked shall not prevail against them, but fall on their necks, and therefore ought patiently to abide God’s time, and in the meanwhile bewail their sins, and offer up their tears as a sacrifice of their obedience.

*{plotteth}* Or, *practiseth.*

*{gnasheth upon him with his teeth}* Psal. 35.16.

**V. 13. ***{The Lord shall laugh at him}* Psal. 2.4.

**V. 14. ***{such as be of upright conversation}* Heb. *the upright of way.*

**V. 15. ***{Their sword shall enter into their own heart}* Their plots shall hurt themselves, and not others, whom they sought to hurt.

**V. 16. ***{A little that a righteous man hath}* This is the way that many ancient philosophers have gone, and after them some ancient Fathers, in vindication of the divine providence, showing with much wit, and eloquence, that happiness does not consist in abundance, nor greatness; or anything else that ordinary men most gaze upon with greatest admiration, or hunt after, with equal thirst and greediness; but in moderation, and a contented mind: which a mean fortune, as they argue and prove at large, is more likely to afford, than a great. So that in very deed, he is not rich properly, that has riches; nor he happy, that has the outwards of happiness; but he both rich and happy, truly and really, that enjoys himself, in what condition soever, with competent comfort and a thankful heart. See Prov. 15.16. & 16.8. and again, 10.22. & Eccles. 5.19. & 6.2. But all this granted as true, and pertinent; yet because it is certain, that there be many rich wicked men, that enjoy their means, even to the last, not a few, with contentment enough; and many, good and godly, oppressed with poverty, and other evils, who though in their greatest extremities, they submit in heart and will to God, yet are, otherwise, very sensible of their wants, and miseries; it will not therefore serve for an absolute, or general answer, to meet with all objections, in this point of providence.

**V. 17. ***{For the arms of the wicked shall be broken}* Psal. 10.15. All their strength to do hurt withal, Ezek. 30.24.

*{the Lord upholdeth the righteous}* Though they be weak.

**V. 18. ***{The Lord knoweth the days of the upright}* God knows what dangers hang over his, and by what means to deliver them. See ver. 23, &c.

**V. 19. ***{They shall be satisfied}* For God will give them contented minds, and that which shall be necessary, Psal. 33.19.

**V. 20. ***{as the fat of lambs}* They shall vanish away suddenly and eternally: for they are fed for the day of slaughter. In the Hebrew כיקר כרים, which by some is translated, *velut preciosum camporum:* which also is followed by some Rabbins. It is sure enough, that the word כרים, as it signifies *agnus,* (rams or lambs;) so also, fields and pastures. So *preciosum camporum,* or, *the preciousness of the fields,* would be the flower of the field; (to which the life of man is elsewhere compared:) precious for the beauty, though not for the use. Some think that יקר, by a metathesis, may be taken for ירק; and then it would be plainly, *the grass of the field.* Some of the Rabbins expounded it of the morning light, which soon passes away. But our translation is the most received, and as warrantable as any. In the expression, there is a tacit allusion to the sacrifices of the Law: in which, by express Law, Lev. 3. (often repeated in that chapter,) the fat of beasts offered was to be burned upon the altar, and so to consume in smoke.

**V. 21. ***{borrowed}* Deut. 28.44.

*{but the righteous sheweth mercy}* God so furnishes him with his blessings, that he is able to help others, Psal. 112.5.

*{giveth}* Psal. 112.9. And, *It is more blessed to give, than to receive,* (which was one of our Savior’s apophthegms,) Acts 20.35.

**V. 22. ***{such as be blessed of him}* Of the Lord, verse 22.

**V. 23. ***{The steps of a good man are ordered by the Lord}* The steps of such a man; that is, of a man blessed of God, ver. 22. God prospers the faithful, who walk in his way with an upright conscience, Jer. 10.23.

*{ordered}* Or, *established.*

**V. 24. ***{Though he fall, he shall not utterly be cast down}* Prov. 24.16. 2 Cor. 4.9. When God does exercise his faithful with divers temptations, Prov. 24.16.

*{upholdeth him with his hand}* Or, *upholdeth his hand.*

**V. 25. ***{I have been young, and now am old, &c.}* The words are very express and emphatical; and out of doubt, literally intended, as they sound: though there be, who because they could not fancy the literal, have devised a spiritual sense, who would have this bread here mentioned, to be meant of spiritual, not material bread, food: and others would make it an hyperbolical speech. But neither the tenor of the words, being so express, as I have said, will bear the one; nor the *ratio contextus,* or general scope and drift of the words, the other. David therefore spoke this; according to his own experience; and if other men’s experience does not agree with his, in this particular, it is no wonder. Which is a way of reconciling many different, yea contrary speeches and assertions, to truth; because as the experience of men in divers things, is divers, so their opinions; and as their opinions, so their words. *Varia circa haec opinio, ex ingenio cujusque vel casu:* says Pliny very well in a place, speaking of a much controverted matter. In this very point of providence, of the happiness or unhappiness of the righteous, &c. in this world, men of good judgment and experience, speak very differently, differently deciding the matter according to their own experience, which according to variety of times and places (besides other circumstances) may vary very much.

But in general, to make the observation as general as it can be made, it must in the first place be observed, that all such, either speeches, or promises, must always be understood with an exception of times of general calamities, and inundations, as it were: for then (in point of suffering) all men, both just and unjust, even innocent children, as others, are (for the most part) equally involved. See before upon Psal. 11.3. *If the foundations be destroyed,* &c. Secondly, by *his seed* may be understood they only, that follow the steps of their fathers, in treading paths of holiness and piety. If they degenerate, that may alter the case. Thirdly, by צדיק (translated here, *the just,*) may properly be understood the merciful, and charitable; as the word is sometimes taken: whence it is, that צדקה is ordinarily taken among the Jews, for alms. Now, though he deserve not the title of a righteous man in general, that is unmerciful, yet there be degrees of goodness and mercy, in point of alms and relieving of the poor. And, of a more peculiar promise made to the merciful, see Prov. 19.17. & Psal. 41.1, &c. according to some translations.

**V. 26. ***{ever}* Heb. *all the day.*

*{his seed is blessed}* Though the just man be liberal in his life, yet God blesses his posterity, that they want not. This David speaks, of that which himself had observed.

**V. 27. ***{Depart from evil}* Psal. 34.14.

**V. 28. ***{they are preserved for ever}* See before, upon verse second, *For they shall soon be cut down,* &c.

**V. 29. ***{inherit the land}* Prov. 2.21.

*{dwell therein for ever}* He shall not be driven out of it till he die.

**V. 30. ***{The mouth of the righteous speaketh wisdom}* Matth. 12.35.

**V. 31. ***{The Law of God is in his heart}* Psal. 40.8.

*{steps}* Or, *goings.*

**V. 33. ***{condemn him}* Or, *suffer him to be condemned*, Psal. 109.31.

**V. 34. ***{keep his way}* Psal. 18.21.

*{see it}* Psal. 91.8.

**V. 35. ***{a green bay tree}* In the margin, *a green tree, that groweth in his own soil;* which agrees better with the propriety of the Hebrew word אזרח, *indigena,* properly. And it stands with reason, that a tree so growing, should be more flourishing, and lasting, than those that grow in a ground that is not natural to them. Some would have a bay-tree particularly meant; and some, a cedar.

**V. 36. ***{Yet he passed away, and lo he was not}* The transitoriness and vanishing condition of wicked prospering men, as here by David, and elsewhere; so by Job (by them that speak there in Job I mean) is largely pressed and insisted upon, Job 20. and elsewhere. Now this transitoriness being, for the most part, as true of the prosperity of the just, as of the unjust, why here more particularly applied to these, see before upon the first and second verses. Here also, in further confirmation of the same, it may be observed, that the very word here used, *to pass away,* is the word both by St. Paul, and St. John, used to set out the vanity of all worldly pomp and glory, in general. A very proper word indeed it is to that purpose, as any that can be thought of. St. Paul’s words, 1 Cor. 7.31. are, *For the fashion of this world passeth away.* Where the word σχῆμα, or *fashion,* is to the same purpose, a very pregnant and emphatical word, as by Greek Interpreters is excellently well showed. St. John’s words, are not less emphatical, 1 Joh. 2.15,16,17. *Love not the world,* &c. *For all that is in the world,* &c. *And the world passeth away, and the lust thereof:* it follows, *But he that doth the will of God abideth for ever.* Where we have that antithesis, which (upon the first and second verses) we have spoken of, of the different condition of the just from the unjust, in point of worldly prosperity, (which by David and other writers of the Old Testament is sometimes altogether omitted; sometimes obscurely pointed at, as by *dwelling in the land for ever,* verse 29. of this Psalm: or the like,) clearly expressed. See also upon Psal. 73.20. *When thou awakest,* &c.

**V. 37. ***{Mark the perfect man}* He exhorts the faithful to mark diligently the examples of God’s mercies, and also of his judgments, and assures them they shall not lose their labor.

**V. 38. ***{end of the wicked shall be cut off}* The Hebrew word אחרית, signifies posterity likewise; but that signification is not so proper here, because it is more likely that as it is in the former verse, so here also it must be taken. But there, (*the end of that man is peace,*) it would be very improper to take it so: no reason therefore that we should seek a new interpretation here which the sense requires not; and would make the coherence worse. The same word אחרית, signifies also reward, as Prov. 23.18. *For surely there is an end, and thine expectation shall not be cut off.* So there in the text; but in the margin, *reward:* as by others also it is expounded. And so τέλος in the New Testament is taken sometimes. This I note the rather, not that the difference itself, whether *end,* or *reward,* is much considerable; but the better to compare these words of David, with a parallel place of St. Paul’s; taking the next verse also, (part of it; *But the salvation of the righteous is from the Lord;*) as part: The words of St. Paul are these; *For the wages of sin is death:* (here, *the end of the wicked shall be cut off;*) *But the gift of God is eternal life through Jesus Christ our Lord.* See also upon Psal. 55.23. *bloody and deceitful men.*

**V. 39. ***{But the salvation of the righteous is of the Lord}* He shows, that the patient hope of the godly is never in vain, but in the end, has good success, though for a time God prove them with sundry temptations.

# PSALM XXXVIII

## TITLE

*{to bring to remembrance}* Psal. 70.1. To put himself and others in mind of God’s chastisement on him for his sin, that the fruit of it might continue by frequent remembrance. So some learned men here, who though they please themselves in it, and would not by no means that להזכיר here should be the beginning of some known song, as elsewhere in divers other titles other words according to common opinion, which themselves are not against. Yet I know no ground they have for it, but their own conceit: to which if we may indulge here, why may not others upon other titles? which most of best Interpreters dislike: as before has been said, more than once, upon divers titles.

But they that send us to 1 Chron. 16.4. Exod. 30.10. as, besides that accurate English examiner of words, and Scripture phrases; I find learned Diod. does, (but that his quotations are misprinted in the late Folio edition, and would trouble a man that had no other help:) they may well be thought to speak to the purpose, and upon good ground, as I conceive. For first, in that first place, 1 Chron. 16.4. *And he appointed certain of the Levites to minister before the Ark of the Lord, and to record* (ולהזכיר) *and to thank and to praise the Lord God of Israel:* there we have the very word, that is here; Englished (and so it might have done here well: or this that is here, there: for the help and benefit of them, that cannot have recourse to the original text:) *to record.* Whereby it does appear that, as *to thank* and *praise;* so *to record,* was a particular head, for which (as in all probability the words seem to import,) certain songs, psalms, hymns, or prayers were appointed. If any such; why not those, (but two in all in the whole book; this, and Psal. 70. *Make haste O God,* &c.) that bear that title? I do not say, those only; since we find but two: but those, certainly, that have that inscription.

But this granted, that this title has reference to that place; yet it may further be doubted, what is the effect and purport of the words, *to record;* or, *to bring to remembrance.* We are told it is a phrase taken from sacrifices, wherein there were certain offerings named *of memorial.* So indeed, Exod. 30.13. Levit. 2.2. and 9. and elsewhere. This is likely: But then here again we meet with new doubts, why those offerings were called, *a memorial.* Some say, because it called into God’s remembrance (speaking this by allowance, according to men) his covenant, to accept the service of faith, which his people offer to him by Christ. Much more is said to the same purpose, grounded upon allegorical interpretations, of the oil and frankincense, mentioned Levit. 2.2. But not much to be collected from thence, that can be very pertinent here.

The words of the Angel to Cornelius, Acts 10.4. may be as much, or more perchance, to our purpose: *Thy prayers and thine alms are come up for a memorial before God.* There also Interpreters agree, that allusion is made to those offerings. To say, that this is the title of a Psalm composed by David to put God in mind, that he may help him, &c. as some do hence: I conceive this to be too general. There is no question but there is somewhat in it more than so. It is an expression of some great confidence, certainly; or of some pressing motive, in great extremity; but upon what especially grounded, is not easy to determine. By comparing divers Scripture places and phrases, especially Psal. 20.3. *Remember all thy offerings, and accept thy burnt sacrifice:* (יזכר there too, from the same זכר *to remember:*) I think it very probable, that David by this word and title, in this his extremity (which is conceived to have been some great sickness,) does put God in mind of his constant zeal to his service, and more particularly of some things done by him in relation to it, which he was confident God had taken special notice of. Somewhat to this purpose we have in Hezekiah his prayer (and that too, upon occasion of sickness, and danger of imminent death) *Remember now, O Lord, I beseech thee, how I have walked before thee in truth, and with a perfect heart, and have done that which is good in thy sight,* Esay 38.3. *Lord remember David, and all his afflictions: How he sware unto the Lord,* &c. Psal. 132.1,2,3,4,5. that too, very pertinent, as I conceive, to this purpose. See more there.

**Verse 1. ***{Rebuke me not in thy wrath}* He desires not to be exempted from God’s rod, but that he would so moderate his hand, that he might be able to bear it, Psal. 6.1. Jer. 10.24. Of God’s wrath, how to be understood, see upon Psal. 90.11. *Even according to thy fear, so is thy wrath.*

**V. 2. ***{thine arrows stick fast in me}* The diseases you have inflicted upon me, Job 6.4.

**V. 3. ***{rest}* Heb. *peace or, health.*

*{because of my sin}* Here David goes the right way to obtain his desire, and to make his prayers available, when he reflects upon himself, ascribing the cause of his misery, to his sins. Job 33.23. *If there be a messenger with him,* &c. and verse 27. *if any say, I have sinned,* &c. We may be sure, that God afflicts no man, but for sin; though there may be other concurrent causes for particular afflictions, as God’s glory, or the like; and sometimes, some blessing intended to the patient himself, as the consequent (as it does fall out sometimes: *Periissem nisi periissem,* &c.) of that affliction; or the avoiding of some greater evil. When therefore our Savior says of the blind man, so born; that neither for his, nor his parents’ sins (John 9.3.) that had happened unto him: it must be understood comparatively, not unto him, rather, to others, because of his, (he could not be guilty of any, but original sin; common unto all:) or his parents’ sins greater than others: but that God, &c. as elsewhere in the case of them upon whom the Tower in Siloam fell, &c. our Savior does expound himself, Luke 13.1,2, &c.

**V. 4. ***{mine iniquities are gone over mine head}* They flow over my head like waters ready to drown me, Psal. 42.7.

**V. 5. ***{because of my foolishness}* It is ordinary to the Scripture, to comprehend any sin under the name of folly. See upon Psal. 14.1. *The fool hath said in his heart,* &c. More particularly, such as bring diseases upon themselves through their excesses and transgressions, may be called fools; (*Fools because of their transgression, and because of their iniquity, are afflicted. Their soul abhorreth all manner of meat,* &c. Psal. 107.17,18.) because diseases are properly the rod of the Lord, and, *the rod for the fool’s back,* seems to have been a proverb among the Jews, Prov. 26.3. & 10.13.

**V. 6. ***{troubled}* Heb. *wryed.*

*{go mourning all the day long}* Job 30.28. Psal. 35.14. and 42.9.

**V. 7. ***{For my loins are filled with a loathsome disease}* The original word נקלה might as well (as by divers other Interpreters,) have been translated, *with scorching,* or *vehement heat,* (*ardore:*) those parts being subject to divers inflammations, and sharp pains, as *Nephritis,* and the like: and so the impertinency of some Expositors (and more Readers, perchance) might have been prevented, which would have David here to complain of some disease, (*quod verecundè non possit nominari:*) which cannot modestly be named: more immodest they that can entertain any such suspicion, upon so light a cause. Whether any outward corporal disease, (whatever it was,) was the occasion of this Psalm, as most suspect, and divers words in this, and other verses of it seem to import; I will not take upon me absolutely to determine. I am the more inclinable to believe it, because of the title. See there. However, it must be confessed, that there be no words in the whole Psalm, but may admit of a metaphorical interpretation, warrantable by other places of Scripture, where the miserable condition of a sinner is set out by corporal diseases: as particularly, Esay 1.5,6. *The whole head is sick: and the whole heart faint. From the sole of the foot,—but wounds and bruises, and putrifying sores,* &c. But whatever we judge of the rest, some whereof (as before is said) seem very literally to import a corporal disease: this particular verse may very well admit a metaphorical sense: and to that purpose the Greek translation (αἱ ψόαι μου ἐπλήσθησαν ἐμπαιγμάτων) *my loins are filled with illusions,* or *contempt:* since that too, the original will bear, would not perchance be much amiss. A very like metaphorical expression to this (if we like to make a metaphor of it) we have Lament. 3.13,14. *He hath caused the arrows of his quiver to enter into my reins. I was a derision to all my people,* &c.

**V. 8. ***{I have roared by reason of the disquietness of my heart}* This example warns us never to despair, be the torment never so great, but always to cry unto God with sure trust, for deliverance, Psal. 32.3.

**V. 9. ***{Lord all my desire is before thee, and my groaning is not hid from thee}* A great argument of a well-grounded faith, and a special comfort in affliction, to be confident, whilst we groan under misery, and have done so a long time; yet still to be confident, that God does behold and see: takes notice of our sufferings, our patience, our tears, our sighs: in a word, is present with us, as a witness, and spectator; when as a helper, and deliverer, he seems furthest off. It does add much courage and valor to a soldier, that is not a very rascal; even to put him upon extraordinary enterprises, and to endure more than can reasonably be expected; if his general or captain be present, and takes notice of what he does. Now from this, and other like places (see also upon Psal. 33.13. *The Lord looketh*) we may conclude, when David, as frequently, does expostulate with God, why he is absent; stands far; hides himself, and the like; how he is to be understood: There is no question, but he believed, when he so spoke; as he did here: but here speaks simply and positively, as he believed; there, by a double figure, (ἀνθρωποπάθεια, and a *metalepsis*:) as common use, and language suggested. It is not possible that men (especially, in any passion, or affliction) should speak unto God, or of God, but in the language of men: and God does allow of it: for himself (by a divine condescension) uses so to speak unto men: yet such expressions have been the occasion of many gross mistakes.

**V. 10. ***{My heart panteth}* Heb. *runneth about, or, is tossed to and fro:* meaning, that he was destitute of all help and counsel.

*{it also is gone from me}* In the Hebrew it is הם, not הוא: *they also are not with me;* or, *are gone from me:* as the words are translated by others; meaning his eyes; which, he says, were not with him, because he had lost the use of them. The sense is the same.

*{is gone from me}* Heb. *is not with me.*

**V. 11. ***{sore}* Heb. *stroke.*

*{my kinsmen}* Or, *my neighbours.*

*{my kinsmen stand afar off}* Partly for fear, and partly for pride; they denied all duty and friendship, Psal. 31.11.

**V. 12. ***{lay snares for me}* To trouble me further, who was sick already.

**V. 13. ***{But I, as a deaf man, heard not}* For I can have no audience before men; and therefore patiently wait for the help of God, Psal. 39.9. 2 Sam. 16.9,10.

**V. 15. ***{For in thee, O Lord, do I hope}* He shows a reason of his silence, because he expected help from God.

*{in thee, O Lord, do I hope}* Or, *thee do I wait for.*

*{hear}* Or, *answer.*

**V. 16. ***{For I said, Hear me, lest otherwise they should rejoice over me}* David uses here a very pressing motive, as may be gathered from Proverbs 24.17,18. of which, see before upon Psal. 35.4. *Let them be confounded,* &c. Much like to this, is that expression, in God’s own person, Deut. 32.27. *Were it not that I feared the wrath of the enemy, lest their adversaries should behave themselves strangely;* that is, should be insolent, should insult, and magnify themselves; as the Chaldee there, and Psal. 40.8. or unmercifully rejoice without any sense of humanity, forgetting what relation all men have to one another by nature.

*{when my foot slippeth, they magnify themselves against me}* That is, if they see that you succor me not in time, they will mock and triumph, as though you had forsaken me.

**V. 17. ***{For I am ready to halt}* I am without hope to recover my strength. See Psal. 35.15. and Jerem. 20.10. *All my familiars,* &c.

*{to halt}* Heb. *for halting.*

**V. 18. ***{For I will declare mine iniquity}* Prov. 28.13. *He that covereth his sins shall not prosper; but whoso confesseth and forsaketh them, shall have mercy.* But some Interpreters would not have *iniquity,* and *sin,* taken here properly, as Psal. 32.5. *I acknowledged,* &c. but, as often elsewhere, for the punishment of sin and iniquity; as intending to say, that whilst he freely bemoaned himself, and sadly deplored his lamentable condition, showing himself thereby thoroughly sensible of God’s heavy wrath and indignation against him; (a thing which all men upon such occasions are not always very prone to do; some rather, through height of spirit, affecting to conceal part of their sufferings, as well as they can; than to abase themselves, as they interpret it, to such low complaints, and acknowledgments:) his enemies, &c. This sense may be allowed of, I grant: yet I rather prefer the former, as more literal, and not less proper to the coherence. But whereas it is here rendered in the future; I would have it (with most Interpreters) in the present, or preterit: *I declare;* and, *I am sorry:* (or, *I have declared,* &c.) the words so taken affording a doctrine very considerable and useful: to wit, that a man, a godly man, such a one as David was, may confess, and be sorry for his sins, and do whatsoever else does belong to true repentance, and yet for all that find no ease or amendment; (as to the particular grief:) either not at all, whilst he lives; which probably might be St. Paul’s own case, 2 Cor. 12.8,9. *For this thing I besought the Lord thrice,* &c. but is certainly, and has been at all times, the case of many truly godly and religious: or not presently; some being put off longer, for months, or years perchance; some, not so long, yet not so soon delivered, as from the sincerity of their repentance and conversion unto God, (if not well prepared and practiced in this point:) they might expect, and promise themselves: which seems to have been David’s case here. He had found it otherwise, at other times: as when he said, *I acknowledged my sin,* &c. Psal. 32.5. but now the case was altered. And yet, all for the best, doubtless: as David himself might find and acknowledge afterwards. Where therefore God says, *Call upon me in the day of trouble, I will deliver thee,* &c. Psal. 50.15. it must be understood with due limitation, except God have a further special end in our sufferings, besides our chastisement: which nevertheless is not without a respect to some particular good to accrue to ourselves thereby; for which it is our duty, as submissive children, to trust God as a Father, though we discern not, at present, wherein, or how.

**V. 19. ***{But mine enemies are lively}* In my greatest weakness they were strongest.

*{are lively}* Heb. *being living, are strong.*

*{they that hate me wrongfully are multiplied}* Psal. 64.4.

**V. 20. ***{render evil for good}* Psal. 35.12. & 109.5.

*{because I follow the thing that good is}* 1 Joh. 3.12. He had rather have the hatred of all the world for his piety, than fail in any part of his duty to God-ward. And again, *because I follow the thing that good is:* not to commend, but to comfort himself, in his sufferings, because he suffered for a good cause, and for God. For if otherwise; he had had no reason to complain. *For what glory is it, if when ye be buffeted for your faults, ye take it patiently? But if when ye do well,* &c. 1 Pet. 2.20.

**V. 22. ***{to help}* Heb. *for my help.*

*{O Lord my salvation}* Which uses to deliver me, and I look to be delivered by you now.

# PSALM XXXIX

## TITLE

*{Jeduthun}* 1 Chr. 25.1.

**Verse 1. ***{I said I will take heed to my ways}* This Psalm, if in things equally sacred and divine, comparison may be made without offense, well deserves to have place among the chiefest and choicest of the whole book, as containing instruction of special use, and comprehending in few words, but very pithily and loftily too, the chiefest motives unto godliness, and that which being well pondered, and understood, may suffice unto any man, without any further persuasion, or exhortation; to bring him to heaven. It is therefore very strange to me, that some Expositors (of best account) should so mistake the drift of the Psalmist, as if his intention had been to make confession unto God of his sin, a sin of impatience, and over-bold expostulation with God; and that so punctually and particularly, that his very words which he had used unadvisedly, might stand upon record to all ages. A thing not very probable, I think; especially being many; except they had told us to what use, or end. But the ground of their mistake, as does appear, is this: David, as he professes at the beginning of the Psalm, had proposed to himself to take heed, that he might not offend with his tongue. But not being able to hold, burst out at last, and spoke, as himself relates. Upon which they infer, that what himself makes himself to have spoken, must needs be such, as should not have been spoken: and thereupon they seek out interpretations, of his words, to fit their purpose, which have no ground at all of any truth, or probability; as may easily appear by comparing them (an easy task) with other like places of Scripture. A very great mistake, as I hope we shall make clear; whether more reproachful to David himself, or prejudicial to the Reader, who by their false glosses is bereaved of the fruit of his doctrine; I know not.

What the aim of the Psalmist was in this Psalm, will more certainly appear, if we compare it with Psalm 49. *Hear this all ye people.* That too, a most heavenly Psalm, and of special use: as of one subject, so of one method, both. First they begin with a pretty large preface, to make the matter more solemn and considerable: as the manner of all Oriental writers upon extraordinary subjects and occasions, has been, and is to this day. Then, they set out most emphatically and pithily, the vanity of all worldly things, the brevity of this our mortal life: so short, so brittle, so soon forgotten, that no wise man, be he never so rich, or so great, can promise himself any solid happiness in this world. Upon this conclusion, that this present life is nothing but mere wretchedness and vanity; the use, or inference in both Psalms is the same, that they that would be happy, must look further than this world, and trust to God for future good, which may have the truth and reality of good, or happiness; all men’s aim, and desire. But this is more fully expressed in the 49. Psalm, *But God will redeem my soul from the power of the grave, for he shall receive me: Selah:* (see there the note) than it is here, (and yet here, plainly enough, according to the condition of those times, and measure of light; whereof see upon Psal. 37.1. *Fret not thyself:*) *And now Lord, what wait I for? my hope is in thee:* verse 7. See there. See also upon the last words, *before I go hence and be no more.*

When therefore the Psalmist says here in the preface, that he purposed to take heed that he might not *sin with his tongue,* &c. it tends but to this, (in this place:) to make his Reader the more attentive, and to raise an expectation of what he was to say. For if he that had made a vow of silence, as it were, accounting it great wisdom (as it has always been by wise men) that he could hold his peace, even when he might have spoken seasonably perchance; yet even then exercised himself, as a man void of all ambition, and ostentation of wit, self-conceit, and the like; to hold his peace; in great humility suspecting himself, lest he might offend: if nevertheless after long silence, and command of himself, he was forced by a violent inward flame (that is, by divine instinct, and inspiration:) to open his mouth, and publicly to vent his thoughts: surely we may well expect, that it is no ordinary petty thing, that he will speak: but somewhat that may both argue his wisdom, and be of some consequence to the hearer.

Much to this purpose, but much longer, is the preface of Elihu, in Job, Chapter 32.6,7, &c. *And Elihu the son of Barakel the Buzite answered and said; I am young, and ye are very old; wherefore I was afraid,* &c. and verse 18,19. *For I am full of matter, the spirit within me constraineth me: behold my belly is as wine, which hath no vent, it is ready to burst,* &c. After his preface our Psalmist his first words are: *Lord make me to know mine end, and the measure of my days, what it is: that I may know how frail I am:* verse 4. Are these words of impatient murmuring against God, as some make them? Why more these, than so many in the Scripture, as plain and peremptory to that end? But what then is the drift? what is it, that the Psalmist does aim at, by these words? If the context of the words do not, (which I think it does:) sufficiently import, we may learn from Moses’ song, Ps. 90. where Moses having in very high terms insisted upon the vanity of man; he proceeds in the 12. verse, in these words: *So teach us to number our days, that we may apply our hearts unto wisdom.* And this is the way so much commended and insisted upon by ancient philosophers and wise men, in their disputations and commentations of man’s *summum bonum;* or, true happiness, wherein it consists: showing very well, and with much evidence of reason, as well as eloquence, that it cannot be, that it should be found in the enjoyment of worldly goods: but in this, grossly mistaking, most of them; that instead of going to God for it, in his beatifical vision in another world: they place it in the soul of man, (*terminative:*) and in the natural constitution of it; endowed with such and such qualities; making a very god of it: which to make good, so much has been written by so many men of excellent parts, with so much eloquence, and subtlety, as indeed cannot be read without admiration of their parts; and yet to very little purpose, as to finding of anything, that can really satisfy the soul of man, in the pursuit of solid happiness.

*{I will keep my mouth with a bridle}* As a beast is muzzled. Corruption must be kept in by violence, Jam. 1.26.

*{keep my mouth with a bridle}* Heb. *Keep the bridle to my mouth.*

*{my mouth with a bridle}* Heb. *a bridle, or muzzle for my mouth.*

**V. 2. ***{I was dumb with silence}* Though he thought to have kept silence in the presence of the wicked, yet his sorrow caused him to change his mind.

*{I held my peace even from good}* Some think he might mean, that in great humility he did forbear in his affliction, to speak to his own justification, by telling some of his good deeds, or the like. But if it were humility in him, to forbear the mention of such, in his sufferings; it would relish of too much arrogancy, now to tell God, or men, of this his forbearing. Except this *good,* have reference to his enemies only, against whom he might justify himself, as unjustly, and unthankfully too, perchance, persecuted by them; though at the same time he might accuse himself before God. But the most approved sense is, that he did altogether forbear to speak, be it that which was good, much more that which was bad, and offensive. Such an expression we have (which will much clear this) Gen. 31.29. *Take thou heed that thou speak not to Jacob either good or evil.* It is apparent enough from the context, that there was no intention to forbid Esau to use his brother kindly, either by words or deeds; but only, that he should not meddle with him to hurt him. So Numb. 23.25. *And Balak said unto Balaam, Neither curse them at all,* (which yet, as appears by the context, he did extremely desire, and earnestly endeavor) *nor bless them at all.* The observation of such idioms, or peculiar expressions, does often bring great light to obscure places, at which many are not a little puzzled.

*{stirred}* Heb. *troubled.*

**V. 4. ***{How frail I am}* Or, *what time I have here.* See before upon verse 1. *I said I will.*

**V. 5. ***{Behold thou hast made my days as a hand breadth}* See upon Psal. 1.3. *And he shall be,* &c.

*{an hand’s breadth}* Heb. *hand breadths.*

*{mine age is nothing before thee}* Psal. 90.4.

*{at his best state}* Heb. *settled.*

*{vanity}* Psal. 62.9. & 144.4.

**V. 6. ***{a vain shew}* Heb. *an image.* See upon Psal. 73.20. *when thou awakest thou shalt despise their image,* and 73.36. *Yet he passed away,* &c.

*{and knoweth not who shall gather them}* Whether his children or strangers; and this troubles him, Eccl. 2.18,19.

**V. 7. ***{And now, Lord, what wait I for? my hope}* A hope, of immortality, certainly: or full of immortality, as the Author of Wisdom speaks, 3.4. See upon Psal. 73.1,2. See Eph. 2.12. *having no hope,* of immortality; that is: as appears, 1 Thes. 4.13. *that ye sorrow not, even as others which have no hope.* See also ver. 12. *for I am a stranger with thee,* &c. compared with Heb. 11.13,14,15,16. *These all died in faith, and confessed that they were strangers and pilgrims on the earth. For they that say such things,* &c. See also upon Prov. 10.28. *The hope of the righteous.*

**V. 8. ***{make me not the reproach of the foolish}* Of the wicked, in general, see upon Psal. 31.5. & 14.1. but here (*foolish*) more particularly, such, who by what happens unto men in this world, judge of men’s merits, and God’s love, or anger, towards them. Of whom the Psalmist elsewhere, *A brutish man knoweth not, neither doth a fool understand this: When the wicked spring as the grass,* &c.

**V. 9. ***{I was dumb}* I do not conceive that these words have relation to those in the preface, *I was dumb with silence,* &c. for so, besides other reasons from the matter itself, he should contradict himself: for how dumb here again, after he had said, that he did speak, &c. I know it may be interpreted of a renewed resolution of silence, and submission, upon better thoughts, &c. but I have said enough already upon the first verse, in opposition to the main ground of this interpretation. These words therefore must be referred to those in the seventh verse. *And now Lord what wait I for? my hope is in thee:* as a consequent of that hope. For what more available, to make a man to bear any worldly cross or affliction patiently, than to have hope in God, after this life? *For which cause we faint not,* says the Apostle: why? *For our light affliction, which is but for a moment,* &c. *While we look not at the things which are seen,* &c. 2 Cor. 4.16,17,18. Should not we take all things patiently at his hands in this world (*because thou didst it,*) in this momentary life; from whom we expect so much good, such lasting joys in another world?

*{was}* Or, *am.*

*{opened}* Or, *open.*

**V. 10. ***{blow}* Heb. *conflict.*

**V. 11. ***{Thou makest}* Though your open plagues light not upon him, yet your secret curse continually frets them.

*{his beauty to consume away}* Heb. *that which is to be desired in him to melt away.* The word signifies, all that is desirable in him, as health, force, strength, beauty.

*{like a moth}* Suddenly, or as a moth is crushed, Job 4.19,20; insensibly, as a moth devours a garment, Job 13.28. Hos. 5.12.

*{surely every man is vanity}* Though in a natural course man discerns not himself to be vanity, yet when God’s anger lays hold on him, it appears what he is. He alludes to the names of Adam and Abel; as also, Psal. 144.4.

**V. 12. ***{for I am a stranger}* Lev. 25.23. 1 Chr. 29.15. Psal. 119.19. Heb. 11.13. 1 Pet. 2.11. I have but a while to stay, and flee to your protection, therefore spare me, Psal. 102.23,24. and 119.84. See before on v. 7. *And now Lord,* &c.

*{as all my fathers were}* Protect me, as you have done my fathers, being strangers as I am, Gen. 47.9.

**V. 13. ***{before I go hence}* He shows that he should die quickly, unless God gave him ease.

*{and be no more}* Somebody may be scandalized at this expression, as an intimation of utter abolishment. God forbid. The original word, very elegant, (ואינני that is, verbatim, *& non ego.*) might have borne a more favorable translation, *and be no more seen,* or *found;* to wit, among men. So of Enoch, Gen. 5.24. *And Enoch walked with God, and he was not:* (Heb. ואיננו, *& non ille:* οὐχ εὑρίσκετο: *was not found:* Heb. 11.5.) *for God took him.* I hope it is clear enough, that a man may be said, *not to be,* or *to be no more,* according to this translation, that yet has a better being elsewhere. The same phrase is used by Daniel, concerning the Messias: but we need no more proof.

# PSALM XL

**Verse 1. ***{I waited patiently}* Heb. *In waiting I waited.* Though God deferred his help, yet he patiently abode, till he was heard, Psal. 37.7. See also upon Psal. 70.1. *make haste to help.*

**V. 2. ***{out of an horrible pit}* He has delivered me from most great dangers. The word signifies, a dungeon, which echoes and resounds with dreadful noises.

*{and horrible pit}* Heb. *a pit of noise.*

*{miry clay}* Psal. 69.2. Jer. 38.6. Zech. 9.11.

**V. 3. ***{a new song}* That is, a special occasion to praise him: for God’s benefits are so many occasions for us to praise his Name.

**V. 4. ***{and respecteth not the proud}* To follow their example; which he must needs do, that trusts not only in the LORD.

**V. 5. ***{Many, O Lord my God, are thy wonderful works}* The coherence of these words, with the following verses, is generally conceived to be this: First David insists upon some particular mercy and deliverance, (the subject and occasion this Psalm) of which being very sensible, he proceeds to a more general consideration of God’s infinite goodness and mercy towards mankind with great admiration, which he does here set out by way of exclamation, and being desirous to approve to God his thankful heart to the utmost of his power, he falls into a consideration of that worship and service, which he conceives would be to God most acceptable: not oblations and sacrifices, which most Jews of those times, as well hypocrites, as others; upon such extraordinary occasions, were ready enough to offer; but sincere and absolute obedience, &c. This as I do not reject, but rather commend to others as probable and warrantable enough: so I conceive another reason may be given of the coherence, though not so obvious, yet perchance not less pertinent: which is thus; David being to say, (as he does in the following verses) there was a way to serve God without the oblations and sacrifices appointed by the Law, by yielding (so far as corrupt nature is capable) perfect obedience to the moral Law; that is, in effect, by holiness of life and conversation; upon which follows, that in all nations they that feared God, and did that which is right and just before God, to the utmost of their power (or, as S. Peter, *that work righteousness*) are accepted with him, Act. 10.35. A secret of God’s dispensation not so fully revealed in those days; nor among ordinary Jews very readily entertained, when it was afterwards more fully divulged by the Gospel; David, therefore as a prophet, partly; and partly as one, who for his extraordinary piety, and degree of favor, and acceptation with God, was made privy to the counsel and ways of God far above other men; before he deliver this doctrine, with admiration he begins with a contemplation of God’s wisdom and goodness in ordering the means of men’s salvation: like as in the 50. Psalm also, being to treat of the same matter, he begins with a very solemn preface, suitable to the nature of the thing. See the application of these words of David, made by the Apostle, Heb. 10.5,6,7, &c. Yet for the words that are here used, those Psal. 139.17,18. *how precious, also, are thy thoughts unto me, O God,* &c. though spoken upon another occasion (but that too, God’s power and providence towards men, but in another kind), come nearest unto them.

*{they cannot be reckoned up in order unto thee}* Or, *none can order them unto thee.*

**V. 6. ***{mine ears hast thou opened}* It passes for current among all Expositors of our times, both of this and the former age, that David by these words, does allude to the law concerning servants Exod. 21.6. *and his master shall bore his ear through with an awl, and he shall serve him for ever.* One only (but of the most considerable) I find, who calls it *coactam argutiam.* True it is, that the law speaks but of one ear to be bored; and here we have the plural, ears. Whence we may well infer, to which the very context does also lead, that David had a further aim and meaning, of God’s fitting him for hearing, (since by hearing, especially comes believing, &c. Rom. 10.14,17.) by opening, not one of the two, but both his ears: to which purpose is that in Job also, *Then he openeth,* (or *revealeth,* or *uncovereth*) *the ears of men, and sealeth their instruction,* Job 33.16. Yet since obedience and conformity to the will of God (*I delight to do thy will, O my God,* &c.) is not also, only; but immediately spoken of and mentioned; it does not follow but that he might, and is very probable that he did allude to that Law and custom also. Besides that rite of boring there expressed, I would have those words also considered, *And if that servant shall plainly say, I love my master,* &c. to which the word *delight* here might have some reference. And as for that observation of *ear* in the singular, in the Law; and of *ears,* plural, or dual rather here; we might perchance retort upon the author of it his own words of *coacta argutia,* since that we had before out of Job in the dual, is afterwards repeated in the singular, chap. 36.10. *He openeth also their ear to discipline;* and ver. 15. of the same chapter, *He delivereth the poor in his affliction, and openeth their ear* (Heb. אזנם as in the tenth verse; though here translated, or at least printed, *ears*) *in oppression.*

These words of the Psalmist are cited by the Apostle in his Epistle to the Heb. 10.5. but there translated, *a body thou hast prepared* (or, *fitted*) *me.* Whence and how came this difference, is a great question. Some of great account have thought it probable, that this alteration was made by the Greek Translators (whose translation, because commonly then received, is often followed by the Apostles, and Evangelists, though varying from the Hebrew) of purpose, that the Gentiles, at whose request it was first undertaken, unacquainted with this custom of boring the ear of servants, might not be offended. And because it was the fashion of the said Gentiles, to print some marks upon the bodies of their slaves; they think the word of *body* was put instead of *ear,* to fit the expression better to their custom. But if offense were the thing that was feared, I do not see that this alteration could much help the matter; but rather make it worse; since that I do not read that such prints were wont to be made, but upon the bodies of rogues and fugitives, by way of ignominy and punishment; and not as fitting, or preparing the body for service.

But we need not trouble ourselves much about this alteration. For first (as the most learned of late have well observed) it came not from the Septuagint, it being a thing certain by the testimony of some ancients, and the vulgar Latin, (expressed out of the Greek, as all know) to this day, that ancient Greek copies of the Psalms had it ὠτία, *ears,* (as in the Hebrew) and not σῶμα, *a body.* And there be that say as much (some that have written upon the Epistle to the Hebrews) of that passage of the Apostles, that in some ancient copies it is found to this day ὠτία, and not σῶμα. But however, it is most probable to me, as to others of late, that this alteration was made by the Apostle himself; neither is it any alteration of sense, but only the expression made a little more general, and easier to be apprehended by them that should not be acquainted with that particular of the Law, of boring the ear of servants. The Hebrews, of one that was called (or at least, thought so) by God to some extraordinary duty, or action, were wont to say, (as before has been said) that he was, so and so, (as *separated,* in Saint Paul, of himself) *from the womb.* See before upon Psalm 22.9. *that took me out of the womb.* It may be this alteration might have some reference to that ordinary speech. But it may be more considerable to us, and of more necessity for the right understanding of this Psalm, to consider of the words the Apostle uses, in citing this passage; *Wherefore when he cometh into the world, he* *saith, Sacrifice and offering thou wouldest not, but a body,* &c. *When he cometh into the world,* that is, when David represents (by the figure called *Prosopopoeia*) the Son of God, as now come into the world; and so the Apostle expresses himself elsewhere, where to the same purpose he says; *And again, when he bringeth in the first born into the world, he saith,* &c. Heb. 1.6. So that the Apostle takes these words, and those that follow, as many as are cited by him, to be spoken, and intended by David, not of himself, but of Christ. So some argue from those words of the Apostles. Of Christ indeed, especially; so much we would willingly grant: but that David himself should altogether be excluded, will not follow, by any necessary consequence from those words of the Apostles. But they press us further, That the words themselves will not bear it, because David could not say (they think) as of himself, and in his own person, *Burnt-offering and sin-offering hast thou not required.* For, say they, God had commanded them, and David, living under the Law, was obliged as much as any. But if this be all, the matter may be answered; and is indeed, by Mollerus upon this place, and others; though it seems their answers were not thought satisfactory.

But what then (for it would be too long, to enter upon the question of legal sacrifices, and their end, and to answer all objections) will they make of Jer. 7.22? *For I spake not unto your fathers, nor commanded them in the day that I brought them out of the Land of Egypt, concerning burnt-offerings or sacrifices: But this thing commanded I them, saying, Obey my voice,* &c. and of Hosea, *For I desired mercy, and not sacrifice,* Hos. 6.6. But we need go no further for an answer, than this very place of Hosea. For it follows: *and the knowledge of God, more than burnt-offerings:* so that in one, and the same verse, what in the first member is expressed by an absolute negative, is in the second, comparatively only rejected, to let us know how to interpret and understand the former also. And not only there it is so, but also Pro. 21.3. *To do justice and judgment, is more acceptable to the Lord than sacrifice.* And so (to instance in another example of the same nature) Luke 14.26. *If any man come to me, and hate not his father, and mother, and wife, and children, and brethren and sisters, yea and his own life also, he cannot be my disciple.* It might well seem strange, that he that elsewhere so strictly enjoins to love our enemies, should now bind us to hate them, whom nature itself teaches not Christians only, but even godless heathens, to love and to reverence. But Mat. 10.37. clears the business where that expression is: *He that loveth father or mother more than me, is not worthy of me; and he that loveth son or daughter more than me, is not worthy of me.* So here then; *thou didst not desire; thou hast not required;* that is, comparatively: true obedience and spiritual worship, is that only worship which God for itself, and absolutely, requires or regards: Not any oblations, or sacrifices, nor anything at all, merely external, in comparison of that.

The objection therefore is not unanswerable but that David also (well understood) might speak the words. It would trouble a man more to apply this Psalm, (if wholly intended of him, as some have thought) unto Christ, by reason of these words in v. 12. *For innumerable evils have compassed me about, mine iniquities have taken hold upon me,* &c. But that also may receive a commodious interpretation, since עונתי may be translated, *poenae meae,* or *crimina;* as well as *iniquitates;* (as learned men, upon that of the Hebrews, have observed) and that there is no necessity at all if one part of a Psalm be intended of Christ, that all the rest, or the whole Psalm should. See more upon Psalm 69.5. *O God thou knowest,* &c.

**V. 7. ***{Then said I, Lo I come: in the volume of the book it is written of me}* There is not so much question of the meaning of the former verse, as there is of this; and not without reason; the words of this being of themselves, much obscurer. It would be a long work to gather all that has been said; which nevertheless, upon such an extraordinary occasion, we should the more willingly do, were it not that at the last, as we verily believe, the right meaning has been found; which to make plain here, and confirm, we conceive will be more to purpose, than to confute what has been said by others. Yet of that too, somewhat; that the Reader may the better be satisfied.

This, well translated here, (and so by all others, upon this place) *in the volume of the book;* is by the LXX. rendered, ἐν κεφαλίδι βιβλίου: (by the vulgar Latin, *in capite libri:*) which translation being retained by the Apostle, Heb. 10.7. it has much obscured the place, putting men to a search, what particular place or chap. in the beginning of the Bible, was intended, where Christ was mentioned to this purpose, here insisted upon by the Psalmist. Divers have been thought of, but not any found, generally satisfactory. But translating the Hebrew words, במגלת ספר, *in the volume of the book* (which is their proper signification) and taking this *volume of the book,* (so called from the old fashion of folding books into scrolls or rolls, both among Jews and other nations) κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν, for the Bible, or Old Testament at large, (so much of it, as was then extant) there shall we find indeed, in many places, serious exhortations and injunctions to all men in general, pretending to piety and religion, for conforming to the will of God, and keeping of his commandments: but for any that concern either Christ, or David particularly, to which those words may probably relate, we are yet to seek. It is true that Christ indeed is the chief object both of the Law and the Prophets; and that his passion in this book of Psalms, and in the Prophets, is spoken of, to which this obedience may be referred. But of his obedience or sufferings, I know nothing in the Law, but typically; and what is in the rest, to this purpose, is of a later date. And then for David, what shall be said of him? Somewhat is said, I know, to this purpose; That although the precepts of the Law be general, yet every godly man has a particular interest in them, and may appropriate them to himself: which in some sense I know may be said; but how to be applied to these words of the Psalmist, so express and so emphatical in the designation of a particular person, (*in the volume of the book it is written of me,* &c.) I do not see.

The first therefore, that began to see the right, (if we fail not in our judgment ourselves) are those, who by this *volume of the book,* understand a scroll or catalogue, wherein soldiers’ names were registered: *Porro libri nomine Legem intelligi,* &c. [That by the *volume of the book,* the Law of God, which prescribes to all men how to live godlily, (or, which is the rule of godliness unto all men,) should be understood, I do not think absurd. But nevertheless I take this to be the more genuine exposition, that David should profess himself *in eorum catalogo censeri,* to be of their number, enrolled by name, who yield obedience unto God:] So judicious Calvin upon the Epistle to the Hebrews. Now this once granted, that by מגלת ספר there is no necessity the Scripture should be understood, the words of themselves being general, which may as well be translated, *in a volume of a book,* as, *in the volume of the book:* and that by ספר any writings, evidences, bills, court-rolls, &c. may be understood, as Deut. 24.1. and in divers other places; as no man makes any question, then I see nothing why this should not be thought the most literal and convenient interpretation, that David should make here a solemn protestation of his purposed absolute obedience unto God, as one that were legally bound and obliged, by a written and registered covenant, or engagement. *In instrumento (inquit) inter nos facto scriptum est, me facturum quicquid vis. Sic gladiatores auctoramentum faciebant uri, scindi, ferro necari.* More to that purpose, in confirmation of this interpretation, you may find in learned Grotius his Annotations both upon the Epistle to the Hebrews, and upon the Psalms. Neither is it unusual to the Scripture, by mention of a book, to intimate the certainty and irrevocableness of things; as also to deliver things with more solemnity. Besides God’s book, Exod. 32.32,33. called *the book of the living,* Psal. 69.28. and the like: see Job 19.23, &c. Psal. 139.16. Neither will this interpretation of the words make the Apostle’s citation of them, or application less pertinent; if David (as his type) be understood to speak in Christ’s person. So the words may be ascribed to Christ, as well as to David; which is all can be made of that quotation, and is ordinary.

**V. 8. ***{within my heart}* Heb. *in the midst of my bowels.*

*{my heart}* Psal. 37.31. Jer. 31.33.

**V. 9. ***{in the great congregation}* In the church assembled in the sanctuary.

**V. 10. ***{I have not hid thy righteousness within my heart}* What is meant by these words, is fully declared by those that follow here: and those before, *I have preached righteousness,* &c. all, in effect, to this purpose; That he has not been slack and remiss in the execution of his charge: neither has he been afraid to tell men the truth with all convenient liberty. So S. Paul in his farewell speech to those of Ephesus: *For I have not shunned to declare unto you all the counsel of God,* Act. 20.27. The expression may seem contrary, though there be no contrariety of sense, to Psal. 119.11. *Thy word have I hid in mine heart;* to what purpose, appears by the words following, *that I might not sin against thee.* As public men, in relation to other men’s salvation, we must not hide the word: as private men, in relation to our own, we must. Or to speak yet more distinctly and properly; In the first expression, *to hide,* is to conceal: that must not be: it is a fault in any man. In the second, *to hide,* is to cherish; often to think of, and ruminate upon: that which Latin philosophers say, *in interiorem animi partem,* or, *ima praecordia admittere:* which must be done by all men, who make any use of the word of God, to their own salvation. Both expressions, proper in their kind we see: so that sometimes there may be both in the words and actions of men, an appearance of contrariety, where both the sense and purpose is the same.

**V. 11. ***{lovingkindness and thy truth continually preserve me}* Psal. 57.3. & 61.8.

**V. 12. ***{mine iniquities have taken hold upon me}* That is, the punishment of mine iniquities, Gen. 4.13. Psal. 31.10. Sin lays hold on us, when it brings us to punishment, Num. 32.23. Job 8.4.

*{not able to look up}* Psal. 38.4.

*{my heart faileth me}* As touching the judgment of the flesh, I was utterly destitute of all counsel, yet faith inwardly moved my heart to pray, Psal. 73.26.

*{faileth}* Heb. *forsaketh.*

**V. 13. ***{Be pleased, O Lord}* See upon Psal. 70. first verse.

**V. 14. ***{Let them be ashamed}* He desires that God’s mercy may contend for him against the rage of his enemies.

*{Let them be ashamed and confounded together, that seek after my soul}* Psal. 35.4,26. & 70.3,4.
