**V. 15. ***{Let them be desolate}* Let the same shame and confusion light upon them, which they intended to have upon me.

*{that say unto me, Aha, aha}* The wicked mock God’s children in their afflictions.

*{Aha, aha}* Psal. 35.21,25.

**V. 16. ***{The Lord be magnified}* The faithful delight in praising God for his benefits.

**V. 17. ***{But I am poor and needy, yet the Lord}* See upon Psal. 31.7. *Thou hast known my soul in adversities.*

# PSALM XLI

**Verse 1. ***{Blessed is he that considereth the poor}* This translation is defective. משכיל is not simply, *that considereth;* but *that wisely considereth:* *qui prudenter judicat:* as Mollerus, Calvin, and others, here: and this very translation, Psal. 64.9. *And all men shall fear, and shall declare the works of God; for they shall wisely consider* (ישכילו *prudenter considerabunt,* vel, *expendent:* as some there) *of his doing.* Neither is it, *that considereth the poor;* but, אל דל *super egeno:* concerning the poor; that is, (according to best Expositors) that judges not rashly of his case, that he is poor and afflicted, because wicked and hated of God. This (the sufferings and calamities of good and godly men in this world) is a mystery of God’s providence, which they that do not understand, are much scandalized at, and either conclude that there is no God, or they not godly, that are afflicted. But wise men make very good use of it. For knowing even by sense, (by the sight of his works) that there is a God; and if a God, in common sense and reason that he must be a just and a good God: (all which even by ordinary heathens and philosophers has been acknowledged) hence their faith of a future judgment, and another world after this, is further confirmed, because they see not, that in this world, either bad or good fare according to their deserts, and according to the dictates of ordinary justice and providence. See Psal. 92.6. *A brutish man knoweth not,* and Psal. 11.6. *an horrible tempest.* See also upon Psalm 28.5. *Because they regard not,* &c.

*{the poor}* Or, *the weak, or sick.*

*{the Lord will deliver him}* Not him, *that considereth* (though of him too, it is true enough, That he that judges of others in their miseries charitably, may expect charitable judgment both at the hands of God and men, Mat. 7.1.) but *him,* that is, the poor, and afflicted. So best Interpreters. So that David’s aim in these words is to comfort the afflicted (whether himself, or any other) and to profess his faith and confidence in that particular, of God’s merciful dealing towards them that suffer, in crowning their sufferings with a joyful and glorious issue. And besides, it is a good motive to others, to induce them to judge charitably, that God, in due time, will raise and restore them, whom for a while he has cast down. Which nevertheless is to be understood of God’s ordinary providence, and manner of dealing (in those times especially) for the most part: not to extend to extraordinary cases and examples, when God for hidden reasons, prolongs the afflictions of the afflicted, even to the last; providing them a proportionable reward after this life. In this case, God’s deliverance must be understood of that, more than ordinary, patience, (of all worldly blessings, in the judgment of many ancient philosophers, the best and choicest) and those spiritual comforts, which God does vouchsafe to them that are so tried and put to it, to be examples of patience unto others. See before upon Psal. 37.1,2, &c. See also upon Psal. 18.2. and Psal. 38.18. *For I will declare.*

*{in time of trouble}* Heb. *in the day of evil.*

**V. 2. ***{thou wilt not deliver}* Or, *do not thou deliver.*

**V. 3. ***{thou wilt make all his bed in his sickness}* You give him ease, as they do to sick men that make their beds.

*{make}* Heb. *turn.*

**V. 2. ***{I have sinned against thee}* I do with grief acknowledge that I have offended you.

**V. 5. ***{Mine enemies speak evil of me}* That is, curse me, and cannot have their cruel hate quenched, but with my shameful death.

**V. 6. ***{And if he come to see me}* Any one of my enemies, v. 5. or, some principal one.

*{he speaketh vanity}* For, pretending to comfort me, he conspires my death in his heart, and brags thereof.

*{to itself}* None need teach him to devise how to destroy me.

**V. 7. ***{my hurt}* Heb. *evil to me.*

**V. 8. ***{An evil disease}* Heb. *a thing Belial.*

*{An evil disease, say they, cleaveth fast unto him}* The enemies thought, by his sharp punishment, that God was become his mortal enemy, for some great sin committed, Act. 28.4.

**V. 9. ***{Yea, mine own familiar friend}* Not mine enemies only, but my nearest friend conspired against me. See more upon Psal. 35.4. *Let them be confounded and put to shame:* towards the end of what is there noted.

*{mine own familiar friend}* Heb. *the man of my peace.*

*{which did eat of my bread}* He means some of his own court; (many good princes, besides David, have met with such:) perhaps Achitophel, Ps. 55.13. Christ applies it to Judas, Joh. 13. verse 18.

*{hath lift up his heel against me}* Like a wild horse, to kick at me, or trample me under foot.

**V. 10. ***{That I may requite them}* This open profession of David, of his intended revenge, has troubled Interpreters, both Jews and Christians. Jewish Interpreters; though for the most part they allow of revenge, nay think it a virtue, yet they are troubled how to reconcile these words with those Psal. 7.4. *If I have rewarded evil,* &c. They have devised many ways of reconciliation, avoiding the literal sense; nay gathering a quite contrary, *that I may requite them:* that is, say they, that I may render unto them good for evil: which indeed may be deemed a good and godly man’s way of revenge: commended unto us, nay commanded by Christ and his Apostles; and even by some ancient heathens much approved. I find, that some very learned Christian Expositors profess themselves very willing to embrace this sense: yet so, as a man may see by their very words, that it is rather their good will, than their judgment: or if it be, then (to say nothing of their willingness, against their own light, if it should be so; which of the two may be the worst, and more derogatory to the Scriptures) I must profess against it. The tenor of the words is against it, besides what may be objected from other like places in the Psalms; and the consent of all other Interpreters. Neither is it a hard thing to reconcile David to himself. For he might be the same man, and yet not always deal, at all times, and with all sorts of enemies, and upon all kinds of offenses and provocations, in the same kind. The observation of circumstances is a main point of justice, neither is our justice only to be guided by it, but our mercy also: else, mercy may prove rigor sometimes, as well as rigor (a thing observed, and amply declared by ancient philosophers) mercy.

But what if David were not the same man neither, in point of execution; and yet still the same in his own mind, and merciful inclination? that is; a private man before, wholly to be guided, as rigor, or mercy should prevail, by private interests, and considerations: and now, a public man, a magistrate, a king, *a minister of God, a revenger to execute wrath upon him that doth evil:* Rom. 13.4. So that neither Jews nor Christians, need to take offense at these words, if we say that David speaks here as a king not as a private man. But it is added, that he speaks also as a prophet, by a particular warrant from God himself. But after all this, it must be added, that though private revenge was even by the old Law declared against, (whereof see upon Psalm 26.6. *So I will compass,* &c. and Psal. 35.4. *Let them be confounded,* &c. to which may be added, Deut. 32.35. *To me belongeth vengeance,* &c. as expounded and applied by St. Paul, Rom. 12.19. and Heb. 10.30.) and forbidden: Yet not so clearly and generally forbidden, as to us Christians, by Christ our lawgiver. As we have no reason therefore to expect such preciseness of words in this case, from them that lived under the Law; so neither can we draw any consequents from their examples, if such offer themselves unto us, that can justify us, or excuse us, against the clear dictates and decisions of the Gospel, without doing great injury to the Name, whereby we are called; and for which, if we rightly understand the worth of it, nothing should be hard, or grievous unto us.

**V. 10. ***{By this I know that thou favourest me}* Psal. 86.17. *show me a token,* &c.

*{By this I know that thou favourest me, because mine enemy}* This had need as much caution, as the words before, now last spoken of: or rather more. We must take heed therefore that we take them not as absolutely delivered, and make a general doctrine of them, which are intended here by David in reference to his particular case at that time: and not without respect perchance (as some chief Interpreters are of opinion:) to some particular promise, which God had made unto him, upon the accomplishment whereof, after long and anxious expectation, he might well infer, that now he knew that God in very deed, according to his promise, did favor him, &c. For otherwise we know, that it was once Christ’s own case, to be delivered into the hands of his enemies, and to hear the revilings and reproaches of profane people, *He trusted in God: let him deliver him, if he delighteth* (so ought the words to have been rendered: see on Psal. 22.8.) *in him:* Matth. 27.43. from whence, (laying the mystery of our redemption by Christ’s death aside, since that, as he suffered for our justification, so for our example too;) we learn that it may be the case of the most religious, whether prince or prelate in the world, to fall into the hands of his enemies, and by them to be entreated at their pleasure, even unto all extremity either of pain, or of ignominy. Now on the other side, if any shall make this an argument of God’s favor, To prevail against an enemy, and to be successful in enterprises; without any further consideration either of the cause, or of the persons, and their condition; and this upon warrant of these words of David: let them remember where they be, who by David elsewhere are said, *To boast of their heart’s desire,* Psal. 10.3. not to insist (because obvious enough) upon other like expressions and testimonies either of David’s, or any others of equal authority. But of successes in war particularly, see Habakkuk his expostulation with God, Hab. 1.13,14, &c.

**V. 12. ***{Thou upholdest me in mine integrity}* Either in prosperity of life, or in the true fear of God against all temptations.

*{and settest me before thy face for ever}* Showing me evident signs of your fatherly providence.

**V. 13. ***{Amen, and amen}* Here ends the first book of the Psalms. Of which division see before, upon the first Psalm. Some take no notice of it. Others do, who prefix (as Junius, Bucer, &c.) a new title before the next Psalm, *Secundus liber,* &c. and so after the 72. *Tertius liber,* &c. The word, *Amen,* at the end of prayers (as of blessings and curses, Num. 5.22. and elsewhere) implies a concurrence of wishes and affections; and is also an expression of a confidence, that it shall come to pass accordingly. Twice repeated, as here, it makes the speech more pathetical. But at the beginning of sentences, as Mat. 24.47. and elsewhere, it stands for an asseveration, and is as much as, *verily, verily.*

# PSALM XLII

## TITLE

*{Maschil, for the sons of Korah}* Or, *a Psalm giving instruction, of the sons, &c.*

*{for the sons of Korah}* To be sung by them in after-times.

**Verse 1. ***{As the hart panteth after the water brooks}* By these similitudes of thirst and panting, he shows his fervent desire to serve God in his temple, Psal. 119.131.

*{panteth}* Heb. *brayeth.*

**V. 2. ***{thirsteth for God}* Psal. 63.1,2. & 84.7.

*{for the living God}* Hereby he is distinguished from idols, Psal. 135.15,16,17. 1 Thess. 1.9. *and how ye turned,* &c. But God is not styled *living,* or *the living God,* in opposition to idols only, but also in opposition to all creatures of the world, as being the fountain of life, unto all, Psal. 36.9. in whom whatsoever is, does live, move, and has its being, Acts 17.28. Again, God is so styled, in opposition to mortal man particularly, whose life, both in regard of the brevity, Psal. 39.5. *Behold,* &c. 90.5,6, &c. and uncertainty, Luke 12.20. *this night thy soul,* &c. is altogether inconsiderable, Is. 2.29. *cease ye from man,* &c. Psal. 146.3,4,5. *put not your trust,* &c.

*{when shall I come and appear before God}* See upon Psal. 27.8. *Seek ye my face,* &c. where these words are expounded; and this translation of them showed not to be right.

**V. 3. ***{My tears have been my meat}* I could not eat my meat for weeping, Psal. 102.9. Or rather, sorrow and grief of heart (set out by tears) have so possessed me, that they have taken from me all appetite to meat: so that through the continuance of the one, and the forbearance of the other, I may be thought to live by grief and sorrow, as others do by meat and drink, Psal. 8.5. *Thou feedest them,* &c.

*{Where is thy God?}* He hath quite forsaken thee.

**V. 4. ***{for I had gone with the multitude}* It would be plainer, *for I did go,* or, *was wont to go.* He remembers his former happiness, when he had the benefit of religious assemblies, and of God’s presence in his church or sanctuary: which how much he valued see, as before, upon Psalm 27.8. by comparing those blessed times with his present condition, being then either by Saul, or his son Absalom, driven away, and far out of sight of the sanctuary, &c. he bemoans himself. *I pour out my soul in me,* says he. Which some refer to that which follows of his happiness in former times: as if he intended to say, that his joy in those sacred assemblies and solemnities, was wont to be so transcendent, as that through excess he was almost besides himself: as in great extremities of either joy or sorrow does happen sometimes. Either of the two, joy or sorrow, if transcendent, may produce the same effects. But it is more probable, that he expresses his present excessive grief by those words. So Job 30.16. *But now my soul is poured out upon me; the days of affliction have taken hold upon me.* See also Psalm 22.14.

*{that kept holyday}* They kept feasts when they went to sacrifice, Exod. 23.14. Deut. 16.14,15. Isai. 30.29.

**V. 5. ***{cast down}* Heb. *bowed down.*

*{hope thou in God}* Though he sustained grievous assaults of the flesh to cast him into despair, yet his faith, grounded on God’s accustomed mercies, gets the victory.

*{I shall yet praise him}* Though my state be low, yet God will give me cause to praise him by raising me.

*{praise him}* Or, *give thanks.*

*{for the help of his countenance}* Or, *his presence is salvation.*

**V. 6. ***{therefore}* Because I find no help in myself nor others.

*{from the land of Jordan}* These words admit of a different, yea contrary sense. *From the land,* say some, that is, *concerning;* as it was in the old English translation. As if he desired to be restored to his country, mentioning some particular places of it, Jordan, and the mount Hermon; and *the little hill,* (as it is here in the margin) that is, Sion; little in point of greatness, if compared to others: but in esteem, because of the place and situation, (and afterwards of the temple, there seated) in Jerusalem, greater to him, than the greatest. In which respect also it is said in Isai. *That the mountain of the Lord’s house shall be established in the top of the mountains, and shall be exalted above the hills,* Isa. 2.2. As for hill Missar there is none such elsewhere mentioned, or known to the Jews. Thus some, and not improbably. But others, more in number, would have David to mention these places, as places of his banishment; *from,* as it is here; and not *concerning.* And by the *little hill,* they understand the lower parts of mount Hermon: or think that he speaks it in scorn of that mountain; which though much greater, yet he made little reckoning of, in comparison of those near, and about Jerusalem. *Quod mihi coactum videtur,* says one; (and so it seems to me) though he also go the common way.

**V. 7. ***{Deep calleth unto deep}* Afflictions come so thick upon me, that I felt myself as it were, overwhelmed, as with a storm at sea, Psal. 88.7.

Or, afflictions follow so close the one upon the neck of the other, that they might seem to invite one another; as is commonly observed of the waves of the sea, and elegantly expressed by one of the Latin poets; *Sed ut unda pellitur unda, urgeturque eadem veniens, urgetque priorem.* Which does much illustrate the metaphor here used by the Psalmist.

*{at the noise of the water spouts}* By water spouts, or cataracts, he understands the clouds, from which (at such times) the water falls upon the earth with great violence and precipitancy: as it uses to run from great water spouts and sluices, where the current of itself, is strong; and the fall, great. By *noise,* the cracks of thunder are intended, which break forth out of the clouds, and are followed usually with fierce and mighty showers. Now whether by *deep,* in the words before, he intends these showers and storms from the clouds; or the billows and rollings of the sea in great tempests, is not easy to determine. The word תהום, *abyssus,* is more proper of the sea, and such secret receptacles of water, as are conceived to be under the earth. The other sense seems the more proper to the coherence. But to the main matter; either, is indifferent: the one as proper as the other. If by *deep,* we understand great thunder showers, there will be (which will make the whole the more acute and elegant) some reference between *calleth,* and *noise;* those showers that consecutively (as it often falls falls out) follow one another after every thunderclap, being as it were called for by the noise of the thunder.

**V. 8. ***{Yet the Lord will command his lovingkindness in the daytime}* He assures himself of God’s help in time to come.

*{command his lovingkindness}* Psal. 44.4.

*{and in the night his song shall be with me}* God will give me cause to praise him in the night, Job 35.10. Isai. 30.29.

*{my prayer unto the God of my life}* That saves my life miraculously.

**V. 10. ***{As with a sword in my bones}* That is, I am most grievously tormented. There is some difference of reading in the original, as is acknowledged: ברצח in some copies; in others כרצח. The one has a *nota instrumentalis* prefixed; and the other, a note of similitude. But the sense is one: The English here has expressed both; which I think better than to leave both out, as some do.

*{with a sword in my bones, mine enemies reproach me}* Psal. 35.15.

*{sword}* Or, *killing.*

**V. 11. ***{Why art thou cast down, O my soul}* This repetition does declare, that David did not overcome at once; to teach us to be constant, forasmuch as God will certainly deliver his.

*{the health of my countenance, and my God}* In Hebrew ישועת פני ואלהי. But because in the fifth verse, it is, ישועת פניו with *Vau* more at the end; which produces a different sense, *the health of his countenance,* (so it should be, and perchance so it is, in more correct English copies, not as I find it in my copies, *help for health:* for it is the same word in the original, both in the 5. and 11 verses; as also in the fifth verse of the next Psalm: except it was altered of purpose, because *the health of his countenance,* being spoken of God, did not sound so well; though even in that consideration, I should have thought, *the salvation of his countenance* might have done better: But I am the more suspicious, because I find that in the eleventh verse also, some copies printed A.D. ⟦625?; the opening numeral(s) are unclear⟧U1 have it, *who is the help of my countenance, and my God;*) there; *the health of my countenance,* here, and verse 5. of the next Psalm, where the same words are repeated. This difference has made some Expositors of best credit to suspect somewhat; *Nescitur autem adsit in hoc loco litera, quae tertiam personam constituit;* says Calvin upon this verse. But certainly if any such thing may be suspected, (See upon Psal. 25. in the end, concerning Alphabetical Psalms, Mollerus his opinion there, &c.) it is much more probable, that the fifth verse has suffered, and should be mended by the eleventh of this; and the fifth of the next Psalm; than that these two should be corrected by that one. And thus it may be, without any addition of any letter: Whereas the sixth verse begins, (as now divided) אלהי allow that word to the end of the former verse; and the ו there at the end of פניו to be prefixed before אלהי: it will be, as in those two other verses, (this here, and that other of the next Psalm;) ישועת פני ואלהי, that is, *the health of my countenance, and my God.* So shall all the verses be reconciled to one another, as in probability (being but an intercalary verse, as they call them, thrice repeated) they were made. Though this by the way; that it is not without example, that an intercalary verse should be repeated with some alteration of some words. So we shall find it Psal. 107. where these words, *Then they cried unto the Lord in their trouble, and he delivered them out of their distresses:* being often repeated, (verses 6,13,19,28.) are not always repeated in the very same words, though indeed without any alteration of sense. However, that which makes the matter more probable here is, because both in the Septuagint, and Vulgar Latin, those three verses agree, (σωτήριον τοῦ προσώπου μου, καὶ ὁ θεός μου: *salutare vultus mei, & Deus meus:*) and that the reconciliation offers itself in so ready and plausible a way.

# PSALM XLIII

**Verse 1. ***{Judge me}* Or, *Judge for me,* psal. 28.1. or, *Deliver me,* 1 Sam. 8.20. or *Defend,* psal. 82.3.

*{Judge me, O God}* He desires God to undertake his cause against the enemies, but chiefly, that he would restore him to his tabernacle.

*{against an ungodly nation}* That is, the cruel company of mine enemies.

*{ungodly}* Or, *unmerciful.*

*{from the deceitful and unjust man}* Heb. *from a man of deceit and iniquity.*

**V. 3. ***{O send out thy light}* When God (as often, though for just and good always; yet hidden and unsearchable causes and ends, he does:) does suffer wicked men to prevail in a bad cause, then the light of his providence and care of men, by which most are led to the acknowledgment of God (for *God is known by the judgments which he executeth,* Psal. 9.16.) and the truth of his promises either general, to the godly in general; or particular, to some upon particular occasions, is, as it were, eclipsed: both which are restored, or renewed again, when God does return to his ordinary course, and way of administration. And David does here invest God’s cause with his particular interest, not as a pious man only, more solicitous for God, than himself; but also to make his prayer the more considerable. This, or to this purpose, will be the sense of the words, if we take (which the coherence does here require) the words following *holy hill,* and *tabernacles,* &c. literally and materially. So also upon Psal. 58.11. *So that a man shall say.* If we follow the spiritual sense, which he that inspired David for the benefit and instruction of others after David, might aim at, though himself at that time, extended his thoughts no further than to the literal, and to his present occasion; according to that the sense will be, that no man can come to the church of God, to be a lively member of it, in this world: Nor hereafter be admitted to that sight and fruition of God, wherein eternal happiness consists; except both his understanding be enlightened, and his heart sanctified and justified by the spirit of God. See also upon Psal. 15.11. *who shall abide,* and Psal. 23.3. *in paths of righteousness.*

**V. 4. ***{Then will I go unto the altar of God}* Esay 38.22. He promises to offer a solemn sacrifice of thanksgiving, in token of his great deliverance.

*{my exceeding joy}* Heb. *the gladness of my joy.*

**V. 5. ***{Why art thou cast down, O my soul}* Psal. 42.5,11. See notes on psal. 42.11.

# PSALM XLIV

**Verse 1. ***{We have heard with our ears}* This Psalm seems to have been made by some excellent prophet for the use of the people, when the church was in extreme misery, either at their return from Babylon, or under Antiochus, or in such like afflictions.

**V. 2. ***{How thou didst drive out the heathen}* That is the Canaanites.

*{plantedst them}* psal. 80.8. To wit, our fathers.

*{afflict the people and cast them out}* The Hebrew word תשלחם is otherwise translated by most; *thou didst cause them to propagate,* or, *to spread.* And this agrees better with the other words, to make the antithesis perfect in both members; *Thou didst drive out the heathen with thy hand; and plantedst them;* (the Israelites, that is; or, *our fathers*:) *thou didst afflict the people;* (or rather as others, *contrivisti populos — thou didst bruise the nations*) *and cause these to propagate.*

**V. 3. ***{For}* Or, *That, or How.*

*{got not the land in possession by their own sword}* Deut. 8.17.

*{by their own sword}* They fought valiantly at God’s command, yet God did help miraculously, verse 6.

*{light of thy countenance}* psal. 4.6. & 89.15.

*{because thou hadst a favour unto them}* God’s free mercy and love is the only fountain of good to us, Deut. 4.37.

**V. 4. ***{Thou art my King}* Because you are our King, therefore deliver your people from their misery.

*{command deliverances for Jacob}* Deut. 28.8. psal. 42.8.

**V. 5. ***{Through thee will we push down our enemies}* Pushing and treading under, signify subduing, Deut. 23.17. 1 King. 22.11. Dan. 4.2. 2 Chr. 22.7.

**V. 6. ***{trust in my bow}* psal. 20.7.

**V. 8. ***{boast}* Or, *glory,* as psal. 56.4.

**V. 9. ***{But thou hast cast off, &c.}* Hitherto the mercies of God, and his goodness to his people, have been set out and acknowledged. And this but as a preface or introduction to a freer expostulation, which now follows to the end of the Psalm. This holy method of expostulating with God, upon grounds of his former love and favor, (upon which a tacit inference, that God will not utterly cast them off, to whom he has been so good and gracious, is to be presupposed) you may observe elsewhere also; as Psal. 89. where, to verse 38. God’s love and gracious promises to David, his people and posterity, are largely set out: then follows, as here, a large expostulation concerning their present estate, contrary to those former favors and promises. Thus godly people stir up themselves in their extremities, to hope against hope: and besides, it becomes them well, in such cases, not so abruptly to begin with complaints, which might savor of murmuring; but after such insinuative expressions, as might show their interest in God, and God’s in them.

**V. 11. ***{Like sheep appointed for meat}* Heb. *as sheep of meat.*

**V. 12. ***{Thou sellest thy people for nought}* As slaves, which are sold for a low price, neither look you for him that offers most, but take the first chapman.

*{for nought}* Heb. *without riches.*

**V. 13. ***{Thou makest us a reproach to our neighbours}* Psal. 79.4.

**V. 14. ***{Thou makest us a byword among the heathen}* Jer. 24.9. Deut. 28.37.

**V. 15. ***{My confusion is continually before me}* I dare not lift up my head for shame.

**V. 16. ***{and avenger}* Meaning the proud and cruel tyrant, Psal. 8.2. See there: It might be also, that this term might be given to a common oppressor of God’s people, as *God’s hand,* (Psal. 17.14.) to punish them for their sins, and to execute his just judgments upon them. This title might be given to such a one, thereby the better to make public acknowledgment (a necessary circumstance of our humiliation before God in such cases) of God’s justice, and their deserved punishment. *O Lord thou hast ordained them for judgment,* &c. Hab. 1.12.

**V. 17. ***{All this is come upon us, yet have we not forgot}* What is here spoken, in this and some following verses, must not be understood as intended for a justification of themselves by a general profession of their innocency; which whether more false and ridiculous to imagine (as being spoken of the generality of the people, which certainly, when at best, were not without faults that might deserve judgments;) or whether more impious (as implying a charging of God with injustice) I know not. That which they intended by the words, is, to justify themselves from the heinous sin of idolatry: professing, that notwithstanding God might seem in a manner to have forsaken them, having delivered them into the hands of idolaters, from whom they suffered all manner of persecution; yet they had not forsaken him in that kind, as to prove idolaters, but rather submitted to all manner of torments and cruel deaths, than to be drawn unto it. And this is it, which both by Josephus the Jewish historian, by S. Jerome, and divers others, is observed, (being indeed well worth our observation) that the Jews, since the return from the Babylonish captivity, never since relapsed (to which before that they had been so obnoxious) to idolatry. It does also appear by the New Testament, that they were not guilty of any such thing in those days; for neither is it charged upon them by Christ, or his Apostles, anywhere. It is very likely that this Psalm has reference to those times and things related in the Maccabees, where much is recorded of the sufferings of the Jews for their religion. It seems then, that a nation may be guilty before God, and deserve dreadful judgments, yea destruction, for other crimes than idolatry: a thing which many in these days would little think, who if they can acquit themselves of superstition and idolatry, or that which they call superstition and idolatry (which sometimes is more in their imagination, to which they are extremely addicted, than where they would have it thought, by lamentable, but groundless outcries) make themselves sure enough of God’s favor, though guilty of many foul vices, as pride, perjury, covetousness, (which is *Idolatry,* Col. 3.5. that is, as bad before God, as idolatry itself; nay, and very near to idolatry in its nature, Job 31.24,25, &c.) extortion, uncleanness, malice, and the like: besides sacrilege, which alone does counterpoise (in point of abomination before God) grossest idolatry, Rom. 2.22.

**V. 18. ***{steps}* Or, *goings.*

**V. 19. ***{in the place of dragons}* Into desolate places, Esay 34.13. & 35.7. Or, *of whales,* that is, as into the bottom of the sea, where we may be sunk or devoured, Jon. 2.3. See the power of faith, which can be overcome by no perils.

*{dragons}* Or, *whales*, as Gen. 1.21. Psal. 73.13.

**V. 21. ***{Shall not God search this out?}* They take God to witness, to make their protestation of their innocency in this kind, the more solemn and considerable. And so we may safely, without fear of taking God’s holy name in vain, when it is in a matter of consequence, and that we may do it with a clear conscience: which does presuppose a serious examination of our own hearts, which otherwise are apt enough to deceive us, or the Devil, rather, them; if great care be not taken. But in point of idolatry, this appealing to God, *the searcher of the hearts,* is the more seasonable, because we read in the Law of a twofold idolatry, public and apparent, set down, with the punishment of it, Deut. 13.12,13,14. &c. *If thou hear say in one of thy cities,* &c. and private, or secret idolatry, liable not to the Law (as long as secret) but to those solemn curses, (that is, to the immediate judgment of God) pronounced upon Mount Ebal, Deut. 27.15. *Cursed be the man that maketh any graven or molten image,* &c. *and putteth it in a secret place,* &c. See also 2 Kings 17.9. *And the children of Israel did secretly those things that were not right,* &c. But how secretly, since it is said there, that *they set them up images and groves in every high hill,* &c. It is likely, that they began with secret idolatry, colored with many plausible pretences, before they proceeded unto public. But this by the way only, so far as may concern us here. But the words of Ezekiel are very pertinent, which shall here be set down, for further confirmation of the reasonableness of this interposing of God as a witness, in this solemn protestation against idolatry: *Then said he unto me, Son of man, hast thou seen what the ancients of the house of Israel do in the dark, every man in the chambers of his imagery? For they say, The Lord seeth us not, the Lord hath forsaken the earth,* Ezek. 8.12. It is not for nothing then, that they not only call to God for witness here, but profess also to believe, that he *knoweth the secrets of the hearts.*

**V. 22. ***{Yea, for thy sake are we killed all the day long}* Rom. 8.36.

*{for thy sake are we killed}* The faithful make this their comfort, That the wicked punish them not for their sins, but for God’s cause, Mat. 5.10. 1 Pet. 4.14.

**V. 23. ***{Awake, why sleepest thou O Lord?}* Psal. 7.6. & 35.23. & 59.4. & 78.65. There is no hope of recovery, except you put to your hand, and raise us up.

**V. 24. ***{Wherefore hidest thou thy face?}* Psal. 10.1.

**V. 25. ***{soul is bowed down to the dust}* Psal. 119.25.

**V. 26. ***{for our help}* Heb. *a help for us.*

*{for thy mercies sake}* Which are the only sufficient means to deliver soul and body from all kind of slavery and misery.

# PSALM XLV

## TITLE

*{Shoshannim}* An instrument of six strings; as some would have it: as others, the beginning of some known song. The word properly signifies *lilies,* or *roses:* which might have some reference to the argument of this Psalm, as some are of opinion. But concerning these titles, all is uncertain, as often has been said. See upon former Psalms, 7, &c.

*{Maschil}* Or, *of instruction.*

*{A song of loves}* Of the love betwixt Solomon and Pharaoh’s daughter, typifying the love of Christ and the church; or, *of the beloved ones*, kings’ daughters attending on the bride, ver. 9. In the Hebrew it is שיר ידידות and because Solomon was called *Jedidiah,* as we read 2 Sam. 12.25. some Rabbins, and others, have thought that Solomon might be intended by this word. This is certain, that many such allusions, with some little alteration in the proper names, are to be found in the Scripture. See before upon Psal. 7. upon those words of the title there, *Cush the Benjamite;* and upon the title of the 9. Psalm. But whatever we think of that word in the title, a greater question is made concerning the argument of the Psalm itself; which some are so far from yielding, that it was composed upon Solomon’s marriage, and immediately applicable to him, (though chiefly intended of Christ, as all Christians maintain) that they think it great impiety for any man to think so. The Genevenses by name are charged by Genebrard: *Impii, ut Genevenses, de Solomone hunc Psalmum exponunt,* says he. Now his reasons why it cannot be so; the first is, because the sons of Chore, the authors of this Psalm, as he takes for granted, did prophesy in the time of Moses, many ages before Solomon. And though prophets, yet that God would have Solomon an object of their prophecies rather than David, or any other, I confess is not very likely, except we had more assurance of it. But what can Genebrard say to us, why we should believe, that these sons of Corah here mentioned, lived in Moses his time; whom all others conceive to have been of their posterity in David’s, and since David’s time? S. Jerome by name, makes them to have been keepers of the doors, and several porches of the Tabernacle in David’s time. But this is a mere fancy of Genebrard’s, only to serve his present purpose. 2. That the expressions of this Psalm are more sublime, than to belong unto any terrestrial King: neither is this a sufficient argument, since it is acknowledged, that Christ is chiefly intended, and that there be other Psalms where as high things are spoken of David, immediately; though belonging to Christ more properly. A third argument, is, that the Apostles apply it to Christ. So they do divers other things, which nevertheless were literally spoken of others, as all grant. Their application of things to Christ, does not take away the literal sense. That it is against the sense or opinion of all Christians; (his fourth argument:) how false that is, I will appeal to Lorinus, who though he name not Genebrard, yet he names others that have said as much as he, and confutes them by divers opposite authorities, besides his own. Lastly, he alleges the Rabbins; but that also not truly: or else S. Jerome was not in the right, who is cited by the same Lorinus to the contrary: though I will easily yield, that all Rabbins are not of one opinion about it; as neither in many other things, belonging to the Psalms. Taking it therefore for granted, that Solomon was the immediate object, and that this Psalm was intended to him as an *Epithalamium,* or marriage-song; I think their interpretation very probable and almost unquestionable, who translating the words according to the most grammatical construction, *carmen dilectarum,* (for it is a feminine) understand thereby certain choice virgins (according to the custom of those times) appointed to compass about, or accompany the bride; and to sing this Psalm, properly called *the beloved,* or, *the friends,* as they that waited on the bridegroom, were properly called, οἱ φίλοι τοῦ νυμφίου: *the friends of the bridegroom,* Joh. 3.29. though it be there in the singular, ὁ φίλος, *the friend;* because S. John Baptist speaks of himself, in particular: but under that one, more may be comprehended. And Mat. 9.15. the same are called in the plural, οἱ υἱοὶ τοῦ νυμφῶνος, *the children of the bridechamber:* of which see more there in learned Grotius his Annotations. Thus much of the type. Now how fitly Christ, and his relation to his church, were represented unto us under this type of a bridegroom, &c. see more upon Canticles.

**Verse 1. ***{Is inditing}* Heb. *boileth, or bubbleth up.*

**V. 2. ***{Thou art fairer than the children of men}* Solomon’s beauty and eloquence to win favor with his people, and his power to overcome his enemies, is here described.

*{grace is poured into thy lips}* Power to speak learnedly, and elegantly, Esay 50.4.

**V. 4. ***{ride prosperously}* *prosper thou, ride thou.*

*{because of truth}* He alludes to them that ride in chariots, in their triumphs; showing, that the quiet state of a kingdom, stands in truth, meekness, and justice, not in worldly pomp and vanity.

**V. 6. ***{Thy throne, O God}* Heb. אלהים: a title often ascribed to judges and magistrates, as all grant: but some, both here, and upon Heb. 1.8. where these words are quoted, and applied to Christ, observed, that although אלהים (*God,* or *Gods*) be sometimes ascribed to men, or Angels, yet never to any one under God, singly, or personally; but to more than one, always. But I doubt whether the observation be solid: For Judg. 13. vers. 22. it is directly, (as appears by the foregoing verse) ascribed to one single Angel: and if to one single Angel, then as well to a single man, especially such a one as Solomon was accounted, though perchance no other examples occur of it elsewhere.

*{Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever}* Heb. 1.8. Under this figure of this kingdom of justice, is set forth the everlasting kingdom of Christ. See more upon the 16 verse, *whom thou mayst make princes,* &c.

**V. 7. ***{thy God hath anointed thee}* Esay 61.1. Has established your kingdom, as the figure of Christ’s; wherein is peace and joy.

*{above his fellows}* Heb. 1.9. & Joh. 3.34. *For God giveth not the Spirit by measure unto him:* which may seem to be spoken with reference to the Psalmist, expressed in plainer words.

**V. 8. ***{smell of myrrh, and aloes, and cassia}* That the word *aloes,* came from the Hebrew word in the text, there is no question; and therefore we are right enough for the word: but what is the right aloes, here intended, is not so easily resolved. However, somewhat odoriferous, all agree; which is more than we can say of the next word, which by some is translated *plicas,* or *angulos;* the folds, or corners: A learned Rabbin I know, is the author of that interpretation; but besides his authority, I see not much for it, neither is it followed by many. A chief reason that moved him thereunto, it seems, is, the defect of an *and,* in the original, to join the word with the former. But that has sufficiently been answered, and examples produced of the like construction. But yet what *cassia* properly is, is not certainly known. Till we know more certainly, we may take it for a kind of cinnamon, as most do.

*{ivory palaces}* Either edifices, 1 Kings 22.39. Cant. 7.4. or ivory coffers, and wardrobes, whence those garments were taken, and where kept.

*{whereby they have made thee glad}* By the gift of those precious things mentioned; as some expound it. But rather, *out of which;* or, *from which* (*prodeunti scilicet;* upon your coming abroad, and solemn appearance; as Psal. 19.5. *Which as a bridegroom coming out of his chamber:* See more there) *they* (or those that came to meet you) *have* (by their applause and manifold congratulations) *made thee glad.* Some translate it, *more than theirs, that make thee glad;* that is, as they expound it; your garments are more odoriferous, than theirs, that make you glad: that is, your fellows; as verse 7. The word מני in the original, will bear it, even this too, well enough, which signifies *prae;* as well as *de,* or *ex:* but I do not like that sense so well. Others, again, because *Minni* is the name of a province, Jerem. 51.27. which divers understand to be *Armeniam minorem;* they make a proper name of it here too. So Jerome, and the Chaldee Paraphrase, which the *Psalterium Nebiense* does exhibit: *à palatiis quae constructa dentibus elephantorum de Armenia;* are his words, as there interpreted. But after all this; why not, *whereby;* that is, by which odoriferous smells, and perfumes, or ointments: which have a natural power, both to revive, and to increase the spirits of man, as physicians teach? whereof among others, see Mars. Ficinus, *de Sani.* l. 2. c. 18. *De nutrimento spiritus, & conservatione vitae per odores,* out of Galen and others. And what can be more plain, or pertinent, than Prov. 27.7. *Ointment and perfume rejoice the heart?* This as I conceive to be the most literal; so I should say, the only true sense, but that I have no authority for it, but mine own. I leave it therefore to the reader, to judge. But there be, and those of the most approved, as Diodati by name, that expound all this of Christ, and make that the literal, (though the expression be metaphorical) and immediate meaning, taking no notice of any other, in reference to Solomon, and his marriage. So here, by *garment,* &c. they interpret, the gifts of the Holy Ghost, which spread forth a most sweet odor of grace and power: and *out of the ivory palaces,* that is, say they, from heaven, which is Christ’s royal habitation; *make thee glad,* namely, with everlasting and heavenly joy, Heb. 12.2. But for my part, as I believe that Christ was the chief aim of the Psalm, in general, to set out by it, the glory of his kingdom, and the happiness of the church under him, and the near relation of Christ to the church, and of the church to Christ: yet my opinion is, (and I see most others of ours, most in request, go that way, who though they apply it to Christ also, some one way, some another, yet ground upon a literal sense with reference to Solomon;) that all particulars are not so well applicable to Christ, except we take much liberty: as for example, the particulars of this verse: at least, that they are not so properly applicable to Christ, as to Solomon. Now if we go to allegories, and remote applications, there is great liberty left to every man’s fancy, and it will be very hard to pitch upon anything, that everybody will think to be proper and solid. As for example, others interpret these garments, of *the garments of salvation,* and *the robe of righteousness,* Esay 61.10. wherewith Christ does clothe his church. And the fragrancy or sweet smell of those garments they make to be the evangelical doctrine concerning justification and remission of sins in Christ. And then by these ivory palaces, some understand, not heaven, but beautiful and magnificent churches, erected to the honor and glory of Christ. Thus every man may indulge to his fancies, and not exceed, perchance, the liberty of allegorical expositions: but much beyond the compass, as I conceive, and purpose, of such notes as these.

**V. 13. ***{The King’s daughter is all glorious within}* Of the mystical sense, and application, either to the church in general, oftentimes in the sight of God and his Angels; both in its living saints, by their holy conversation; and in its dying martyrs, by their patient deaths and sufferings in several kinds. *Most glorious,* when most oppressed and trampled upon by men: Or to every particular member of it, whose beauty and excellency is not external, but internal, in the several endowments and qualifications of the soul, both natural and divine, or infused. Of this mystical sense and application, I say, there is no question. It is very common and ordinary, in all writers, both ancient and late. But because I make this but the secondary sense, not immediately intended by the prophet (whoever he was) and holy penman, though chief in the intention of the Holy Ghost: we must inquire into the literal, of which there is more question among interpreters. *Within,* that is, not to be seen, whilst the bride, is yet kept close, and expected abroad, by the company, with great earnestness, (a good part of the solemnity, according to the custom of those times among the Hebrews. See upon Psal. 19.5. *Which is as a bridegroom:*) until the prefixed time of her solemn coming forth and appearance. So some. Others, by *within,* understand the sight and presence of the bridegroom; in their inmost rooms and palaces, into which none have admittance, but the bride alone: wherein consists her chiefest glory and happiness. Others again; *within,* say they; that is, not according to the fashion of vainglorious people, who if they make a good show abroad, care not how meanly they are attired at home: but she, both without, and *within,* and more *within* than without, is such and such. I bring divers expositors, because I would be sure not to want a literal sense; which some because they cannot readily find, betake themselves to the mystical and allegorical, as the only. But that which I like best, is yet behind. How ordinary is it, even among profane poets and authors, to compare mental perfections, with external beauty; and to prefer those, as of themselves more considerable, and more lasting? *Forma bonum fragile est,* (one of them) *quantumque accedit,* &c. *Jam molire animum, qui duret,* &c. So this sacred poet, though he set out the external glory of this bride, with all imaginable magnificence; yet her chiefest glory and beauty he does ascribe unto her soul, her better part; which comes nearest to the mystical, and is as proper to the literal sense.

**V. 16. ***{Instead of thy fathers, shall be thy children}* It is generally conceived, that these words are directed unto Solomon himself, not to his bride, because of the masculine affixes, or pronouns, in the original: and the sense to be this, that his posterity shall not degenerate, but equal their fathers, in wisdom, prudence, piety, and all true nobility. Indeed if the words be directed to Solomon, I know not what else to make of them. But because it is not without example, that a masculine should be put for a feminine; as upon this very place is well observed by some; it were better, perchance, to take them as directed to the same, to whom the former from v. 10. (most of them) are directed, or, at least, belonging; to wit, the bride. So the meaning would be, which I conceive would fit the place much better, to persuade her not to take too much to heart, (which many women in this case of marriage are apt to do, and sometimes it is the cause of much trouble to them and theirs) that she is brought away from her kindred and parents, father, mother, &c. and that all these she must now forsake; because instead of these, she shall have children, of whom she may expect as much and more comfort, than from any parents.

*{whom thou mayest make Princes in all the earth}* This indeed cannot be applied unto Solomon, but as spoken very hyperbolically. Not but that it is lawful enough for poets and orators, especially in such an argument as this, to use such figures: (though indeed the hyperbole was not so much in the first penning, since so much, of so glorious a King as Solomon was, and coming to the Crown with so much expectation, by way of good omen, might be said well enough: we know of kings within memory, who by marriages have had children princes in divers parts of the earth: not in the first penning therefore I say, so much, as in the application of it to the event) but considering our poet here, as a prophet inspired by God; to whom things future, were present; and then looking into the story, how it fared with Solomon’s posterity; so indeed we may say, that of Solomon it is very hard to make good the words, without the liberty of a very large hyperbole. But to Christ, the application is very proper and obvious: who by the preaching of his ministers, having converted nations and kingdoms to his obedience, may, in that respect, be said to have made them, and with them, those that have been converted by them, princes in all the earth. For unto all true Christians, an eternal kingdom we know, is appointed and prepared by Christ in heaven. So divers; of the ancients especially. But in another respect too, and more literal, as I conceive, the words are applicable. For as many kings and princes, as either at the first were converted, or since that, have professed Christianity; as literally, they were princes, truly and really, so as truly and really, though spiritually, Christ his offspring. Even the same both kings and queens, who in regard of their temporals, are called *nursing-fathers* and *nursing-mothers* of the church. Esay 49.23. *And kings shall be thy nursing-fathers,* &c. are spiritually the church’s, and Christ’s children. As of these, so we may say of those words in the first verse, *Thy throne, O God, is for ever and ever,* &c. that they belong unto Christ more immediately and properly than unto Solomon. But see more there.

# PSALM XLVI

## TITLE

*{for}* Or, *of.*

*{Alamoth}* It may be a Psalm for the virgins to sing at Solomon’s wedding; see Psal. 45.1. and verse 9. of this Psalm: or, on the virginals, instruments which virgins played on: or, to be sung to the treble tune, 1 Chron. 15.20. which is shrill like a virgin’s voice.

**Verse 1. ***{A very present help in trouble}* Heb. *an help in troubles found much.*

*{in trouble}* In all manner of troubles, God shows his speedy mercy and power in defending his.

**V. 2. ***{will we not fear, though the earth be removed}* There is a provident fear, of which the Wise man speaks, Prov. 14.16. *A wise man feareth,* &c. which elsewhere he expresses, *A prudent man foreseeth,* &c. Prov. 22.3. And there is a natural fear, as the fear of death, and of sudden chances, by fire, water, and the like; which things may be terrible to the best, and most resolute, without any disparagement, either to their courage, or to their faith. Religion does not destroy nature, or take away sense; which it does, if it take away all fear in such cases. And therefore Aristotle, the most rational of mere natural men whose writings are come to our hands, his resolution upon the case, is, that whoever does not fear μήτε σεισμὸν, μήτε τὰ κύματα; neither earthquake, nor sudden inundations; (or perils upon the water) is either mad, or senseless, *Ethic. Nic.* 3.7. I make no question, but St. Paul was afraid in that tempest upon the sea, described, Act. 24. which (besides other circumstances) may be gathered from the words of the Angel to him, *Paul, fear not,* ver. 24. But then there is a godless and comfortless fear; when there is no hope, either of present deliverance, nor of future good to ensue: no sense of any trust, or confidence in his mercy, whose praise and promise is, in times of greatest danger, not utterly to forsake them, and to leave them comfortless, that put their trust in him. Of this kind of fear, those words of the Psalmist must be understood, not of natural fear. He that said, *Si fractus illabatur orbis, impavidum ferient ruinae:* spoke finely, but spoke only, and no more. But whether all this, of *the earth being removed,* &c. be to be understood literally; or whether by all this, nothing else but troubles and commotions of estates, wars, and the effects of wars; great armies, &c. metaphorically be meant: See more upon the 4. verse.

*{not fear}* What the Psalmist here, by, *not fearing;* the Prophet Habakkuk does express by *rejoicing:* intending one thing both, that they would not so fear, but that they should be sensible of some comfort. Hab. 3.17, &c. *Though the fig-tree,* &c. So the Apostle forbids us to *sorrow for them that are dead,* &c. 1 Thess. 4.13.

*{the midst of the sea}* Heb. *the heart of the sea.*

**V. 3. ***{Though the waters thereof roar}* Matth. 7.25. Psal. 93.3,4. Jer. 5.22. Though afflictions be never so violent, yet God’s people look for help from God.

**V. 4. ***{There is a river, the streams whereof shall make glad the city of God}* To the frights and fears, and confusions of worldly men, in time of trouble and public dangers, implied, though not expressed in the former verses, he now opposes the peace and security of the church and people of God; because God is with them, *in the midst of them,* &c. And so far the coherence is plain: but how this river comes in, whether as opposed to the roaring billows of the sea, before spoken of; or, as any means of this peace and security, of either city, or men, insisted upon afterwards, is not so easily known. Neither will it serve to make good the coherence, that it be opposed to the sea, barely; (as a still river, and a roaring sea) except it be opposed to it, in reference to this point of fear, and no fear, the chief subject of the words. Although, say they, all be never so full of rage and tumult abroad, and that nations and kingdoms be laid waste; yet shall the city of God, in the meantime, enjoy her peace and tranquillity, contenting itself with her little streams. But it does not yet appear where the antithesis does lie, or to what purpose this river is mentioned. But then it is further said, that great rivers are a great safeguard and protection to towns, and that the prophet Esay does upbraid the Jews of their infidelity, Esay 8.6. for that they despised their own rivers, as unsafe and useless, because of their smallness; and commended such as Nilus and Euphrates, which for their greatness and swiftness could keep out an enemy, and secure towns. This indeed makes the antithesis clear: but it is a great deal, to be supplied and presupposed; and more indeed, than I can well gather out of that passage of Esay, *Forasmuch as this people refuseth the waters of Shiloah, that go softly, and rejoice in Rezin and Remaliah’s son: Now therefore behold,* &c. Here I find (as best interpreters there) a small army, (or contemptible means in sight) compared to soft waters; and a great potent army, to an overflowing torrent, &c. but nothing to that purpose, as is alleged. It is ordinary among all men, to compare great armies to great inundations; and by an opposite metaphor, (which otherwise would be obscure, because not so ordinary) a less army, or strength, is here compared to a little river. If therefore we interpret the Psalmist here, from that passage of Esay, (which may be, whether the author of this Psalm wrote before Esay, or Esay before him, which is uncertain) then taking the former words not literally, but as an allegorical expression of great stirs, and great armies in the world, the reason of the antithesis will require, that by a river, (opposed to the sea, &c.) small means and small forces (in comparison, and in appearance) should be understood. But this upon a supposition, that the Psalmist would be interpreted by Esay; which is the more likely, because wars are directly mentioned afterwards, and God, in relation to it, as is probable, styled, *The Lord of Hosts,* ver. 7. & 11. But again; there may be a further antithesis in this, that whereas the neighborhood of the sea, or of great waters, may be the overthrow of great mountains; mount Sion, (the type of the church) the further it stood from any such, the more it might be conceived out of danger. Now applying these things to the church of God in general, as most interpreters do, who hence infer, (and insist at large upon it, some of them) that notwithstanding the troubles and commotions of kingdoms and estates, the church, by the power and providence of God, may enjoy her peace and tranquillity; this must be understood with due limitation. For otherwise, the saying of an ancient is true, that, *Non Respublica in Ecclesia, sed Ecclesia in Republica;* that is, That the commonwealth is not in the church, but the church in the commonwealth: and as part of it, it is like enough as to enjoy the peace; so to partake of the troubles and distempers of it. Which is one reason why the church, in what state soever collected, is enjoined to pray for the peace and prosperity of that state; *For kings, and for all that are in authority; that we may lead a quiet and a peaceable life, in all godliness and honesty,* 1 Tim. 2.2. as is well observed, and pressed by the same ancient author. However, it is true too, and observed by many, that by a secret power and providence of God, the church of God, though it suffer for the present, as part; yet in process of time, it often gains and increases by the troubles and commotions of states and kingdoms. This God, who can bring light out of darkness, can do: but it is not the way that he has appointed for the preservation of his church; and we must pray against temptations, though sometimes used by God, as means of our salvation.

**V. 5. ***{God is in the midst of her}* The ground of their confidence. *Yet will they lean upon the Lord, and say, Is not the Lord among us? None evil can come upon us,* Mic. 3.11.

*{and that right early}* Heb. *When the morning appeareth.* Speedily in time of trouble. With allusion perchance to that proverbial speech among the Hebrews; *Weeping may endure for a night, but joy cometh in the morning,* Psal. 30.5. See there.

**V. 6. ***{The heathen raged, the kingdoms were moved}* Psal. 2.1.

*{he uttered his voice}* God miraculously delivers his sometimes, 2 Sam. 22.7.

**V. 7. ***{The Lord of Hosts is with us}* They are assured, that God can and will defend his church from all dangers and enemies.

*{our refuge}* Heb. *an high place for us.*

**V. 8,9. ***{what desolations, &c. He maketh war to cease}* Esay 45.7. *I form the light, and create darkness; I make peace, and create evil: I the Lord do all these things.* See also Amos 3.6. *Shall a trumpet,* &c.

*{desolations}* Or, *wonders.*

**V. 9. ***{he breaketh the bow}* Psal. 76.3.

**V. 10. ***{Be still, and know that I am God}* Most interpreters understand it as spoken by God to the enemies of his church: though some confess, that it may be as well an exhortation to the faithful. And indeed I rather take it so, and it fits better to all that has gone before, by which we are taught to trust to God, though the means, in outward appearance, be never so despicable: and also to that which follows; spoken of, not, to the heathen, among which God says, he will be *exalted;* that is, (as I conceive) by some great and miraculous deliverance that he will vouchsafe unto his people, in their extremity; when all visible means shall fail, that his immediate hand and power may be the better seen and acknowledged. So Esay 33.9,10. *The earth mourneth,* &c. *Now will I rise, saith the Lord: now will I be exalted,* &c. and Joh. 11.40. *Said I not to thee, that if thou wouldst believe, thou shouldst see the glory of God?* that is, a miracle: because at such times, the glory is more immediately given unto God, than when he works by ordinary means. *Be still;* so Esay 30.15. *For thus saith the Lord God, the Holy One of Israel; In returning* (Hebrew בשובה, by some translated, *in quiete;* in rest: as from ישב, not שוב:) *and rest shall ye be saved: in quietness and in confidence shall be your strength: and ye would not.* And in the 7. verse there, *Their strength is to sit still.* And lastly, the word here in the text, הרפו, is the same we have Psal. 37.8. הרף מאף *Cease from anger:* which is spoken to the godly, exhorted there, (as here perchance) not to take too much to heart, nor be moved to impatience, when they see the success and prosperity of the wicked; and contrary events to befall the just and righteous. So also Psal. 76.8. & Psal. 4.4.

# PSALM XLVII

## TITLE

*{for}* Or, *of.*

**Verse 1. ***{O clap your hands}* In token of joy, Psal. 98.8. Esay 55.12.

**V. 2. ***{The Lord most high is terrible}* Here is figured Christ, unto whom all his should give willing obedience, and who will show himself terrible to the wicked.

*{a great King over all the earth}* Mal. 1.14.

**V. 3. ***{He shall subdue the people under us}* Christ shall bring all the enemies of the church into subjection to her, Revel. 3.4.

**V. 4. ***{He shall choose our inheritance for us}* He shall bring us to heaven where Jacob is.

**V. 5. ***{God is gone up with a shout}* That is, the Ark of God: as the Rabbins, and most other interpreters, are of opinion; and that this Psalm was penned upon occasion of some such solemnity, as bringing in and settling it in some certain place. Then was God said to arise, when the Ark was carried; as we learn, Num. 10.35. Psal. 68.1. And then, of shouts and trumpets; we read 2 Sam. 6.15. *So David and all the house of Israel brought up the Ark of the Lord with shouting, and with the sound of the trumpet.* Spiritually and mystically, Christ his glorious ascension into heaven, is hereby intended and foretold: for which we have good authority from the Apostle, who Ephes. 4.8. applies to the same the words of the Psalmist, 68.18. *Thou hast ascended on high,* &c. which literally were intended and spoken concerning the Ark. See more there.

**V. 7. ***{Sing ye praises with understanding}* He requires, that understanding be joined with singing, lest the name of God be profaned with vain crying, 1 Cor. 14.15.

*{with understanding}* Or, *every one that hath understanding.*

**V. 9. ***{The princes of the people are gathered together, even the people of the God of Abraham}* Or, *the voluntary of the people are gathered unto the people of the God of Abraham.*

*{even the people of the God of Abraham}* All the spiritual seed of Abraham shall come to worship God, and seek protection from him.

*{the shields of the earth belong unto God}* God alone is able to protect the world, Psal. 5.12. & 18.35.

# PSALM XLVIII

## TITLE

*{A song, and Psalm}* Where the voice went before the musical instruments; as some are of opinion. See upon the title of Psal. 30.

*{for}* Or, *of.*

**Verse 1. ***{In the city of our God}* Albeit, God show his wonders through all the world, yet he will be chiefly praised in his church.

**V. 2. ***{Beautiful for situation}* יפה נוף, which some render, *pulcher surculo.* And because נוף the verb and *radix,* signifies *sufflavit, suffivit,* &c. or I know not for what other reason, but it has been affirmed by some, that נוף is properly that branch which drops balsam; and hereupon applications have been made by many to the doctrine of the Gospel. But I do not find any good ground for that interpretation. The more received and warrantable, is, *tractus, clima,* as here.

*{The joy of the whole earth is Mount Sion}* *The whole earth,* might be taken here for *the whole land;* as Exod. 10.15. and Mat. 27.45. though some there out of ancient records of astrologers are of opinion, that that darkness extended further than Judea. But however, of Exod. 10.15. there is no question: and so it might be here too. Neither indeed do I find, that either Judea or Jerusalem were in such esteem among the Gentiles. But we may take the words as prophetically spoken, because salvation was to come from thence to the Gentiles.

*{is Mount Sion, on the side of the North, the City of the great King}* These words I find variously printed in the English copies. Some, after *Mount Sion,* have a colon; and some a semicolon: others, a comma only. How it was intended by the translators, I know not. Most interpreters join, *on the side of the North,* to *Mount Sion:* upon a supposition, that mount Sion stood northward of Jerusalem; grounding upon the words in Esay, 14.13. *I will sit also upon the Mount of the Congregation in the sides of the North.* But this is much opposed by some others, who would have these words, *on the side of the North,* to belong to the words following, *the City,* &c. They bring Josephus, and some others to prove, that mount Sion stood in the south, and had Jerusalem northward: and that the chiefest parts of the city, were to the north. Others again, who maintain the same concerning the situation of both, yet take these words, *Mount Sion, on the side of the North,* together; because though Sion itself were southward, as they maintain, yet it had Jerusalem, (the chiefest parts of it) on the north. *On the North* then, not absolutely, in regard of itself; but with reference to Jerusalem. The further discussion of this I leave to geographers; no point of consequence depends upon it: and the difference, as I apprehend it, is of no difficult conciliation; rather verbal, than real.

*{the City of the great King}* *The City of our God,* ver. 1. or, *the City of God,* Psal. 64.4. and so Christ’s words, Matth. 5.35. *the City of the great King,* (which probably had reference to these of the Psalmist) are expounded by very learned men. *The great King,* being referred to men, was the proper title or epithet of the kings of Persia.

**V. 3. ***{for a refuge}* Except God were the defense thereof, neither situation nor munition could prevail.

**V. 4. ***{the kings were assembled}* Psal. 46.6. They conspired and went against God’s people, and were so terrified by God, that they hastily returned, ver. 5,6,7.

*{they passed by together}* Or, *and passed away presently.*

**V. 5. ***{They saw it, and so they marvelled}* Or, *They saw the foundation, they marvelled.*

*{so they marvelled}* At the beauty of it, ver. 2. or saw it too strong for them to overcome, ver. 12,13.

*{they were troubled}* Terrified by God, ver. 6,7. suddenly, and inevitably, as a woman’s travail, Esay 13.8.

**V. 7. ***{Thou breakest the ships}* You have violently destroyed, or driven away our foes, as ships at sea, with a tempest.

*{of Tarshish}* That is, of Cilicia, or of the sea, called Mediterranean, or Midland sea.

**V. 8. ***{As we have heard, so have we seen}* To wit, of our fathers, Psal. 44.1. so have we seen in this deliverance, that God will defend this city: or, as you have promised, so you have done.

**V. 9. ***{We have thought of thy lovingkindness}* To give you praise for this great deliverance.

**V. 10. ***{According to thy name}* In all places where your name shall be heard of, men shall praise you, when they hear of your marvelous works.

**V. 11. ***{Let Mount Zion rejoice}* Let Jerusalem and the cities of Judah rejoice, for the just judgment upon the enemies, ver. 5,6.

**V. 12. ***{Walk about Zion}* View, if any one tower, or bulwark be hurt by the enemy, and praise God that none is damnified.

**V. 13. ***{Mark ye well her bulwarks}* Heb. *set your heart to her bulwarks.*

*{consider}* Or, *raise up.*

*{that ye may tell it to the generation following}* How God has kept them all untouched of the enemy.

**V. 14. ***{For this God is our God for ever and ever}* Who has kept us and our towers, and driven away our enemies.

# PSALM XLIX

## TITLE

*{for}* Or, *of.*

**Verse 2. ***{Low and high, rich and poor together}* Psal. 62.9.

**V. 4. ***{I will incline mine mine ear to a parable}* I will deliver that which with much study and diligent attention I have got, or attained unto. No trivial ordinary matter, which every man of himself is capable of; but such as I have received from the learned professors of piety, or the Author of Wisdom himself. A parable in the Scripture, and חידה too, (here translated *a dark saying*) is taken sometimes for a sententious speech, or excellent doctrine; though otherwise neither very obscure, nor parabolical: as Psal. 78.2. *I will open my mouth in a parable,* &c. the subject of that Psalm being nothing but an historical (but poetically set out) commemoration of God’s mercies to his people, in several kinds; and their frequent unthankfulness. And such is the doctrine of this Psalm, which very well deserves the solemnity of this extraordinary prefacing: a doctrine of as much comfort and consequence to men, as any can be: delivered here more plainly, than in any other part of Scripture, till Christ’s time, Daniel excepted. The sum is this; That we should not be troubled at the prosperity of the wicked and ungodly, since that this world, with all its delights, are things so transitory: God having provided better things for them that live in his fear, in another world. That this doctrine was not so common, in those days; and why, has been showed upon the 37. Psalm: so that in that respect, though otherwise plainly enough delivered here, it might be styled *a parable,* or *enigma.* See more there.

**V. 5. ***{when the iniquity of my heels shall compass me about}* There be no less than 17. or 18. several (as is pretended) interpretations of these words; so many collected by Lorinus: besides what more may be gathered by others: so many, but most of them so forced and impertinent, or coincident with others, as even to recite them, I conceive would be but loss of time and labor. The chiefest, and almost only considerable, are but two. These will be well worth our consideration, because it is a point of high consequence. First then there be, (as most ancients) who by *heel,* understand the last of a man’s life, or the day of death, to this purpose; Why should I commit anything, that I should need to fear, when that dreadful day shall come, when sentence, in private first, and in public afterwards, must be passed upon me. The principal cause of fearing, is, if it should so fall out, that at the end and close of my life I should be compassed about with sins; such especially, through which, to get wealth to myself, I made no conscience to oppress others. But since it is in my power, through God’s grace, to prevent it; or in case I have sinned, through his mercy, to repent in time, and obtain pardon; why should I fear so much? Death indeed I cannot avoid; all men are born to it: but that which only should make dreadful unto men, I may avoid. So, the words would be translated; *Why should I fear in the evil day, that the iniquity of my heels will compass me about?* For the translation, there is no question of it to be made, but that it may be right enough, as to the Hebrew words: but to make this sense of it, much must be supplied, and yet it will hardly do. Much less do I conceive what convenient sense and coherence can be made, if following the translation, by, *the iniquity of my heels,* the sins of my life, or the punishment due unto my sins, as some others would have, be understood. The second interpretation therefore, which I reckon considerable, is, to take עון *iniquity,* for wicked men; the abstract for the concrete: which is usual enough; to this effect; Why should I fear, &c. when wicked unjust men (encouraged by their success and worldly prosperity;) pursue me close, on every side? Either I fear, and be dismayed; or any other godly man, that puts his trust in God? Why not rather consider, that thus and thus is the case of the wicked; and his worldly prosperity accordingly to be valued: Whereas *God will redeem my soul,* &c. ver. 15. See there. They are said in some languages, *to pursue to the heels,* that pursue very closely, and with great animosity. And so Psal. 56.7. המה עקבי ישמרו: *they observe my heels:* it is the same word there, as is here: and is translated, *calcanea mea observant,* by some of best account.

**V. 8. ***{the redemption of their soul is precious}* That is, of their life, Exod. 21.30.

*{it ceaseth for ever}* It is so great a price, that it can never be paid by any, Job 36.13,19. Deut. 23.22. Zech. 11.11.

**V. 9. ***{not see corruption}* Psal. 89.48.

**V. 10. ***{wise men die}* Eccles. 2.16. Death makes no difference between persons, it spares none.

*{leave their wealth to others}* That is, not to their children, but to strangers, ver. 11. yet the wicked profit not by these examples.

**V. 11. ***{Their}* Or, *Yet their.*

*{to all generations}* Heb. *to generation and generation.*

**V. 12. ***{man being in honour, abideth not}* That is, mankind, or men in general; who being endowed with the gift of reason, above other creatures, and honored by God, to have dominion over them, are nevertheless subject unto death, as well as they.

*{abideth not}* בל ילין: properly, *non pernoctabit,* or, *pernoctat.* Which some take to be spoken of Adam particularly, *Adam being in honour,* &c. and hence infer, that he fell the first day, he was created: the last hour of the day. But this is too high, to be spoken with any confidence, upon no other ground. The common and most warrantable interpretation is, of man in general, who *abides not:* that is, has no permanent estate in this world. His time is short; and of that short time no part is stable, but subject to all manner of chances and mutabilities: in which respect, this whole world is said, even while it lasts, *to pass away,* 1 Joh. 2.17. in opposition to that reality of being, which properly belongs unto God only; and after him in an inferior degree, to immortal essences. See before upon Psal. 37.36. *Yet he passed away, and lo he was not.*

**V. 13. ***{yet their posterity approve their sayings}* They commend their fathers’ foolish courses, and continue in them, Rom. 1.31. Psal. 10.3.

*{approve their sayings}* Heb. *delight in their mouth.*

**V. 14. ***{Like sheep they are laid in the grave}* As sheep are gathered into the fold, so shall men be brought to the grave.

*{death shall feed on them}* That is, consume their bodies, as we do meat.

*{the upright shall have dominion over them in the morning}* That is, The righteous will soon have the better of them, whilst these remain in the power of death and the grave; and the righteous are exalted, being delivered from both, and received into heaven; as is more clearly expressed in the next verse. *In the morning,* as before, Psal. 46.5. *God shall help her, and that right early.* See there. Some think the resurrection is intended by this word, which in divers respects may be likened unto the morning. *Wherefore he saith, Awake thou that sleepest, and arise from the dead, and Christ shall give thee light,* Eph. 5.14.

*{in the grave from their dwelling}* That is, their form, or beauty, shall consume in the grave, being thither transferred from their dwellings: or, being forced from their stately houses and palaces, to betake themselves to the obscurity of a grave. The Hebrew word מזבל here translated, (the first letter of it being taken for a preposition) *from their dwelling,* may also be taken for *habitaculum,* alone; which has produced that other translation in the margin, *the grave being an habitation unto them,* or, *to every one of them.* Either of these may fit well enough.

**V. 15. ***{But God will redeem my soul, &c.}* If *Selah* be a note (as many think; of which, see upon Psa. 3.) of exaltation, or elevation of mind, I know not any place in the whole book, that better deserves it, or more requires it. Here is a clear testimony for the immortality of the soul, and for a better life after this: without which assurance, we might say with the Apostle, *Let us eat and drink, for to morrow we must die,* 1 Cor. 15.32. Why this doctrine was not so fully revealed in those days, has been said upon Psal. 37. to which we must often refer, that we be not forced to repeat the same thing often. However, divers passages there be, even in the Psalms, out of which the same may be proved: but not any so clear, but that they may be, and are by the generality of interpreters, that profess to be most literal, and *eo nomine* are of best account, if not eluded, (for it is not their purpose, I suppose) yet I know not how turned another way, so that little can be made of them, to convince a gainsayer. Even that Psal. 17.15. *As for me, I will behold thy face,* &c. though the words seem to me as direct, as any need to be, by reason of the antithesis there, as well as here; (of which see more there) yet there be, that have not that apprehension of them that we have. But, God be thanked that we find not any such opposition here: all men seem to be very well satisfied, that these words must be understood of eternal life. Indeed the antithesis here is so plain between those whom death has power over, (power over so far, as to end all their happiness) and those whom it has not, who therefore comfort themselves in that hope, and oppose it to all that wicked and ungodly men can boast of in this world: so plain, I say, that either we must say there be no words, by which this hope of eternal life can be expressed, or grant that it is here. Yet will I not press them so far, as to bind any man to believe, that the resurrection of the body was hereby intended. It is the opinion of many; and it is not improbable: but I will content myself with that which cannot be denied, That eternal life and happiness is here opposed to the worldly prosperity of faithless wicked men.

*{from the power of the grave}* Heb. *from the hand of the grave.*

*{grave}* Or, *Hell.*

*{for he shall receive me}* So it is in the Hebrew, by the future, *he shall,* &c. but because it is ordinary with them by a future to understand a present, some translate the words, *quia suscepit me;* that is, say they, because he has favored me; or, taken a liking to me: and this to be the ground of his alleged hope, and confidence, God’s voluntary grace and favor. The doctrine is good: but it may do as well, to keep to the future, *for he shall receive me;* as a further explication or prosecution of this excellent doctrine; to this effect, That whensoever his fatal hour shall come, that body and soul must part, God will receive his Spirit, Act. 7.15. *Lord Jesus receive my spirit.* See also Psal. 73.24. *and afterwards receive me to glory.*

**V. 17. ***{For when he dieth, he shall carry nothing away}* Job 27.19.

**V. 18. ***{whiles he lived}* Heb. *in his life.*

*{and men will praise thee}* Flatterers praise them that live in delights and pleasures.

**V. 19. ***{He shall go}* Heb. *The soul shall go.*

*{He shall go to the generation of his fathers}* And not pass the term appointed for life, Job 14.5.
