*{they shall never see light}* Both they and their fathers shall go out of this world, and return no more unto it, Psal. 56.13. Job 33.28,30.

**V. 20. ***{and understandeth not}* This verse agrees with ver. 12. but that there it is, בל ילין with a *Lamed:* *non pernoctabit: abideth not;* and here, ולא יבין with a *Beth:* which difference is so small, that some have suspected, that it was at first alike in both places, and that the alteration has been since. There might be more ground for this suspicion, were all the difference in those two letters only, which indeed alone make all the difference of sense; but there is a further and greater difference in the writing; בל in the 12. verse; ולא in this: by which it appears, that that other difference did not happen by any chance. It is likely therefore, as is well observed by some, that the Psalmist in the 12. verse having abased the condition of man in general very low, not excluding any; that he does of purpose now mitigate that harsher expression, exempting those, who make use of that reason and understanding, wherewith God has endowed them, to improve themselves in their better part, above the condition of brutes.

*{is like the beasts that perish}* Before, in the twelfth verse, *to be like the beasts that perish,* was, to be subject to mortality, as well as they: which is the condition of all men, both good and bad. Now that he has distinguished, and speaks of the wicked only, *to be like the beasts that perish,* must be, to die without hope of any further good or happiness; which is the case of all brutes. Or, like brutes in their life, in that they take care only for the present, to fare well, and to pass the time merrily, not considering what will become of their souls; as creatures, that had no souls, but what perish with the body. But in very deed, the Psalmist in this Psalm, may seem to set out the case of the wicked in point of death, as no ways different from that of brutes: *Like sheep they are laid in the grave,* &c. ver. 14. & 19. *He shall go to the generation of his fathers, he shall never see light:* Not that he believed so himself; but because it was the common opinion then, there being no impiety in it, until the contrary was certainly and publicly revealed. See more upon Psa. 37.1,2. *Fret not thyself,* &c. and Psal. 73.32. *I was as a beast before thee.*

# PSALM L

## TITLE

*{Of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph.* Who was either the author, as being a prophet, and the author of some Psalms, or spiritual songs, as we learn, 2 Chron. 29.30. or a chief singer, to whom it was committed, 1 Chron. 16.5,7.

**Verse 1. ***{The mighty God}* Heb. *God of gods.* He that rules over angels and kings, and all that is called God.

*{called the earth from the rising of the sun}* To plead against his dissembling people, before heaven and earth.

**V. 2. ***{the perfection of beauty}* Because God has chosen it to have his Name there called upon, and also his image shined there in the doctrine of the law, Psal. 29.2. & 110.3,4.

*{God hath shined}* Showed himself glorious, like the shining sun.

*{Out of Sion, the perfection of beauty}* Since it has been thought fit, that the Turkish (so called, though written in Arabic) Alcoran, a book as full of blasphemies, in matter of religion, as it is of senseless absurdities, in point of human reason, should be communicated unto the people, in the vulgar English tongue; it will not be thought amiss to take notice that of all the Scriptures either of the Old, or New Testament, there being two places especially that Mahometans take hold of, and are wont to object unto Christians; this is one of them. I have lately seen a letter from one of that sect, to a Christian in Rome, as I remember, (the letter is printed) where those two places are much pressed and insisted upon. They quote the words of this verse thus: *God hath shewed out of Sion* ⟦*Gelilan* *Mahmudan*; the first word and the damaged line-end are uncertain⟧U2; that is, *a glorious,* or *renowned Crown.* By which crown, they would have a kingdom; and by *renowned,* (*Mahmudan,* in Arabic) Mahomet, (more properly written, *Muhammed*) because of the affinity, or rather identity of letters, to be meant. But granting that in some Arabic translation of the Psalms (which yet some very learned and well-versed in these languages and books, having seen many besides them that are printed, profess they have not yet met with) it were so found; yet what is that to the purpose, except in the original Hebrew it were so too? where we find neither letters, nor sense to that purpose. The words, which they press in the Hebrew are, מכלל יפי. In the first indeed, *Micklal,* for the letters, (but that is not it will serve their turn) there is affinity enough: but in the second, (*jophi*) none at all. And yet were it so in very deed, even in the original text; what were this, to justify all their impostures and forgeries; not to be refuted, nor maintained by casual allusions and affinities of bare words and letters, but by evidences of truth, reason, and history?

**V. 3. ***{a fire shall devour before him}* As when God gave his law in mount Sinai, he appeared terrible with thunder, and tempest: so will he appear terrible to take account for the violating thereof.

**V. 4. ***{He shall call to the heavens from above}* As witnesses against the wicked of his people, Deut. 31.28. & 32.1. Isa. 1.2.

**V. 5. ***{Gather my saints together unto me}* God in respect of his elect calls the whole body, holy, saints, and his people.

*{that have made a covenant with me by sacrifice}* Sacrifices were seals of the covenant, between God and his people.

**V. 6. ***{God is judge himself}* Psal. 75.7.

**V. 8. ***{I will not reprove thee for thy sacrifices}* Ye have wearied me with your sacrifices, Isa. 1.11,14. Mic. 6.6,7. Act. 4.4,5. so that I cannot blame you for want of outward ceremonies, but of moral service.

*{or thy burnt offerings to have been continually before me}* It should have been, either *not to have been continually before me;* that, *not,* being to be supplied here out of the first, *I will not reprove thee,* &c. according to the idiom of the Hebrew, acknowledged and observed here by some of the most skillful in that language: or without any supplement at all, but to the same purpose, placing a colon after sacrifices: *thy burnt-offerings have been continually before me.* Either of these, makes the sense clear, and keeps to the propriety of the words. As for the matter itself, the reproving or slighting of sacrifices, and opposing to them, or substituting instead of them, spiritual service (the chief subject and aim of the whole Psalm) See before upon Psal. 40.6. *Mine ears hast thou opened.*

**V. 10. ***{For every beast of the forest is mine}* If he did delight in sacrifices, yet had he no need of man’s help thereunto.

*{upon a thousand hills}* Heb. *upon the hills of a thousand owners.*

**V. 11. ***{mine}* Heb. *with me.*

**V. 12. ***{for the world is mine, and the fulness thereof}* Exod. 19.5. Deut. 10.14. Job 41.11. Psal. 24.1. 1 Cor. 10.26,28. See before upon Psal. 24.1. *and the fulness thereof:* in the end there.

**V. 13. ***{Will I eat the flesh of bulls}* Though man’s life for the infirmity thereof has need of food, yet God, whose life quickens all the world, has no need of such means.

**V. 14. ***{pay thy vows unto the most High}* Show yourself mindful of God’s benefits, by thanksgiving, and obedience promised in Sinai, Psal. 116.13,14. See more upon Psal. 5.5. *For in death,* &c.

**V. 15. ***{call upon me in the day of trouble}* Job 22.27. See upon Psal. 38.18. *For I will declare mine iniquity.*

**V. 16. ***{What hast thou to do to declare my statutes}* Why do you feign to be of my people, and talk of my covenant, seeing you are but a hypocrite?

**V. 17. ***{Seeing thou hatest instruction}* Rom. 2.21,22. How to live according to my word.

*{and castest my words behind thee}* Yet he was said before, to *declare his statutes,* &c. They are therefore said *to cast the words,* or, the word of God, *behind them:* who though they show much affection to it, and love to talk of it, and will go many miles, perchance, to hear one sermon; yet apply it not to their hearts, (which is the true use of it) to amend their lives by it, but please themselves in their own conceits, as though they were the only elect people of God; and upon that confidence freely censure and condemn others, whether good or bad, (not sparing them that are of nearest relation, brothers, &c. verse 20. *Thou sittest,* &c.) because they make not so much outward show, or refuse to join with them in their affected ways; though they show the power of godliness in their lives and conversations, much more than themselves. So Ezek. 33.30,31, &c. *Also thou Son of man,* &c. *And lo, thou art unto them as a very lovely song, of one that hath a pleasant voice, and can play well on an instrument: for they hear thy words, but they do them not.* If therefore we admit not the word of God into our hearts, (which will appear by our lives) though we go never so far to please our ears with it, we may truly be said to hate and reject it, because we make not that use of it, for which it is intended.

**V. 18. ***{When}* Or, *Seeing when.*

*{when thou sawest a thief, &c.}* Though the Psalmist insist upon certain crimes, as theft, and adultery, in this; perfidiousness and fraudulency, in the next; and lastly, uncharitableness in judgments, and malicious detraction, in the 20. verse; as signs and evidences of hypocritical hearing, or preaching: Yet we must not so understand him, as though those particulars did all belong unto all that are such. He insists in these, *exempli causa;* as we say: in some for all, or some others, (whether more, or fewer to be found in one man) that are of the same nature. That therefore we must gather from his words, is; that we may know such by their deeds and actions, whether theft, adultery, murder, false dealing, cheating, drunkenness, lying, swearing, and the like; so contrary to God, and all godliness, declared unto us by the word of God, that they that pretend to godliness and the word of God, and yet do such things, must needs be thought, either wilfully, to make a mock of that which they pretend to love and honor; or to be extremely blinded and deluded by the Devil, that they should not see, what of itself is so plain to be seen and understood: but this especially, of open theft and adultery, mentioned in this verse. For as for dishonesty, and false dealing in a cunning way, under pretences of love, and the like; and uncharitableness in censures and judgments; and malicious detraction: in the following verses; these things are very ordinarily to be found in all, or most of them, that are great pretenders, (but hypocritically) to the word of God. Hitherto in the explication of these verses, from ver. 16. *But unto the wicked,* &c. I have followed expositors of best account; (as Calvin for one) who make hypocrites, such as make great show of piety by external performances, (as all kinds of ceremonies in general;) but especially by their zeal to *Praedicationem Verbi,* (that is, sermons,) to be the men aimed at. Indeed if hypocrites in general, the words will infer, that such especially are intended, that make a great brag of God’s word, and show much zeal that way. But when I well consider the original words, I cannot absolutely conclude, whether hypocrites, that make a show of godliness, or openly profane men, are intended. Such I mean, who though they knew themselves and their lives to be bad, neither did much hide it, or were much ashamed of it; yet hoped well for all that, as Jews born, Abraham’s children, and upon that account, having right to the covenant of grace; *heirs of the promises,* visible members of the church, and the like. See Jer. chap. 7.1,2,3. to ver. 17. It is likely there be among Christians too, too many, who though they indulge themselves all manner of liberty, and make little or no conscience of their ways; yet flatter themselves that they are Christians, and love the church, and hope to find mercy: which if they do, (without repentance and amendment) the word of God must be false, Mat. 7.22,23. & 1 Cor. 6.9,10. *Be not deceived,* &c. often repeated (in the same, or the like words) in other places. If we follow this sense, *to declare my statutes,* will be, to boast of my law, (by a special favor delivered unto the Jews only, the seed of Abraham, not made common to all nations) and gladly to talk of any covenant, as the circumcision, &c. as though that could profit them anything, as long as, &c. This in the Apostle’s expression, (which is very elegant and proper,) is ἐπαναπαύεσθαι τῷ νόμῳ, *to rest in the law,* Rom. 2.17. that whole passage of the Apostle, from v. 17. to 23. containing a full explication of the words of the Psalmist, taken in this sense.

*{hast been partaker with adulterers}* Heb. *thy portion was with adulterers.*

**V. 19. ***{Thou givest}* Heb. *Thou sendest;* or, *And givest.*

*{thy tongue frameth deceit}* Psal. 10.7. Job 27.4.

**V. 20. ***{Thou}* Or, *And.*

*{Thou sittest and speakest against thy brother}* See upon ver. 17. *and castest my words;* ver. 18. *When thou sawest.*

*{thou}* Or, *and.*

**V. 21. ***{and set them in order before thine eyes}* I will write all your wicked deeds in a roll, and make you to read, and acknowledge them, whether you will or no.

**V. 23. ***{that ordereth his conversation aright}* Heb. *that disposeth his way:* As God has appointed. So Psal. 66.18. *If I regard iniquity in my heart, the Lord will not hear me.* Joh. 9.31. *Now we know that God heareth not sinners,* &c.

*{will I shew the salvation of God}* Psal. 91.16. That is, declare myself to be his Savior.

# PSALM LI

## TITLE

*{when Nathan the Prophet came unto him}* 2 Sam. 12.1. and 11.2. To reprove him, because he had committed so horrible sins, and lain in the same so long without repentance.

**Verse 1. ***{According to the multitude}* He is very earnest, as knowing, that the act of forgiving of sins, is an act of greatest mercy, yea and power. It was more to say, (to say it, authoritative; that is, by warrant of one’s proper power and authority, and not by way of delegation, and declaration only, as God’s ministers; though that too be a great power:) *Son, thy sins be forgiven thee;* than to say, *Arise, take up thy bed, and walk,* Matth. 9.5,6. With that zeal therefore that we would pray (and greater, if it be possible) for deliverance from temporal afflictions and extremities of highest nature; we should pray for forgiveness of sins, of great sins especially; as a thing both of greater consequence, to us; and of greater difficulty, in itself, but that, to God all things are equally possible. But few are so sensible of their sins, as they are of any worldly cross; and accordingly they carry themselves in their prayers and devotions. They have good ground to hope well of themselves, that find it otherwise in themselves.

**V. 2. ***{Wash me thoroughly from mine iniquity}* My sins stick so fast to me, that I have need of some singular kind of washing. He alludes to the legal washings. He would not only be forgiven for what is past, but so thoroughly cleansed, and so purified in his soul, that he might not relapse either unto the same, or any other sins, of what kind soever: to wound his conscience before God, and to incur his just wrath and judgments again. He alludes to the legal washings. Then may be an intimation also of the greatness of his sin in the word *thoroughly;* as needing more than ordinary washing.

**V. 3. ***{For I acknowledge my transgressions}* Upon this acknowledgment, which includes a promise and serious purpose of amendment, he grounds his plea for mercy: because *He that covereth his sins, shall not prosper; but whoso confesseth and forsaketh them, shall have mercy,* Prov. 28.13. And so 1 Joh. 1.9. *If we confess our sins, he is faithful and just to forgive us our sins, and to cleanse us from all unrighteousness.*

**V. 4. ***{against thee, thee only have I sinned}* Some interpret these words, as spoken by David by way of aggravation, or exaggeration of his faults; as if he intended to say, That it was not the judgments of the world that he stood upon, whether accusing, or excusing; but that which troubled him, was, that he had so grievously offended God; so good a God, whose just indignation he had incurred; and so just a God, whose righteous judgment, though all men should acquit him, he knew he could not avoid. Others more simply, that he speaks as a King, who though he were not, as a King, liable to the punishment of the Law, yet he was accountable to God: so most ancients, and some modern. Others again there be, who also follow the simple meaning, but to another sense; *Against thee only have I sinned:* because I have used great secrecy in the fault, that it might not be known unto men; both in the matter of adultery, and in that of the murder; as appears by the history. Whence it is that Nathan tells him from God, *For thou didst it secretly; but I will do this before all Israel, and before the Sun,* 2 Sam. 12.12. Nevertheless, it appears even by that which follows there, ver. 14. *Howbeit, because by this deed, thou hast given great occasion to the enemies of God to blaspheme,* &c. that the matter was come abroad: and withal, (which I would have well observed, because it may lead us perchance to the right meaning, as much as anything that has been said) how much God (if we may so speak) did suffer in these foul acts of his servant David. Why might not then David, out of a zeal to God and his glory merely, without any regard to himself, say, That nothing grieved him so much, neither matter of shame and reproach from men; nor matter of punishment upon himself from God; as that his sin did so much redound upon God; or in Nathan’s (or God’s) own words, that he had *given great occasion to the enemies of God to blaspheme.* This would do very well, I think, if it will as well agree with that which follows, whereof we shall consider when we come to it.

But in the meantime, taking it for granted, that David’s purpose was (which this very repetition of words, *Against thee, thee only,* &c. does argue) to aggravate his sin: by which I do not understand hyperbolically to amplify; but truly and feelingly to set out the heinousness of it: this therefore grounding upon as granted, in further pursuance of the sense, I would have the words of old Eli well considered of, 1 Sam. 2.24,25. *Nay my sons,* said he, *for it is no good report that I hear: ye make the Lord’s people to transgress. If one man sin against another, the Judge shall judge him: but if a man sin against the Lord, who shall intreat for him?* His meaning was not, that their sin, absolutely and precisely, was to God only: for it was public, and could not but offend many in divers respects; but to God, or against God, more immediately. Now David’s sin was against God more immediately; first, as he was a King: I will not dispute about matter of right, how far above, or under the Law, as kings, &c. which I know would be subject to much opposition: but *de facto,* as we say; that is, a King may do much, which others cannot, or dare not; I think will easily be granted. Proud Jezebel knew what she said, when she upbraided her husband for his tenderness about Naboth’s vineyard, *Dost thou now govern the kingdom of Israel?* 1 King. 21.7. Again, David hoped to carry the matter so closely, that it should not have been known. It appears that he did endeavor, what he could, to conceal it. Now in either of these two cases, when the fear, or knowledge of man is removed, and that a man thinks himself secure, as to the world; for a man then freely to indulge himself a liberty of sinning, is a high provocation, and to make a practice of it, would argue a desperate case. Nothing can be more contrary to either true conscience, or true generosity. Lastly, as it was a sin against God more properly, whilst it was concealed; so when it came to be public, it proved a sin against God too, as properly, though in another respect. For David, being such a one as he was, so approved by God, and of such exemplary piety abroad; piety and religion must needs suffer very much in this his so foul and so notorious a fact. *Ye make the Lord’s people to transgress,* was that Eli chiefly objected to his sons: and that *occasion was given to the enemies of God to blaspheme,* was part of Nathan’s charge (as we saw before) unto David. In all these respects then, David might well say, *Against thee, thee only,* &c.

*{that thou mightest be justified, &c. and be clear when thou judgest}* There be, who making a parenthesis of the former verse, (*For I acknowledge,* &c.) and the former part of this, (*Against thee, thee only—in thy sight:*) join these words, *that thou mightest,* &c. with the first and second verses; as though he intended to put God in mind of his goodness, and of his gracious promises made to himself; that God would be as good as his word, &c. But besides what may be objected from the words themselves, the last of this verse, (*and be clear when thou judgest:*) it is apparent by S. Paul’s inference upon them, or citation of them, Rom. 3.4. that that cannot be the meaning. Though it might be said, that S. Paul cited them by way of allusion only, in which case he might take the more liberty; which is not without example, perchance. But in very deed, David’s meaning, was S. Paul’s meaning; and it will fit the context here, as well as there. That which both intended, is, to give all the glory unto God; which is best done, by man, when man freely acknowledges his own wretchedness and sinfulness; and pronounces at the same time, perfect truth and goodness to belong unto God only. Hence it is, that sinners are properly said *to glorify God,* when they freely and fully confess their sins; as is showed before in the beginning of these notes, upon that part of the title; *Of Psalms.* The very heathens would say upon this occasion of man’s infirmity, that it was the privilege or prerogative (Θεὸς ἂν μόνος τοῦτο ἔχοι τὸ γέρας, in Plato; *Let God alone have that honour;*) of God, to be without faults. This I conceive more probable and proper to the place, than that David (as some would have it) by those words should intend to say, that what punishment soever God shall inflict upon him, or howsoever he shall deal with him (whether he sink or swim) yet he would have God acquitted; so that neither his truth (in matter of promises) nor his justice (in his retributions) should upon this occasion be, by any to be questioned. I think this is more about. But if any like it better, I am not against it.

There is a difference in the words too, that may make S. Paul’s quotation to differ from the Psalmist, observed by most upon this Psalm. *and be clear when thou judgest;* says the Psalmist, *and be clear,* (or *mightest overcome:* that makes no great difference,) *when thou art judged;* S. Paul. David, actively: Paul, passively. The matter may easily be reconciled. For at the same time that God does *judge,* (or execute judgment upon any) he may also be said *to be judged;* whilst men pass their censures either as applauding, or disliking his judgments. And then may he be said *to overcome, when judged;* when men acknowledge the justice of his judgments. Besides, it is well observed by some here, and proved by divers examples, that S. Paul by a special idiom, (though in this particular passage he follow the Septuagint,) uses in his citations to turn many active verbs, into passives. But after all this, it may be said, that this difference of the verbs, is rather a misapprehension of interpreters, than any real difference. For the word S. Paul uses is κρίνεσθαι; (καὶ νικήσῃς ἐν τῷ κρίνεσθαί σε:) which word may be taken actively, as well as passively: and so indeed rendered here by Beza; *& vincas, quando tu judicas.*

**V. 5. ***{I was shapen in iniquity}* He here brings all his actual transgressions to the fountain of them, original corruption, Rom. 7.8, &c. By this phrase, *in sin,* expressing that sins possessing the whole man, Joh. 9.34. Act. 8.23. Eph. 2.1. are as waters wherein a man is overwhelmed.

*{conceive me}* Heb. *warm me.*

**V. 6. ***{Thou shalt make me to know wisdom}* Most interpreters, with the vulgar Latin, express it by the preterit; *thou hast manifested,* or *made known unto me,* as if this too, were spoken by way of aggravation against himself, who after such measure of knowledge and wisdom, vouchsafed unto him by God, should nevertheless pollute himself with such gross sins. A great and just aggravation indeed: according to that of our Savior’s, Joh. 9.41. *If ye were blind, ye should have no sin,* &c. Nevertheless, the future, (as it is in the Hebrew,) may do as well: and does agree with the former part of the verse, rather better. Then is there *truth in the inward parts,* when the holiness of our lives, does answer to the light of our understandings: neither can that properly be called true knowledge, or sound wisdom, which has not that operation. So 1 Joh. 2.4. *He that saith I know him, and keepeth not his commandments, is a lyar, and the truth is not in him.* David’s words therefore include an acknowledgment of his sin, by which the light of his understanding was darkened; and may be resolved (as they are by some) into a prayer, that God would restore unto him that wisdom, which by the prevalency of vice he had in a great measure forfeited; or at least, eclipsed.

**V. 7. ***{Purge me with hyssop, and I shall be clean}* Not that David had any opinion of any external thing, as much available to the purging of his soul: he knew well enough, that neither the power of hyssop, nor any water, could reach to those *inward hidden parts,* (the seat of all true wisdom and piety,) by him mentioned in the verse before: but because hyssop and water were appointed by the Law of God in all, or most solemn purifications and expiations, as a sign and seal of inward cleansing, (of which see Exod. 12. Lev. 14. Num. 19.) by the power and mercy of God: the Psalmist by these phrases expresses his faith in God, that though he be through sin become never so foul and filthy; yet God is able speedily (if he please) to restore him to his former purity and innocency. See also upon Psal. 16.17. *My reins also.*

*{and I shall be clean}* Or, *that I may be clean.*

*{and I shall be whiter}* Or, *that I may be whiter.* As Mal. 1.9. *that he will be gracious unto him.* Heb. *and he will be gracious.*

**V. 8. ***{Make me to hear joy and gladness}* He means God’s comfortable mercies towards repentant sinners.

*{the bones which thou hast broken}* By the bones he understands all strength of soul and body, which by care and mourning is consumed.

**V. 10. ***{Create in me a clean heart}* He confesses that when God’s spirit is cold in us, to have it again revived, is as a new creation.

*{a right spirit}* Or, *a constant spirit.*

**V. 12. ***{Cast me not away from thy presence}* This was an effect of God’s utmost anger, Gen. 4.16. 2 Kings 24.20. Jer. 7.15. and 52.3.

**V. 12. ***{Restore unto me the joy of thy salvation}* It is a good sign, if we fall into any great sin, that we are sensible of our condition. There is good hope of them that are, though their sin be great. As their case on the other side, is desperate, who dare sin with security, and think it a testimony of their faith, that they mistrust not themselves for it. But here is a question raised; handled at large by some upon this Psalm: Nathan the prophet, had been with David, before this, and declared unto him from God, that his sin was pardoned: or, *that God had put away his sin,* 2 Sam. 12.13. Why then did he make any further question? This, as is conceived, might argue some kind of infidelity in him. It is answered, that it is possible Nathan came to him about it, more than once; and did not so fully absolve him at his first, though the Scripture make mention but of one coming, and for brevity, comprehend and contract all in one. And besides, that it is not against the nature of faith, to be solicitous, even after absolution: and that such expressions of a deep sense and remorse, do well become a sinner after reconciliation, as well as before; it being the property of an ingenuous heart in this case, the more he finds and feels God gracious and merciful unto him; the more to be affected with the remembrance of his sins, and past provocations. But besides all this, by Nathan’s words that are recorded, it does not appear that God’s pardon, delivered at that time, did extend to another world. *The Lord hath put away thy sin, thou shalt not die;* said Nathan. That the pardon was a general pardon, extending to the soul, as well as to the body, I make no question; yet if the declaration of it were not more express and particular, David might well pray for further assurance, before he could think himself secure.

*{uphold me with thy free spirit}* The Hebrew may be translated, either *spiritu spontaneo;* with a free spirit: meaning, that God would give him grace freely and willingly in all things to submit unto his will: as the word נדיבה is used, Exod. 35.21, &c. Or as here, *thy free spirit;* that is, the Spirit of adoption, Rom. 8.15,16. and *where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty,* 2 Cor. 3.17. Some interpret it, *spiritu liberali; id est, liberaliter in me effuso,* with a free spirit, that is, say they, a Spirit (or gifts of the Spirit) freely poured upon me. This also, the words will bear: but I like either of the former better.

**V. 13. ***{thy ways}* He promises to endeavor, that others by his example may turn to God.

**V. 14. ***{Deliver me from bloodguiltiness}* From the murder of Uriah, and others, that were slain with him, 1 Sam. 11.17.

*{blood}* Heb. *bloods.*

**V. 15. ***{open thou thy lips}* By giving me occasion to praise you, when you shall forgive my sins.

**V. 16. ***{For thou desirest not sacrifice}* See before upon Psal. 40.6. *mine ears hast thou opened,* &c. But some learned men think that David has another meaning in these words; to wit, That if God had appointed any sacrifice in his Law, as he had done for many other things, so for expiation of either murder, or adultery, he would have given it him; but since he had ordained none, save only a broken spirit and a contrite heart, he hoped God would not despise it, but accept that alone for a sacrifice in this case, without which, sacrifice, in no case, is accepted. So a learned author of our own, in a late treatise inscribed, *The reverence of God’s House:* which whether he had out of Grotius, I know not: but I find that it is Grotius his opinion upon this place; by whom also we are referred for further proofs, to his book, *De satisfactione, capite de sacrificiis expiatoriis.*

*{else would I give it}* Or, *that I should give it.*

**V. 17. ***{The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit}* Esay 57.15. and 66.2. Psal. 34.18. A heart wounded for sin, and seeking to God for mercy in faith.

**V. 18. ***{Do good in thy good pleasure unto Zion}* He prays for the whole church, because through his sin it was in danger of God’s judgment.

*{in}* Or, *for.*

*{build thou the walls of Jerusalem}* That is, with best expositors, taking the words proverbially, or metaphorically; protect, defend, and maintain. So the Hebrews use the word *building.* Whether therefore the walls of Jerusalem were up, or down, perfect, or imperfect, (which some would infer upon these words,) he might so speak.

**V. 19. ***{with the sacrifices of righteousness}* By *sacrifices of righteousness,* I understand here, with best interpreters, legal sacrifices, he had before declared what sacrifices they were that God chiefly required and delighted in v. 15. *Lord open thou,* &c. He had spoken very peremptorily, *For thou desirest not sacrifice,* &c. as elsewhere upon the same occasion, whereof see upon Psal. 40.6. *Mine ears,* &c. Now he does as it were recall, or rather expound himself: *So wilt thou be pleased,* &c. as if he said; care being first taken for those things that are chief, and without which all oblations and sacrifices are rather offensive, than pleasing; to wit, hearty sorrow for our sins, and a serious purpose of amendment; trust in God, &c. (which things he had largely insisted upon in the former verses;) So may I, or any other in my case hope, that other sacrifices, such as God himself, for a time, had prescribed and appointed, may be seasonable and acceptable. *Those ought ye to have done,* &c. Matt. 23.23. As for that phrase, *Sacrifices of righteousness,* see upon Psal. 23.3. *He leadeth me.* But Psalm 4.6. we had *sacrifices of righteousness,* in another sense, as is there noted which is no wonder. It is a common observation among interpreters, that the same words, and phrases, are sometimes taken in very different senses: yea sometimes in one and the same chapter; as elsewhere has been said and showed by some example. However, even there, this sense is followed by some; as if David directed his speech to such of the people, as yet stood in opposition to him: inviting them to submit, that so they might, under him and his government, have part in the legal service. But I still like better of the other sense, in that place. See then upon verse 4. and 5.

# PSALM LII

## TITLE

*{when Doeg the Edomite came and told Saul}* 1 Sam. 22.9.

**Verse 1. ***{O mighty man!}* O Doeg, which makes use of that power you have under Saul, not to do good, but to do mischief; contrary to God, whose constant goodness, even towards sinners, (in many things) should be our example; and the nearer we come to him in power, so should we in goodness also; who daily makes his sun to arise upon the good, and the evil; whose long forbearance invites all men, even greatest offenders, unto repentance; *O thou mighty man;* how ill does wickedness and cruelty become so great a place? And again, *the goodness of God,* &c. though you do encourage yourself, that *the Lord shall not see, neither shall the God of Jacob regard:* Psal. 94.7. because God, for secret reasons, has suffered some of your bloody designs to take effect; yet for all this I shall not cease to trust in the Lord, and his goodness, *God hath not forgotten to be gracious,* &c. yea this very cruelty of yours, *thou mighty man,* shall commend the goodness of God unto me; and make me the more earnestly to cast myself upon him: *Surely the wrath of man shall praise thee,* Psal. 76.10.

**V. 2. ***{Thy tongue deviseth mischiefs}* Psal. 50.19. Your malice moves you by crafty flatteries and lies, to accuse and destroy the innocent.

*{razor}* Psal. 57.4. & 59.7. & 64.3.

*{working deceitfully}* Heb. *O worker of deceit.*

**V. 3. ***{Thou lovest evil more than good}* That is, *rather than good.* For he loved not good at all. So after, *lying rather than to speak righteousness.*

**V. 4. ***{Thou lovest all devouring words}* You speak as if you would devour: and his words did devour the priests, and their city, 1 Sam. 22.18.

*{O thou deceitful tongue}* Or, *and the deceitful tongue.*

**V. 5. ***{God shall likewise destroy thee for ever}* Though God forbear for a time, yet at length he will recompense your falsehood.

*{destroy thee}* Heb. *beat thee down.*

*{and root thee out of the land of the living}* Albeit you seem to be never so sure settled.

**V. 6. ***{The righteous also shall see}* For the eyes of the wicked are shut up at God’s judgments.

*{and fear}* With joyful reverence, seeing he takes their part against the wicked, Psal. 58.10.

**V. 7. ***{Lo, this is the man}* Or, *this is the mighty man,* as some have it here. For though it be not altogether the same word (גבר here: גבור there) that we had before, verse 1. yet both are from one root, which signifies *to be strong and mighty:* so that it is very probable that the same thing is intended in both places; and it makes the speech more emphatical: *Lo this is the man* (*the mighty man*) how great and mighty, in his own conceit, and in the eyes of the world; but very lately; how wretched and miserable? now; how much lower? that is the lowest, now; that was, a little before, so high, and so terrible?

*{trusted in the abundance of his riches}* Psalm 49.6.

*{wickedness}* Or, *substance.*

**V. 8. ***{But I am like a green olive tree}* I shall live prosperously among God’s servants, when he is destroyed.

*{I trust in the mercy of God for ever and ever}* Psal. 5.7.

**V. 9. ***{Because thou hast done it}* Some would have it expressed by the future (according to the Hebrew idiom; of preterits for futures, and futures for preterits, promiscuously: or *more prophetico:* expressing for more certainty things to come, as already passed:) *because thou wilt do it,* &c. but there is no need of it. For it is more probable that David, seeing what had happened to so many others in his cause, and for his sake, through Doeg’s malicious information, and Saul’s cruelty; reflects here upon himself, and praises God (though very sensible of what others had suffered) for his own deliverance, so much the more to be regarded, and esteemed, because so many had suffered for it.

# PSALM LIII

## TITLE

*{Mahalath}* Psalm 88. tit. A wind instrument. See notes on Psal. 5. tit.

**Verse 1. ***{The fool hath said in his heart}* Psal. 10.4. & 14.1, &c. This Psalm agrees with Psalm 14. as Psalm 57. (part of it, from v. 7.) with Psal. 108. to verse 6. and Psal. 60. from verse 5. *That thy beloved,* to the end; with 108. from ver. 6. to the end, and Psalm 70. with Psal. 40. from verse 13. Of some petty differences of some words and letters, how they might happen, see what is said upon Psal. 18. *I will love thee,* &c. compared with 2 Sam. 22. The greatest difference here, is in v. 5. compared with 5. & 6. of Psalm 14. It is likely that it passed twice through David’s hands, upon several occasions; and that he fitted it to the occasion, as he thought best himself.

*{There is no God}* Whereas no regard is had of honesty, or dishonesty, of virtue, nor of vice, there the prophet pronounces, that the people have no God.

*{there is none that doth good}* Rom. 3.10.

**V. 2. ***{That did understand, that did seek God}* Whereby he shows that all knowledge is unprofitable, that brings us not to seek God. See upon Psal. 27.8. *Seek ye my face,* &c.

**V. 3. ***{no not one}* See upon Psal. 14.2. *If there were any.* To which may be added, Eccles. 7.20. *For there is not a just man upon earth, that doth good and sinneth not.* That is, no man, no just man, in common reputation, so constant in goodness, but is sometimes exorbitant: or no action of man, how just and good soever, so entirely good, but if strictly examined, may be found defective, in some circumstance: and therefore no man upon earth absolutely just before God.

**V. 4. ***{Have the workers of iniquity no knowledge?}* David pronounces God’s vengeance against cruel governors, who having charge to defend and preserve God’s people, do most cruelly devour them.

**V. 5. ***{were they in great fear}* Heb. *they feared a fear.*

*{where no fear was}* God put a spirit of fear in them, when no man sought to hurt them, Lev. 26.17,36. Deut. 28.65. Job 15.21. Prov. 28.1. So Saul was afraid of David. But because it is well observed by some learned interpreters, that this does not seem so well to agree with the scope of the Psalmist; to wit, to denounce unto such wicked offenders, mere panic fears, accompanied with no real hurt: I think therefore that Aben Ezra (a learned Rabbin) his interpretation deserves well to be taken into consideration, who makes the meaning of the words (there being no more in the Hebrew than thus: *They shall fear with a fear:* or, *greatly be afraid:* (*no fear is,* or *was:*) to be this: they shall be taken with such a fear, to which never was the like before, that is, as great as may be: by *fear,* understanding the objects themselves; great evils, and unusual plagues; such a fear and fright, as is described Rev. 6.15,16,17. and elsewhere: The said Aben Ezra brings a place of Esay for confirmation of this interpretation, where he conceives that such a word, as *similis,* or *par,* must also be supplied. But that place is doubtful; and otherwise interpreted by others. However, it is sure enough that the Scriptures afford store of examples, where sometimes the adjective, sometimes the substantive, and sometimes the verb is to be supplied, to make the sense perfect. This translation and interpretation of the words is followed by some of the best of late interpreters. However, without any such supplement, we need not fly to those panic fears, to make sense of the words: *where no fear is:* that is, where they can see no just ground of fear, but in their greatest security: (as has been said upon Psal. 14.5. already:) which is no small aggravation of their calamity, that it shall be sudden, without any foregoing suspicion, or apprehension of it in them: which could they have foreseen, they might perchance, if not have prevented, totally; by seasonable repentance: yet have lessened in some measure, by some kind of preparation against it, and such means, as worldly wisdom does prompt and afford in that case.

*{God hath scattered the bones}* Be the enemies’ power never so great or the danger so fearful, yet God delivers his in due time.

*{against thee}* Thee, O Israel, verse 6. So *His,* Psal. 87.1. has relation to the Lord, ver. 2.

**V. 6. ***{O that the salvation}* Heb. *who will give salvations;* &c.

# PSALM LIV

## TITLE

*{Neginoth}* See notes on Psal. 4. tit.

*{when the Ziphims came and said to Saul, Doth not David hide himself with us?}* 1 Sam. 23.19. & 26.1.

**Verse 1. ***{By thy name}* Psal. 113.10,11,12. *All nations,* &c. It is very frequent in the Scriptures to put the *name* of God, for his power, and sometimes for God himself. For his power, because when men (the faithful especially) call upon him, by his Name; that is, as he ought to be called upon; then is the proper time for God to show his power. And that he does so, commonly; the Psalmist declares at large, Psalm 107. throughout the whole Psalm: but especially in these words, *Then they cried unto the Lord,* &c. verse 6,13,19,28. And sometimes, I said for God himself, or elsewhere is observed. See upon Psal. 9.10. *that know thy Name.* One reason, among others, may be, because all the knowledge we have of God, may be called a knowledge of his Name, rather than of his Nature.

**V. 3. ***{strangers are risen up against me}* He called the Ziphims, *strangers;* an odious word among the Hebrews, as who should say, *heathens, infidels.* Who yet were Jews, of the tribe of Judah; so called from the name of the city, (some say there were two of that name) which they inhabited: as the city might have its name from some, either mountain, or desert called Ziph. But why called here *Strangers* by David? Some think because they had apostatized from the true faith, and were become idolaters. Such indeed might very well be so called; and as certain it is that the Jews or Israelites in general, did often deserve that title by their frequent apostasies into idolatrous worship: which are recorded in the history of the Bible. But of those Ziphims we find no such thing particularly noted or recorded, which certainly would not have been omitted (at this time especially of David’s flight, and conversation among them) had there been any such thing. Their opinion therefore is most probable, who by *strangers,* understand inhumane, cruel, perfidious, as bad as any professed heathens and idolaters, as the Apostle 1 Tim. 5.8. *worse than an infidel.* So, *Strange children,* Psalm 144.7. And Psalm 58. *The wicked are estranged from the womb;* so also Psalm 59.5. and 8. by *the heathen,* are intended men of barbarous and heathenish condition, whether Jews or aliens by birth. But Deut. 32.27. *lest their adversaries should behave themselves strangely;* there, (though some there too, take it as here:) yet it does appear by the context, that by that phrase are intended, who for want of either ingenuity, or due consideration, take no notice, or mistake in the true cause, by arrogating that to themselves, which should have been ascribed unto God: as of the Assyrian particularly, Esa. 10.5,6, &c. *Howbeit, he meaneth not so,* &c.

*{oppressors seek after my soul}* Saul and his army, which like cruel beasts could not be satisfied but by his death.

*{they have not set God before them}* Psalm 10.4.

**V. 4. ***{The Lord is with them that uphold my soul}* God is one of them that defend me, Psalm 118.7.

*{with}* Heb. *among.*

**V. 5. ***{He shall reward evil unto mine enemies}* Or, *He shall turn the evil upon mine enemy.* To wit, which he devised against me.

*{mine enemies}* Hebr. *those that observe me.* As Psalm 56.6. & 59.1.

*{cut them off in thy truth}* According to your faithful promise.

**V. 6. ***{I will freely sacrifice unto thee}* Or, *I will sacrifice with a free-will offering unto thee,* Lev. 7.16. Psalm 119.108.

**V. 7. ***{mine eye hath seen his desire upon mine enemies}* We may lawfully rejoice in God’s judgments on the wicked, if our affection be pure; see more upon Psalm 35.4. *Let them be confounded,* &c. and upon Psalm 41. verse 10. & 11. *that I may requite them,* &c.

# PSALM LV

## TITLE

*{Neginoth}* See notes on Psalm 4. tit.

**Verse 1. ***{Hide not thyself from my supplication}* There be that make a difference here between prayer, and supplication, as if David by *prayer* required justice against his enemies; and by *supplication;* favor at the hands of God. It is true that the Hebrew word תפלה here translated *prayer,* comes from a *radix,* that signifies both *to judge,* and *to pray:* the reason whereof may be (if reason must be given for words, which indeed in any language, may best be done in the Hebrew tongue) because in former times both among the Jews, and Gentiles (all, or most) it belonged unto the same, both to judge, and to pray, the same, whether firstborn or others; being for the most part, princes (or governors) who also were priests. Another reason may be, because as all other serious things, so judgment especially, which properly belongs to God only, should begin by prayer; which I believe, we shall find, if we should make a business of it, to have been observed even among heathens. This for the verb which as we said, does signify both; but as for the substantive תפלה, I do not find, by any use of it (by which words ought to be examined) in the Scripture, that it has any reference unto justice or judgment. By Psalm 143, verse 1. *Hear my prayer, O Lord,* &c. it should rather be otherwise. For having said, *Hear my prayer, O Lord, give ear to my supplications; in thy faithfulness answer me, and in thy righteousness:* he presently adds, *And enter not into judgment with thy servant, for in thy sight,* &c. This very book of Psalms, is inscribed, and so was the house of God, by the same word: the one, in the original, *The book of Prayers:* the other, *The house of prayer.* Esay 56.7. Neither, as I conceive, with any reference to either justice, or enemies; but to God’s mercy; though it may so fall out, that God’s justice, and the injustice of enemies may be the subject of some prayers. But there is store of other places, where the said word is used, which might be produced against this observation, if need were. We therefore with all, or most interpreters, take *prayer* and *supplication* here for the same thing; variety of words being used, to make the speech, (as usually) more earnest and emphatical. As for *supplications, prayers, intercessions,* 1 Tim. 2.1. whether, and how to be distinguished, we meddle not with it here, because we will not so far digress.

**V. 2. ***{I mourn in my complaint, and make a noise}* The Hebrew word ואהימה (from whence comes the Latin *rudo:*) here translated *I mourn,* implies a very loud crying: by some expressed, *ululo:* which though according to the proper temper and breeding of some men, may seem not well to become such a man, of that rank, and valor, as David was: in all things of this nature that belong to outward behavior, we must distinguish of both times and nations; some things in that kind being very tolerable, nay commendable in some countries, in men, as well as women, (as of dancing, particularly, is well observed by an ancient Latin historian) which in others would be judged either base, or uncivil. Achilles is described in Homer wallowing himself in ashes, pulling his hairs, and weeping bitterly for the death of his friend, which perchance some other man, not pretending in the least degree, to Achilles his valor and courage in other things, would not think he could do without shaming himself for ever, and incurring the reproach of a very poor spirit. Yet sure enough it is, that although constancy and resolution well become any man; yet sometimes great passions are incidental to men that are very magnanimous.

**V. 3. ***{Because of the voice of the enemy}* For the threatenings of my adversaries.

*{they cast iniquity upon me}* They have defamed me as a wicked person: or, they have imagined my destruction.

*{and in wrath they hate me}* They furiously persecute me, or prosecute their hatred against me. A man may *hate,* or be *an adversary,* as the Hebrew word might also be translated; (and is Psal. 38.20.) and yet not be furious.

**V. 4. ***{my heart is sore pained within me}* Like a woman in travail: for so the word signifies.

**V. 5. ***{horror hath overwhelmed me}* There was no part of him, that was not astonished with extreme fear.

*{overwhelmed me}* Heb. *covered me.*

**V. 6. ***{O that I had wings like a dove, &c.}* Whether doves, as Pliny does avouch, of all birds be of quickest flight or no, I know not: neither do I think it so probable, that David had respect unto that: it is enough that they are birds ordinarily known; and as commonly known to fly very swiftly. It is observed to be a melancholy bird too, and its ordinary tune, or tone, wherein it is heard, has somewhat mournful in it: Esay 38.14. and 59.11. *I did mourn as a dove,* &c. and this was enough to make David (in that case he was in) to think of a dove sooner than of any other bird. But I find that others besides David have used the similitude of a dove, to express a quick flight: φύγεν ⟦ὥς τε?⟧U3 πέλεια, &c. *Hom. il.* φʹ.

**V. 7. ***{wilderness}* Rev. 12.14.

**V. 8. ***{from the windy storm}* From the fury and violence of my enemies.

**V. 9. ***{Destroy, O Lord}* Hebr. *swallow.* He alludes to the destruction of Dathan and Abiram, Num. 16.30,33. as appears, verse 15.

*{divide their tongues}* That is, (as words, are often taken for deeds) divide and confound their counsels; that so divided, and contrary the one to the other, they may not prevail in their wickedness; or wicked designs. Haply, this phrase and manner of speech might have its beginning from what happened at the building of the Tower of Babel.

**V. 10. ***{mischief also and sorrow are in the midst of it}* All laws and good orders are broken, and only vice and dissolution reigns.

**V. 11. ***{Wickedness is in the midst thereof}* Heb. *wickednesses:* that is, abundance of wickedness.

**V. 12. ***{it was not an enemy}* If mine open enemy had sought mine hurt, I could have borne it more patiently, and have prevented it more easily, Psal. 41.9.

**V. 13. ***{a man, mine equal, my guide, and mine acquaintance}* Hebr. *a man according to my rank.*

*{mine equal}* One raised by me as high as myself, well nigh; my counselor, my friend, and companion in piety.

**V. 14. ***{We took sweet counsel}* Heb. *who sweetened counsel.*

**V. 15. ***{Let death seize upon them}* Of these and the like imprecations, see, upon Psal. 35.4. *Let them be confounded,* &c.

*{Let them go down quick into hell}* See notes on ver. 9.

*{hell}* Or, *the grave.*

**V. 17. ***{Evening and Morning, and at noon will I pray}* Dan. 6.10.

*{and cried aloud}* See notes on ver. 2.

**V. 18. ***{for there were many with me}* Or, *for with many they were fighting with me;* or *against me,* Psal. 94.16.

**V. 19. ***{Because they have no changes, therefore they fear not God}* Or, *with whom also there be no changes, yet they fear not God.* If *changes* be referred to their temporal estates and welfare; as Job 10.17. (it is the same word there, as here חליפות) *changes and war are against me:* then, according to the first translation, *because,* &c. a reason is given of their perseverance in wickedness, and contempt of God, to wit, their constant and uninterrupted worldly prosperity. See Deut. 8.12,13, &c. *Lest when thou hast eaten and art full,* &c. Therefore David elsewhere praised God, that he had known adversity, *It is good for me that I have been afflicted,* &c. Psalm 119.71. and 67. *Before I was afflicted,* &c. and the Apostle Heb. 12.8. infers, that they that are left *without chastisement, are bastards, and not sons.* Or, according to the second, *with whom—yet,* &c. it is a great aggravation of their impenitency, that notwithstanding so much goodness vouchsafed unto them, they should continue so unthankful, as to requite so ill; or so stupid and insensible, as not to acknowledge the author. But if *changes* be referred, as by many, to the soul; then the meaning is, that through long use and continuance of sinning, they are through God’s just judgment, become altogether obdurate and inflexible: and therefore no wonder, if nothing work upon them to their conversion. *Can the Ethiopian change his skin, or the Leopard his spots? then may ye also do good, that are accustomed to do evil,* Jer. 13.23. But this *changes,* might also have another meaning. The Grecians use to say, στρεπταὶ φρένες ἐσθλῶν, that the minds, or hearts, of good men are changeable: their meaning is, that good men are merciful. *Quo quisque est major, magis est placabilis iras: Et faciles motus mens generosa capit:* as the Latin poet expresses it. He may therefore say, that they show by their cruel unmercifulness, that they have no fear, or sense of God at all. Else they would fear him, whose mercy themselves stood in so much need of; and consider that they, whom they so fiercely persecute, are his creatures, as well as they.

**V. 20. ***{He hath put forth his hands}* I did not provoke him, but was at peace with him, yet he made war against me.

*{He hath broken}* Heb. *he hath profaned.*

**V. 22. ***{Cast thy burden upon the Lord}* Psal. 37.5. Mat. 6.25. Luke 12.22. 1 Pet. 5.7.

*{burden}* Or, *gift.*

*{he shall never suffer the righteous to be moved}* To come to a miserable condition, Psa. 15.5. See upon Psalm 37.

**V. 23. ***{bloody and deceitful men}* Heb. *men of bloods and deceit.*

*{bloody and deceitful men shall not live out half their days}* They shall die in the flower of their age, who by the course of nature might live so long, Job 15.32,33. Prov. 10.27. Psal. 37.38. As by wickedness, so by folly, and carelessness, a man may hasten his death; and die before his time: in which case nevertheless, in justification of their own, or other men’s wilful folly, or carelessness, many will be apt to say, that his or their time was come: which is foolish and impious.

*{bloody and deceitful men}* We may take the words severally for, neither bloody, nor deceitful men: untimely deaths being denounced unto both elsewhere, as to the bloody, notoriously, in divers places, so to the fraudulent and deceitful: Psal. 34.12,13. *What man is he,* &c. and elsewhere. Nevertheless, it is more likely that both words are here intended of one, who is both perfidious, (a great aggravation of cruelty) and cruel, as may be gathered from verse 21. *The words of his mouth,* &c. See also Psal. 140.11. *an evil speakers,* &c.

*{shall not live out half their days}* Heb. *shall not half their days.* See, as before, upon Psal. 37.

# PSALM LVI

## TITLE

*{Jonath-elem-rechokim}* The dumb dove in far countries: That is, himself, who being chased by the fury of his enemies into a strange country, he was a dumb dove: or, *of the fold oppressed by a troop of foreigners*. See more upon psal. 58.1. *O Congregation.*

*{Michtam of David}* Or, *a golden psalm of David.*

*{Philistines took him in Gath}* 1 Sam. 21.11.

**Verse 1. ***{Man would swallow me up}* The fierceness of our enemies is an inducement to God to hasten our deliverance, and to appear to us; when we can plead nothing else for ourselves. God is an enemy to all violence, and it is one of his titles, to be the defender of those, that have no other defense. *All my bones shall say, Lord who is like unto thee, who deliverest the poor from him,* &c. Psal. 35.10. Yea, though our cause be good, and God himself has given us the best of our enemy; yet to take the utmost of what we might by way of just revenge or resentment; is not good for us; as neither to triumph overmuch, *Rejoice not when thine enemy falleth,* &c. Prov. 24.17,18. See there.

**V. 2. ***{O thou most High}* Having mentioned oppression in the verse below, we may well conceive, that by this expression here, *O thou most high,* he does put God in mind of his justice: for which see upon Psalm 7.7. *for their sakes return thou on high.* To which may be added, Psalm 92.8. *But thou Lord art most high for evermore:* speaking there of the present prosperity of the wicked, and God’s future judgments, verse 7. and 9. See Job 31.23. *For destruction from God was a terror unto me, and by reason of his Highness I could not endure.* Other places of Scripture might be added, which without this observation may be thought obscure. The want of it here has made some to devise another translation: *from on high,* instead of, *O thou most high,* and they are put to it, to make sense of it.

*{Mine enemies}* Heb. *mine observers.*

**V. 4. ***{In God I will praise his word}* Some conceive the order of the words to be somewhat inverted; as if he intended, *I will praise God for his word:* See upon psal. 138.2. *for thou hast magnified,* &c. But I conceive them in that order we have them here, more pregnant and emphatical, repeated also in the same order, verse 10. *In God,* &c. *in God* therefore, I take it here as intended in opposition to visible hopes, or present appearance, as in Hab. 3.17,18,19. *Although the fig-tree shall not blossom,* &c. *yet in the Lord* (which is the order of the Hebrew words, and would have done better in the English too, if I be not mistaken) *will I rejoice, I will joy in the God of my salvation. The Lord,* &c. There is much affinity between these two places, if well considered and compared. *In God,* then I say, *I will,* &c. that is, although my case and present condition on every side seems most desperate, not any one thing does appear whereon I can ground any hope of future amendment: yet *in God I will praise,* or *glory:* for the original word signifies both; glory in his word, or praise his word: that is, praise him for his promises made unto me for my establishment, protection, &c. whereof though there be little appearance at present; yet praise him nevertheless in full confidence, that what he has promised, shall in due time (an argument of a great and vigorous faith, to God most acceptable) be accomplished. Besides, in this very word, *word,* being spoken of God, there is an intimation of power; yea of omnipotency: very seasonable to be remembered in time of such extremity; where nothing but a miracle (as it were) can save us. *By the word of the Lord,* &c. *For he spake,* &c. Psalm 33.6,9. And the Centurion in the Gospel to Christ, *speak the word only, and my servant shall be healed:* Matth. 8.8. And again psalm 107.20. *He sent his word, and healed them,* and psalm 147.18. *He sendeth out his word,* &c.

*{praise his word}* Or, *glory,* because of *his word,* Rom. 5.2.

*{I will not fear what flesh can do unto me}* psalm 118.6. Hebr. 13.6.

**V. 6. ***{They gather themselves together}* Many lay plots how to bring me to the grave.

*{mark my steps when they wait for my soul}* Job 13.27.

**V. 7. ***{shall}* Or, *ought.*

*{shall they escape by iniquity?}* It may be read thus, *It is in vain to spare them.* To wit, because they are past mending, Isa. 26.10. Or thus, *Their safety is iniquity:* That is, they trouble others, that they may escape themselves from the punishment of their sins: Or, it is not fit they should escape by their wicked courses.

**V. 8. ***{put thou my tears into thy bottle}* This expression is used, the better to secure us; (which to a man in great misery is a great comfort) that God does take notice, very particular notice of all that we suffer: that every faithful sigh and tear, (in a just cause, and in humble submission to him:) is registered in his memory, to whom all things past and future, always present: nay dear and precious unto him, every such sigh and tear; as those things are unto men commonly, which are carefully kept and preserved in bottles. But the Hebrew word נאד is properly *uter* (as most translate) or a leather pot, or bottle, fit to receive liquid things. And some observe an allusion in the words, נוד *wandering,* and נאד *a bottle;* which though they be different in writing, yet are the same (*Nad*) in pronunciation: the Scripture being full of such allusions, which (among other things) makes the original context incomparably sweeter to the reader, than any translation can be. As for David’s wanderings here mentioned, we know by the history that they were many, and troublesome. Some have reckoned them (you may see them in Genebrard) unto twelve: to equal them with Hercules his labors, as I conceive. The Septuagint, not altogether improperly, have expressed *my wanderings,* by τὴν ζωήν μου, as if David had intended, that his life was but a perpetual wandering: or more generally, that every man’s life, a godly man’s especially, is rather a pilgrimage, than a station or settled abode. And because I have mentioned the Septuagint, and given occasion perchance to my reader to turn to them; lest he find himself puzzled, (which he may very well except he be well versed in them, and acquainted in their way:) I will help him out before I pass to other matter. They translate: Ὁ θεὸς τὴν ζωήν μου ἐξήγγειλά σοι: *thou,* &c. *O God,* is taken from the end of the former verse. For ספרתה they did read ספרתי or מספרתי in the first person: and for נאד in the words immediately following, it is very likely that they read נגדך ἐνώπιόν σου. But enough of them. There is very good use to be made of them many times: but it requires more than ordinary labor and judgment to find out the ground of all their mistakes, or differences.

**V. 10. ***{praise his word}* See verse 4.

**V. 12. ***{Thy vows are upon me}* I shall be bound to pay my vows of thanksgiving, as I promised, when I shall receive what I prayed for, as the bond lies heavy upon an honest debtor, till he have paid it.

**V. 13. ***{wilt not thou deliver my feet from falling?}* As you have delivered me before; so I doubt not but you will now, 2 Cor. 1.10. See before upon psalm 22. v. 21. *For thou hast heard me,* &c.

*{that I may walk before God}* As mindful of his great mercies, and giving him thanks for the same.

*{in the light of the living?}* That is, in this life, and light of the sun, Job 33.30.

# PSALM LVII

## TITLE

*{Altaschith}* Or, *destroy not.* It shows the subject of the Psalm, a deprecation of perdition. Others there be, who think these words have reference to those, 1 Samuel 26.9. *Destroy him not,* being David’s words to Abishai, forbidding him to kill Saul. The same word is there, as is here. Others again, that this was a common title, belonging to certain prayers made to be used in danger of death. But seeing there be divers other Psalms that have this title besides this, (as psalm 58,59,75.) and all other things well weighed; that which can be said of it with most probability, is, that it was the beginning of some known song, at the tune of which this was to be sung. If this be not, we must say, as we have already said more than once concerning these titles in general, that they are altogether unknown, which is the confession of the most learned both Jews and others; so that it is but labor lost, to recite variety of opinions upon every one. See upon psalm 7. and others following.

*{when he fled from Saul in the cave}* 1 Sam. 24.1.

**Verse 1. ***{Until these calamities be overpast}* He compares the afflictions which God lays upon his children to a storm that comes and goes, Isa. 26.20. Tacitly encouraging himself thereby, as a traveler upon the way, that it is not a thing to be wondered at, (which therefore those that are wise use to provide against before they set out) nor like to last very long. There is some anomaly in the original words: *praeteriet aerumnas, vel calamitates:* a noun plural joined to a verb singular: the reason whereof may be perchance, to intimate, that though his calamities were many, and of different kinds; yet they came so thick one upon another, that they might seem but one, divided into many branches. *Deep calleth unto deep,* &c. psalm 42.7. see there. and so it is with sudden showers too, which oftentimes come one after another, divers in a little compass of time.

*{until their calm}* In the word *refuge,* there is an intimation of hiding, as appears more plainly by that passage of Esay, but now quoted, such a refuge David would need no longer than the storm or calamity did last. But of God’s protection in general we stand in need, as well in prosperity as adversity: and it is the part of a wise man, in prosperity, to think of adversity. However, this *until,* does not always so limit, but that the thing spoken of, may extend much longer, yea and perchance to eternity, as amounting to an *Always,* or *never:* as Matth. 1.25. *And knew her not, till she had,* &c. there be many examples of it in the Scripture.

**V. 2. ***{performeth all things for me}* psal. 138.8. It is the same word there, as is here: which does confirm this interpretation of it, doubtful of itself otherwise, and by divers interpreters diversely translated. It is a glorious title, and fit for Almighty God, to be a performer: men, besides that they are all mutable in their wills, and many, perfidious; they can (absolutely) warrant nothing either to themselves or others, because of the uncertainty of their times. psalm 146.3,4,5. and James 4.13,14. *Go to,* &c.

*{performeth all things}* There is no more in the original than, but only *performeth:* If we supply, *all things,* it must be understood, either all things that he has promised unto me, hitherto: not likely therefore now to fail me: Or, *all things,* as disclaiming all confidence in himself, and secondary means, (see upon Psalm 33.16. *There is no King saved,* &c.) but ascribing all unto God, and his blessing. God also *performs all things* for us, when he does fit us for all things that happen unto us, and for all conditions, though never so different: Phil. 4.12,13. *I know both how to be abased,* &c. *I can do all things through Christ,* &c. Lastly, *all things,* that is, all things that I have desired, and have been an humble and earnest suitor for: of which see upon Psa. 21.2. *his heart’s desire.*

**V. 3. ***{He shall send from heaven}* He would rather deliver me by miracle; than let me be overcome, Psalm 18.16. Judg. 5.20.

*{From the reproach of him that would swallow me up}* Or, *he reproacheth him that would swallow me up.*

**V. 4. ***{whose teeth are spears}* He means their calumnies, and false reports, whereby they went about to take away his life, Psal. 35.15,16. & 55.21. & 59.7. and Prov. 25.18. *A man that beareth.*

**V. 5. ***{Be thou exalted}* Deliver me, that it may be known how great you are.

**V. 6. ***{They have prepared a net for my steps}* Psalm 7.16. and 9.15.

*{my soul is bowed down}* Some by *soul,* here, understand *body:* which we know is ordinary enough. But I do not believe that David speaks this in reference to his body, to be literally taken, as though very fear forced him to contract his body; or that he bowed himself, to avoid their ambushes: as some here, more than one. I understand him rather of his heart and spirits, much dejected with continual woes and persecutions, as Psalm 107.12. *He brought down their heart with labour,* &c. and what more grievous than a dejected heart, and drooping spirits? For, *A man’s spirit will bear his infirmity, but a wounded spirit,* &c. Prov. 18.14. according to the common interpretation. No wonder therefore, if *none to help,* &c. (but God Almighty) if once their spirit, or *hearts be quite fallen down,* as in the words but a little before: so also, and in a like sense, Psalm 145.14. *The Lord upholdeth all that fall, and raiseth up all those that be bowed down,* and Psalm 146. verse 8. *The Lord openeth——The Lord raiseth,* &c. See there.

**V. 7. ***{My heart is fixed}* That is, wholly bent to give you praise for my deliverance. Compare with Psalm 108.1,2. &c. and see the like Psalm 53.1. &c. See there.

*{fixed}* Or, *prepared.*

**V. 8. ***{Awake up my Glory}* That is, my tongue. See notes on Psalm 16.9.

**V. 10. ***{For thy mercy is great unto the Heavens}* Psalm 36.5. and 108.4. As it were impertinent to seek a difference (though different of themselves) between *the heavens, and the clouds;* both being used here, to express infinite height; and that infinite height, to set out the greatness of God’s mercy and truth: So I think there is no need that we should distinguish neither here, nor Psalm 36.5. where the same words are, between *mercy,* and *truth,* but take them both for one thing: I mean, the *truth* should be taken for the same as *mercy:* as ἀλήθεια in Greek is sometimes put for justice: and *verus* in Latin, for *aequum,* which comes somewhat near to *mercy.* But Psalm 85.10. *mercy and truth are met together:* there must of necessity be some difference, to make sense of the words. The difference which most expositors pitch upon generally, is, that *mercy* immediately proceeds from God’s goodness and compassion: *truth* is the performance of a promised mercy: mercy, in its original; but justice, or faithfulness, in the performance. So here, in this verse, and so elsewhere they are commonly distinguished: and here indeed, as in divers other places, it may do very well: if we like best to distinguish them: which, as I said before, is not absolutely needful. But Psalm 85.10. before mentioned, where we must admit (to make sense) of a difference, yet how the word *truth* should there be taken, great question is made by expositors, and not easy by any man to be resolved. See more there.

# PSALM LVIII

## TITLE

*{Altaschith}* Or, *destroy not, a golden Psalm of David.* See notes on Psalm 57. tit.

**Verse 1. ***{O Congregation?}* In the Hebrew, אלם about which word, in this place, there is great controversy, and when all is done, little certainty. The proper and usual signification of the word, as it comes from a root that either signifies to bind sheaves, or to be mute, (which is a kind of binding of the tongue) so it would imply some either collection, or silence. Both which senses are followed by some interpreters: some translating, *Are ye mute indeed? Do ye speak Justice?* Or, *Of a truth, do ye speak dumb Justice?* That is, say they, Do you boast of justice, when in very deed, justice is dumb, and opens not her mouth: or, *is tongue-tied*. Others, as it is here, *Do yee indeed——O Congregation.* The first sense is not thought to fit here so well: and therefore the second, *O Congregation,* is by most preferred. But a great exception may be, that the word is found nowhere else in this sense: except we should follow Aben Ezra, who finds it in the title of the six and fiftieth Psalm; interpreting those words there על יונת אלם which by others are generally translated, *De columba obmutescentiae,* That is, *of the silent,* or, *dumb dove:* *De oppressione manipuli: Of the oppression of the Congregation:* that is, says he, Whereby the just and upright is oppressed by the wicked congregation. But he is followed by none, I know of. Neither of these interpretations therefore, of which the one is more proper to the word, the other though more proper to the place, can go for better, than bare conjectures. And it makes the matter so much the more uncertain, that by the Septuagint whom Saint Jerome, and some others follow, the word is taken for a mere adverb, or, *expletiva particula,* as some call it (ἄρα is their word) Εἰ ἀληθῶς ἄρα δικαιοσύνην λαλεῖτε *Si vere utique justitiam loquimini:* as the vulgar Latin expresses it, *Do ye in very deed speak righteousness?* Out of all this, what to conclude upon, that may be certain and satisfactory, is not easy: but what I conceive very probable, though not yet thought upon by any, that I know, is this: That the Scripture has many secret allusions of words, in several kinds; some, with some little alteration of the words, and some, mental only, is a thing certain. See somewhat to this purpose, upon the titles of Psalms 7. & 9. Many examples might be produced, some whereof have already been observed by others, and some not yet observed, were this a place fit for it. See upon Psal. 106.15. *but sent leanness,* &c. and verse 28. *the sacrifice of the dead.* Briefly thus, then: Great men and judges of the earth, are often styled in the Scripture אלים sometimes translated *Gods,* and sometimes, *mighty.* אלהים (which comes very near) Psalm 82.1. where we have both; both אלים (but in the singular there, though translated plural) and אלהים: *God standeth in the congregation of the mighty; he judgeth among the gods.* Most probable therefore it is that the same word (אלים) was here intended also; but of purpose so varied, by leaving out the *Jod* only, closely to intimate their incapacity, or ill carriage in their places (and therefore not worthy the name) in general: but particularly, their silence, or tongue-tiedness (if we may so speak) through fear of Saul, (as is likely, and is often observed by interpreters) in his cause, against their own consciences. An allusion not much unlike to this, we have Deuteronomy chap. 32. verse 15. where *Israel,* by a feigned name alluded to that of *Israel,* is called *Jeshurun:* from ישר *rectitudo,* (as most conceive) but certainly, not without a secret allusion to שור, (an ox) to which, for their unruliness and wantonness they are there compared: *But Jeshurun waxed fat and kicked,* as the text has it there. Neither is it material (which by some is objected) that the word is found in other places too, where there is not the like ground for this allusion: since there, where it is first found and used, there is, apparently: which is enough.

**V. 2. ***{You weigh the violence of your hands in the earth}* You seem to proceed by law, against men, weighing their deeds and rewards; but indeed you weigh out wrong for right. Under color of law and justice (the emblem whereof weights are) you oppress men publicly: implied in these words, *in the earth,* as they are expounded by some.

**V. 3. ***{The wicked are estranged from the womb}* An hyperbolical expression of inveterate and incorrigible wickedness; as if it were born with them: For *Natura non mutatur;* they say commonly; nature is not easily changed. So Joh. chapter 9. verse 34. *Thou wast altogether born in sinne:* where see learned Grotius; and before, upon Psalm 22. verse 9. *that took me out of the womb.* See also upon Psalm 54.3. *Strangers,* &c.

*{estranged}* Eph. 4.18.

*{from the womb}* Isai. 48.8.

*{as soon as they be born}* Heb. *from the belly.*

**Ver. 4. ***{Like the poison}* Hebrew, *according to the likeness.*

*{they are like the deaf adder}* They will hear nothing that may inform them in the right.

*{adder}* Or, *asp.*

*{stoppeth her ear}* Acts 7.57.

**V. 5. ***{To the voice of Charmers, charming never so wisely}* This must be taken but as proverbially spoken (as is most likely, that there was some common speech among the Hebrews, then in use to that purpose. See also, Ecclesiastes, the tenth chapter, and the eleventh verse, Jeremy chap. 8. verse 17.) by David, and not with any purpose to maintain, either the truth of the thing in matter of fact; or the lawfulness of it, in relation to God and his law. For first, as concerning the first, whether there be any such charms effectual: Expositors indeed upon this place tell us of some, that have said or written of such deaf adders, which by a natural instinct and providence, use to lay one ear close to the ground, and stop the other with their tails, because they should not hear the voice of the charmer: they name Jerome, Augustinus, Cassiodor: (I had rather they had named some naturalists) but how many things in this kind are commonly said, and by many believed, which upon further search are, and have been found false, is sufficiently known. Pliny in his Natural History (a man otherwise much censured for his too great credulity) says of charms in general, *maxime quaestionis, & semper incerta est, valeantne aliquid verba & incantamenta carminum.* And elsewhere more particularly of these kind of charms; *Varia circa haec opinio, ex ingenio cujusque, vel casu, mulceri alloquiis feras, quippe ubi etiam serpentes extrahi cantu, cogique in poenam, verum falsumve sit, vita non decreverit.* Plin. lib. 8. c. 16. By occasion of which words it will not be amiss to observe, that the Hebrews also constitute a double charming; the one of greater beasts, which they call *The great,* another of creeping things, serpents, scorpions, &c. which they call *The less charming;* as is observed by Buxtorf, out of the Talmudists. But however Pliny makes the matter very doubtful; they that have seen mastiff dogs, of the fiercest of that kind, such as tanners keep, when ready to fly and fasten, suddenly with a little piping, in a very plain way, without any art, (that can be observed or discerned) first stilled and tamed; and then soon after laid asleep: not to speak of, what has been done by the same man upon fierce bulls, in the view of many: they I say, that have seen this, as many in Sussex have done, and can bear witness if need be; will not make any great question, but that such an art there is, or at least such a practice; and that the thing is feasible: but by what art or power, either natural, or magical, is another question. I am confident, by whatever I could hear or learn of him in Sussex, but now spoken of, that he is not suspected by any that know him, to use any unlawful means. Neither indeed is it probable, all things considered. But such charming they say, is forbidden in the law, and therefore magical. It is true indeed, the word is there in the Law, Deut. 18.11. *Or a Charmer,* &c. it is the same word (חבר) as is here also; but what is meant by that word, is not there expressed: nor whether the prohibition does extend to all kind of words or actions in that kind that may be comprehended under that general word. For so we may say of the word לחש too, which is here also, translated here *charmer,* but elsewhere, *a whisperer,* Psal. 41.7. (it is in the verb there) and, *an eloquent Orator,* or *skillful of speech,* Esay 3.3. But it is not our aim to make a business of it here, neither is there any need: all we would infer, is but this, that David’s mentioning of them in this place, is no argument for the truth of the thing, in point of history and reality of fact: since many things are spoken in the Scripture according to common opinion only, or grounded upon some vulgar speech; much less for the lawfulness of it.
