So in the Latin tongue, for example, no man makes any scruple to use *Sirenum voces;* and *Cygnea cantio,* and the like, proverbially; though he believed not the reality of either such sirens in nature, or of such singing of the swans before their death.

**V. 6. ***{Break their teeth}* Take away all occasions and means, whereby they hurt, Psal. 3.7.

**V. 7. ***{Let them melt away as waters}* The same similitude or much to that purpose, taken from water, we had before, Psa. 22.14. *I am poured out;* to which we may add 2 Sam. 14.14. *For we must needs die,* &c. Of imprecations in general, see upon Psa. 35.4. *Let them be confounded.*

*{bendeth his bow to shoot his arrows}* Ps. 64.3. of the wicked there also; which we the rather take notice of, because some (with the vulgar Latin: at least, as interpreted by most:) think it may as well be referred unto God; *when he;* to wit, God: mentioned in the former verse: *when he bendeth——let them be even cut off.* The Hebrew will bear it, we confess: but comparing this with that other place, it is more probable that here also this bending should be intended of the wicked. The former words also, *which are continued,* will bear another translation: *and pass away:* or *come to nothing,* to wit, the wicked: but this we have here, does as well, or rather better, and the sense, however comes to one.

**V. 8. ***{the untimely birth of a woman}* In the Hebrew, נפל אשת which some of the learned Hebrews put together in one, נפלאשת, which they interpret *talpam,* a mole. But if that be their chiefest reason, because תנשמת, Levit. 10.30. by some there translated *a mole,* is by the Thargum rendered אשותא, there is little reason they should be followed by others in it, since that (besides other considerations) what the said תנשמת is put there for, (Levit. 11. verse 18. & 30. of a different signification in those two verses of that one chapter, being the name of a bird, in the 18. and of a reptile, or creeping thing in the 30. as appears by the context, and all interpreters agree) is not agreed upon. It is translated by some *talpa,* by others *glis,* and by some, *salamandra.* This Rabbinical conceit I had not so much as mentioned, had not some of the most sober and approved expositors, thought it worthy to be taken into consideration: Job 3.16. *Or as an hidden untimely birth, I had not been, as infants which never saw light:* may serve, if need be, for a further confirmation of the translation here followed. See also Eccles. 6.3,5.

**V. 9. ***{Before your pots can feel the thorns}* Of the drift and scope of the words in general, that the suddenness of wicked men’s destruction, often insisted upon, not by David only in other psalms, but by Job, and others, upon this argument; is here set out by a similitude; of this, there is no question at all. So much the context of itself will enforce, and all interpreters apprehend it so. But what is the similitude, and of the meaning, and right ordering of particular words, there is not like consent, as may appear by variety of translations, some of them so unlike to one another, as a man would hardly take them for translations of the same verse. From the word סיר סירות in the feminine plural, as it is here, is the chiefest difference: which as it signifies *pots,* (and so indeed most usually in the feminine) so *thorns* also; in which notion the masculine is more usual. Which consideration especially, has moved some of the most approved, to render the words as they are here expressed, *Before your pots can feel the thorns,* or rather according to the propriety of the Hebrew word, (for *thorns* is a more general word) *can feel the brambles;* that is, the fire of brambles. In further confirmation, we are referred to Eccles. 7.6. where such a fire is also proverbially mentioned, *For as the crackling* (or, *sound*) *of thorns under a pot.* I do not deny but this may be to the purpose: yet it is to be observed withal, that the word there for *thorns,* is not the same as is here, but סירים: but the words are כי כקול הסירים תחת הסיר: Psal. 118.12. *they are quenched as the fire of thorns:* that is, a fire that burns with great noise, and is very fierce for the present, but is soon out. The word there for thorns, is קוץ. But to go on, and see what we can make of the rest, according to this translation of the first words, *Before your pots can feel the brambles,* (or fire of brambles, which uses to be quick, but of little continuance) *he shall take them away as with a whirlwind;* so far interpreters agree; (those, I mean that agree in סירות here to be translated *ollae,* or *pots*) but not in that which follows, which indeed is obscure: כמו חי כמו חרון *both living, and in his wrath:* (or, *as living, as wrath*) is the English here, not much clearer than the Hebrew is. The word חי *living,* properly, is sometimes used of raw flesh: as 1 Sam. 2.15. And because raw flesh and pots have some affinity, many interpreters follow that signification here, and make this sense of the words, That as raw flesh is snatched out of the pot, or, flesh snatched out of the pot being yet raw, before the heat of that light bramble fire be come to it; so God, &c. With this bare similitude, the greater part content themselves. But some go further, and would have the plots and machinations of wicked men against the righteous, understood by this fire of brambles, (the sharpest of thorns) and by the *raw flesh,* the righteous that are persecuted by them, to be meant by the power and providence of God towards them, often rescued out of the hands of their enemies, and the very jaws of hell, before any hurt came to them. And to help this, they think this חי (*living*) may be referred to the bramble; *He shall take it away, as it were quick and living,* to wit, the bramble, by which they understand the violent persecutors of the godly. Certainly, they are most put to it, that are most accurate upon this place, to make that sense which they like best, to agree with the words. They come off clearest, in my judgment, as to the sense, and keep to the words too, not worse than those we have heard hitherto, who taking this חי (*living*) of the persecutors of God’s people, (a third interpretation) render the whole verse thus; *Priusquam caldaria vestra (vel lebetes) sentiant rhamnum; sic vos quasi viventes absorbebit ira, (Dei) quasi turbine:* that is, *before your pots can feel the bramble, so shall the wrath* (of God) *swallow you up as quick;* (or, *yet living*) *as it were with a whirlwind.* Were there not a double כמו, or, *particula similitudinis* in the Hebrew (כמו חי כמו חרון) I should make little question, but that we might rest here, and spare our labor of seeking further. But the omission of this repeated כמו, wherein some *emphasis* seems to lie; which is lost by the loss of one of them, and by disordering the words before which they are prefixed; makes us to suspect all the rest. To pass therefore from these to that other rank of interpreters; not much inferior to these, either for their number, or authority; who translate the controverted סירות *thorns,* to this effect; Before that young slipings of thorns can grow to a perfect bramble; God, &c. changing but the terms of the similitude, thorns, or pots, &c. In all the rest, their expositions agree with the former; some content themselves with the bare similitude, to express the suddenness of God’s vengeance; and some by these *young thorns,* understand besides, the troubles and sufferings of the godly, by the persecution of wicked men; this, and what else was said before of the former, which we shall not need to repeat. In very deed, it has proved a thorny place to interpreters; and they that read them diligently, will find as much intricacy in their expositions, as they have found in the text. As for the double כמו wherein, as I have said, I am most unsatisfied, Junius, of all the rest, gives me best content; he expresses them both, and keeps the *emphasis:* but otherwise much different from all the rest, in the rest of the words. I will not undertake that he only is in the right: but I think I should do him, and the reader wrong, if I should not take notice of his version too, which is this: *Quum necdum sentientur spinae vestrae, spinae cynosbati: tam redivivam, quam adustam procelles quamque.* that is, *Before your thorns can be felt; even your bramble thorns:* (most sharp and offensive, says he, in his notes there: that is, before you can do much hurt) *he will drive away,* (or *bruise in pieces*) *every one of them as with a storm; both that which is quick, and that which is dry.* For, says he in his notes, *vernante cynosbato,* &c. when the bramble does spring, some of the thorns are quick and alive; and some withered and dried up. For conclusion, I would have the reader to consider, that it is but a proverbial speech, (which kind, in all languages, is of most difficult investigation) not any matter of doctrine, about which all this controversy is; so that if he be not fully satisfied, it need not trouble him much, as it does not me, who profess I am not.

**V. 10. ***{The righteous shall rejoice}* With a pure affection.

*{he shall wash his feet in the blood of the wicked}* An hyperbolical, and emphatical expression of a great and glorious victory, as Psalm 68.23. borrowed from the language (if not an usage too) of triumphant conquerors. In ordinary language we say, *to trample over one,* that is, to use with scorn and contempt. The style of the psalms being altogether poetical, such expressions become it the better. Of desire of revenge, and passionate imprecations, see before upon Psalm 35. *Let them be confounded,* &c. and Psalm 41.10. *that I may requite them.*

**V. 11. ***{Verily there is a reward for the righteous}* It is always the part and property of one that is truly religious and godly, to refer all things to God’s glory, especially: to make that upon all occasions the chief object of his sorrowing, or rejoicing. As therefore when wicked men prosper in their wicked designs against God’s people, and his truth; it is the course of the world to judge by the event, and either justify the wicked, and his cause, as favored and approved by God; or to question the providence of God, and to doubt whether he take any notice, or regard what is done by men, whether good or bad: which grieves and afflicts pious souls, far more than their own personal sufferings: So on the other side, when God does execute just judgments, by which ordinary men, that have no other ground for their religion, but their senses, and present sight, are wont to judge of God, and his providence; (Psalm 9.16. *God is known,* &c.) because God’s truth, and his worship is then and thereby much advanced; the same truly pious, and devoted to God, receive greatest comfort from such alterations, suffers more as it has reference to God, and his cause; than to their own particular interest, though much, otherwise concerned. See also upon Psalm 43.3. *O send out light.*

*{a reward for the}* Heb. *fruit of the, &c*.

# PSALM LIX

## TITLE

*{Altaschith, Michtam of David}* *Destroy not, a golden Psalm of David,* Psalm 58. tit.

*{when Saul sent, and they watched the house to kill him}* 1 Samuel 19.11.

**Verse 1. ***{Deliver me from mine enemies}* It is the common course of all men, that pretend to any piety, or knowledge of God, to pray unto God against their enemies, if they have any: but it must be the care of all truly and solidly religious, to consider well first, what enemies they pray against: whether such as David’s enemies; who violently persecuted him, without any just provocation; yea though he sought for peace; and did his utmost to win them by his gentleness, and the like; as himself witnesses in divers places: and here particularly, as verses the second, third, and fourth. So may we hope our prayers may be available; at least not unacceptable. But if we make men our foes, by any proud, insolent, or unjust actions; or if upon apprehension of just provocation, we think to bear ourselves upon God’s protection, or assistance, before ourselves have used all fair and charitable means (which God himself requires at our hands) of reconciliation; we may sooner expect to make God our enemy by our prayers; than that he will grant us our request, upon so weak and ungodly grounds.

*{defend me from them that rise up against me}* Heb. *set me on high.* That is, set me where my foes cannot reach me, Psal. 101.47. *yet setteth he the Prince on high.*

**V. 3. ***{not for my transgression}* For I am innocent to themwards, and have not offended them, Psal. 7.4. & 35.7.

**V. 4. ***{Awake to help me}* God seems to sleep when good men are oppressed, Psalm 35.23.

*{to help me}* Heb. *to meet me.*

*{behold}* Consider in what danger I am.

**V. 5. ***{awake to visit all the heathen}* Saul and his watchmen that carry themselves cruelly against me like heathen men, or, some heathen might be among them, ver. 8. and upon Psalm 54.4. *strangers.*

*{be not merciful to any wicked transgressors}* Seeing it appertains to God to punish the wicked, he desires God to execute his vengeance on the malicious persecutors of his Church. It is not their souls that he prays against, or their conversion; but their temporal welfare, and further prospering in their wicked courses. See also upon Psal. 69.28. *out of the book of the living.*

**V. 6. ***{They return at the evening}* They go to hunt me out, like beagles.

*{they make a noise like a dog}* Out of a desire to devour me, v. 14,15. and Psal. 22.16.

*{and go round about the City}* Watching me, that I may not escape.

**V. 7. ***{they belch out with their mouth}* Speak often of murdering me.

*{swords are in their lips}* Psalm 57. ver. 4. *whose teeth.* See there.

*{who (say they) doth hear?}* Psal. 10.11. and 13. and 94.7. Or it may be understood of men: as trusting (his enemies) to the darkness of the time, (verse before, *at evening*) and to their own subtlety, that they should not be found, or convicted: or to their power, and the licentiousness of the times, that nobody durst question them for what they did. For then also may we be said not to *hear* or *see,* when we take no notice.

**V. 8. ***{But thou, O Lord, shalt laugh at them}* You not only see, but will make their plots vain, that they may be derided.

*{laugh at them}* Psalm 2.4.

**V. 9. ***{Because of his strength will I wait upon thee}* Because Saul’s power is great, therefore I will trust in you, that are stronger. This is the best sense that can be made of those words, though divers others have been devised by expositors, all which to relate, having no better opinion of them than I have, I think would not be worth the labor. Those that think the incorruption of the Bible, which we maintain against the Papists, (though there be among them not a few, that agree with us in this particular) to consist in the maintaining of every tittle and letter that is found in the printed or written copies of these days; they both involve themselves needlessly into many difficulties, out of which to get out, they must trust more to their wit, than to any solidity of reason; and expose the Scriptures to more opposition, that I say not danger, than they are aware of. There be good Protestants, we know, that have been of another mind; if so be that Beza was a good Protestant; who though he wrote in Geneva yet I do not hear that he was ever questioned by any authority, for his many conjectures and emendations of the text of the New Testament which by many have been observed, and by some collected. Neither was Calvin so scrupulous. See before upon Psalm 42.11. *the help of my countenance, and my God.* And well we may refer unto that place, since that just such a business we have here, as was there observed, for all the world. This verse is repeated again in the last of this psalm; where we have it עזי with a *Jod; my strength;* not עזו with a *Vau.* Whereupon Mollerus; *Mihi non dubium est quin incuria librariorum pro י subrepserit ו et עזו scribi pro עזי quod σφάλμα,* &c. He would have this place corrected by that: and for the same reason it is more than probable, that those words, *The God of my mercy,* wherewith the following verse (as here divided) does begin, should belong unto this. But this we need not press, seeing that either way, whether the words be here at the end, or begin there will be good and perfect. Which cannot be said of עזי and עזו, as appears by the divers fancies of expositors. Neither is Mollerus alone of this opinion. For it comes all to one, whether we say עזו must be corrected עזי: or that עזי is put for עזו, and must be translated, *My strength,* or *O my strength,* as in the last verse, which divers do: and so both the Chaldee Paraphrase, and the Septuagint have it too. But in the very next verse, in those words we have already spoken of, thus also it is written in the text חסדו, *his mercy,* but in the margin, and pointed חסדי, and therefore translated by all, *my mercy.* Now if any shall upon this ground further insist, that whereas in this verse it is אשמרה, translated here, *I will wait:* it is in the last verse אזמרה, *I will sing:* (the difference being but of one letter there neither, in the original) and should therefore be here likewise; I deny the consequence. There is no such reason there to induce us. The sense is good and perfect, as it is: all ancient translations observe the difference: φυλάξω, here; ψαλῶ, there in the Septuagint, &c. and besides there be divers examples of such differences in other repeated verses, where no color is of suspicion, but that they were so made of purpose by the author. So here, the sense by this alteration, is not only good and warrantable; but very pregnant, and emphatical. For having first said that he would *wait;* he does now sweetly alluding by this change of one letter to his own word, proceed and say *that he will sing:* which is a further degree of confidence, as if he had already reaped the fruit of his waiting, and his business now, were to give thanks. Many such allusions there be in the Scripture, in every part of it.

*{my defence}* Heb. *my high place.*

**V. 10. ***{The God of my mercy}* Or, *my merciful God.*

*{The God of my mercy shall prevent me}* God will deliver me before I pray to him, or before I expect deliverance.

*{see my desire upon my enemies}* Psalm 54.7.

*{mine enemies}* Heb. *mine observers.*

**V. 11. ***{Slay them not}* Altogether but by little and little, that the people seeing oftentimes their judgments, may be mindful of you.

*{scatter them by thy power}* Make them vagabonds and base people.

**V. 12. ***{the sin of their mouth}* Prov. 12.13.

*{let them be taken even in their pride}* That in their misery and shame they may be as glasses and examples of God’s vengeance.

**V. 13. ***{Consume them in wrath, consume them, &c.}* There is a double seeming repugnancy or inconsistency in the words; the one in reference to the former words; the other, to those that follow. For ver. 11. he said, *Slay them not:* and now he seems to pray the contrary. And then after he has prayed, that God would *consume them that they may not be;* he presently adds, *and let them know that God,* &c. To this I first answer, that in things that are uttered with some passion (though not immoderate, but answered to the indignity of the thing: such as we allow to holy men in God’s cause: whereof see more upon Psalm 35.4. *Let them be confounded:* in the end there) and vehemency of indignation, it is against reason to require that exactness of order and coherence, that is proper and necessary to other speech. Where the thoughts are discomposed, (as in all passions; more or less) the speech may be. But secondly, the speech, in such cases, uses to be short and abrupt. Many things must be supplied; which done, there may appear good coherence in the thoughts, though not seen in the expressions. So here, first he prays that God would not suddenly *slay* or destroy them, but *bring them down;* that is, that they may have no more power to do mischief, than may serve to keep the people of God in a constant watchfulness, lest too much security do them more hurt, than the persecution of enemies. But again, if they be such (as by their present rage and cruelty a man might suspect) as cannot bow, nor be kept within certain bounds: then rather than the people of God should be at this pass, that God will quite break and consume them. Which nevertheless is not to be supposed to be done so suddenly, or so generally, that either they that perish, should not have so much time before they die, as to know what they have done: or that none at all should be left of that number, who by seeing what fearful ends such and such, and so many of their party have made, might *know,* and be forced to acknowledge, &c. So that all things well considered, there will not appear so much repugnancy, as some fancy. Yet besides this, it is certain that the *them* in these words, *let them know,* may as well be understood of other men, as of those before spoken of: it being very ordinary to the Hebrew tongue, and to the Scripture thus abruptly to pass from persons to persons, as by many is observed. So, *let them know,* will be, *let men know,* as Psalm 83.16. *that they may seek thy Name.* Lastly, some conceive, that these words here of *consuming,* and *not being;* have reference only to their power and greatness; and not to their lives. This also may be: but I should rather say, as before.

*{them}* Or, *men,* Psal. 83.18.

**V. 14. ***{And at evening let them return}* He mocks them, and sets down their punishment in the words their sin was set out by, verse 6. Before, they did run about for malice; now let them do it for hunger.

**V. 15. ***{for meat}* Heb. *to eat.*

*{and grudge if they be not satisfied}* Or, *if they be not satisfied, then they will stay all night.*

**V. 16. ***{in the morning}* In the time when they thought to have killed me, 1 Sam. 19.11.

**V. 17. ***{O my strength}* Confessing himself to be void of all strength he attributes the whole to God.

*{the God of my mercy}* See verse 10.

# PSALM LX

## TITLE

*{Shushan eduth}* That is, as the words may be interpreted, *The lilies,* (or *rose*) *of testimony,* or *of beauty.* *Shushan* might be the name of some instrument of six strings, (in Greek called *Hexachordon*) as the propriety of the word in the original does import. See before upon Psa. 45. title. But the most probable conjecture is that *Shushan eduth,* here as also Psa. 80. in the title, was the beginning of some known song. But there is no certainty, as often has been said.

*{Michtam}* Or, *a golden Psalm.*

*{to teach}* To have it learned by the Levites, as some have expounded it. But because as much might be said of every psalm, which have not this inscribed; some therefore, followed herein by the most, and best of interpreters, expound it, as if it related to the doctrine, or pointed the use of this psalm; the end whereof might be, by the commemoration of so many victories, and this in particular granted unto David by God, to teach, and convince the most obstinate and refractory, that God’s purpose was to establish him in the realm, and that he had not in vain, or falsely boasted of God’s promises. I wonder so wide a conjecture should please any besides the first author. And why might it not be the end of divers other psalms besides this, that have no such inscription, if that be a sufficient argument, as is objected against the first exposition? For my part, I will not undertake that the same may not be said of some other psalms, as well as this, though not inscribed as this: neither do I conceive that, from the omission in other psalms, to be a sufficient argument, as might be proved by divers instances: but that the purpose of this, *to teach,* here, is to tell us that the psalm was intended not for the Church, or Temple only, but for more public use, and for continuation; and to that end, to be learned by certain companies and orders, besides the ordinary Levites and quiremen; this I think by comparing with other like places, where the same words are found, may be made apparent enough. So 2 Sam. 1.17. *And David lamented with this lamentation over Saul and over Jonathan his son.* 18. *Also he bade them teach the children of Judah,* &c. which words should not, as in the English copies we find them, be included in a parenthesis, as though they had no relation to the former; which would be a great consequence of sense, and story; and very improper in this place: the meaning being this, that David out of his zeal to Jonathan, to the end, that both his memory that had been so dear unto him, and the testimonies of his good respect and affection to him might continue the longer, he took order that this lamentation of his death, so poetically by him expressed, might be taught to children and others that were taught the use of the bow; and might among other ceremonies be used in all public and solemn occasions of military exercises. There be the same words in the original, as are here: ויאמר ללמד &c. So Deut. 31.19. *Now therefore write ye this song for you, and teach it the children of Israel,* &c. there again the same למד as here; see also 2 Chr. 35.25. *And Jeremiah lamented for Josiah,* &c.

*{twelve thousand}* 2 Sam. 8.13. 1 Chr. 18.12. But those places tell us of 18000. for which we have 12000. Moreover, what is here ascribed unto Joab, 1 Chr. 18.12. seems to be ascribed unto Abishai; and those that are there, as here called Edomites, 2 Sam. 8.13. are called *Syrians.* Such seeming contradictions are frequent enough to be found in the most accurate records of ancient times, whether Greek or Latin: and are often, though at first sight diametrically contrary, happily composed to the good satisfaction of all rational and judicious, by the due observing and weighing of several circumstances. Of several circumstances, I say, to matters of this nature, incidental in such number and variety, as it is no wonder, if they that are not very circumspect and exact, do often vary in their relations, and sometimes say but the same thing when they seem most to differ. Now because we have not the histories of these wars of David at large, so that all circumstances almost are to us unknown, it is not easy, neither should it seem matter of wonder to any, if these seeming contrarieties in sacred histories cannot so easily be composed or reconciled. However, that which upon these is said by most interpreters, may be probable enough. First, for the number, that so many as 12000. might be slain in pitched battle; the rest (that is, 6000 more) afterwards in the pursuit. Or that the 6000 were slain before by Joab alone in another place; and the 12000 when Joab and Abishai had joined; or by Abishai alone; but subordinate to Joab his brother as vice-general in a pitched battle. Some have made this difference greater by 4000. *Quod autem in sacra Historia viginti duo millia numerantur;* says one in his commentary upon the psalms: a man so well versed in the text, and so diligent, as it is a wonder how he should so mistake. Perhaps he had written *duo de viginti.* Which some ignorantly took for *viginti duo:* and so caused it to be printed, because they thought it would be plainer. I had rather it were another’s mistake, than his, if his own words, *circiter dimidium numerum,* a little after, be not against it. But if his, it is but a mistake of memory, which may easily be pardoned; because of the authority of the man, I take notice of it. It may puzzle somebody perchance, as though he had found it so in the Scripture indeed. And then that the same victory should be ascribed unto *Abishai,* as the immediate instrument; and to Joab, as chief minister under David (unto whom for the same reason it is also ascribed, 2 Sam. 1.12.) is no wonder. Lastly, that the Edomites and Syrians should be confounded in this history, is no strange thing, since that they were joined together against David, as may be gathered from the very words of this inscription; where he is said to have fought *with Aram Naharaim, and with Aram Zobah,* that is, with the Aramites, or Syrians: those being parts of Syria, as Syria is taken in the largest notion. See also 2 Sam. 8.3. 1 Chron. 18.3.

**Verse 1. ***{Thou hast cast us off}* Psal. 44.9.

*{thou hast scattered us}* When they were not able to resist their enemies, the people fled hither and thither, for they could not be safe in their own houses.

*{scattered}* Heb. *broken.*

**V. 2. ***{earth}* Or, *land.*

*{thou hast broken it}* As if it were cleft with an earthquake.

**V. 3. ***{Thou hast shewed thy people hard things}* That is, Thou hast made us to feel: as Psal. 4.6. *Who will shew us any good?* so 2 Tim. 4.14. πολλὰ μοι κακὰ ἐνεδείξατο: *shewed,* properly; there translated, *did me much evil.* See also Psalm 71.20. *Thou which hast showed.*

*{to drink the wine of astonishment}* Or, *trembling;* called *the cup of trembling;* Esay 51.17, &c. It is the same word, as is here. A continual trembling, not in the act only, but habitual; is the effect oftentimes of long practiced drunkenness. *Inde pallor,* says Seneca; *& membrorum vino madentium tremor,* &c. and afterwards of habitual, *Inde incerti labantium pedes, & semper qualis in ipsa ebrietate titubatio.* He means therefore, that their evils had continued long.

**V. 14. ***{A banner to them that fear thee}* But in affording succor, and giving me victory over the enemies, you have dealt favorably with us, Psal. 20.5. & 74.4. Esay 49.22.

*{because of the truth}* Not for our merits, but to show the truth of your promise.

**V. 5. ***{That thy beloved may be delivered}* Compare with Psal. 108.6, &c. and of difference, if any, see upon Psal. 53.1. *The fool,* &c.

**V. 6. ***{God hath spoken in his holiness}* Or, *sanctuary;* by which Heaven also, sometimes so called, may be understood. Now David here makes open profession of his confidence in God’s word and promise, that he should be king over all Israel; and to that end, he mentions some places which as yet stood out, or had longest stood out against him; either as a greater argument of his faith and confidence, if as yet in opposition to him; or as a more particular acknowledgment of God’s performance, if already come in and subjected. This understood before, makes that which follows, which otherwise has a show of obscurity, plain enough, but that some proper expressions are used, which to them that are strangers to them, may well seem strange.

*{I will divide Shechem, and mete out}* That is, shall surely and entirely possess. Division and distribution unto others, either by way of gift and reward, as unto soldiers; or by way of elocation, as to tenants, presupposes entire possession. Shechem was a city belonging to the tribe of Ephraim; as Succoth to the tribe of Gad.

**V. 7. ***{Ephraim also is the strength of mine head, Judah is my Lawgiver}* In both these, he alludes to former prophecies, concerning these two tribes. It is ordinary in the Scripture, and so often in this book of Psalms, by *horns* or *horn,* to understand *strength.* Now of Ephraim and Manasses together, under the name of Joseph, because they came from him; Moses had prophesied, Deut. 33.17. *His glory is like the firstling of a bullock, and his horns are like the horns of unicorns: with them he shall push the people together, to the ends of the earth, and they are the ten thousands of Ephraim,* &c. And of Judah, Jacob, Gen. 49.10. *The scepter shall not depart from Judah, nor a Law-giver from between his feet,* &c.

**V. 8. ***{Moab is my washpot}* Under the metaphor of this *vessel of dishonour,* as the Apostle speaks, is intimated the servile and abject condition of the conquered Moabites under David. If we take it of an earthen vessel, the meaning may be, that he will break and bruise them in pieces (as indeed he did, notably) like a potter’s vessel, Psal. 2.9. And so the Chaldee Paraphrast expresses it, Psal. 13.9. But in this place, he turns it quite to another purpose, as though David intended it of a vessel filled with the blood of the slain, wherein he would wash his feet. See before upon Psal. 58.10. *he shall wash his feet in the blood of the wicked.* I like it not, I confess; nor their conceit, (which comes almost to the same effect, though they name not their author) that the whole country through the number of the slain should be as a great pan, or bowl full of blood; that this should be the *wash-pot* here intended. If the reader like it better, he may.

*{over Edom will I cast my shoe}* That is, I will trample Edom under my feet, as some expound it. Or, I will beat with my shoe, and throw it at her. The expression may seem coarse and homely, where use has not made it familiar. But such there be in all languages, which no man wonders at, when they are used to them. The Latins also did use to say, *sculponeis os batuere,* which among them was thought very contumelious and servile: *Soleâ objurgabere rubrâ,* in one of the satiric poets. The Romans had their *jus festucaria,* in trials for possessions; and *exfestucare,* is a great word, and of great importance, (which would seem to be but a strange business, by the propriety of the word) in the Gothic laws, or German code. Some, very learned, keep the vulgar Latin here, *In Idumaeam extendam calceamentum meum:* and expound it, *Idumaeo extendam calceum meum,* &c. I will reach my shoe to Edom, that he may pull it off, which is commonly done by the basest of servants, and belongs to their charge. So in the New Testament, *to bear his shoes after him,* and, *to unloose his shoe latchets,* &c. Matth. 3.11, &c. where see Grotius his Annotations.

*{Philistia, triumph thou because of him}* Spoken either ironically: You that have taken a pride to insult and triumph against me; now being conquered and subdued, triumph as much as you will, &c. Or seriously and soberly; Yield me due obedience, and acknowledge me your king by such acclamations of joy and felicity, as are ordinary among faithful subjects. In the 108.9. it is, *Over Philistia will I triumph:* which comes all to one; and of such differences in repeated, either psalms, or verses, we have spoken before, more than once.

**V. 9. ***{into the strong city}* He was assured that God would give him the strong cities of his enemies, wherein they thought themselves sure.

*{strong city}* Heb. *city of strength.*

**V. 10. ***{hast thou cast us off?}* Psal. 44.9. & 108.11.

**V. 11. ***{vain is the help of man}* Psal. 128.8,9. & 146.3.

*{help}* Heb. *salvation.*

**V. 12. ***{valiantly}* Num. 24.18.

# PSALM LXI

## TITLE

*{Neginah}* See Notes on Psal. 4. title.

**Vers. 2. ***{From the end of the earth}* Or, *land,* as the word is sometimes translated. From the place of my banishment, being remote.

*{that is higher than I}* Judea being a very mountainous and rocky country, it was ordinary for the people, in times of great and public distress, to betake themselves to rocks, for shelter and privacy. So 1 Sam. 13.6. *When the men of Israel saw,* &c. God also for that and some other reasons, is called a *Rock,* in the Scripture: as Psal. 18.2,31,46. Deut. 32.4,15,18,31. and many other places. David therefore by these words does implicitly disclaim confidence in such rocks, as ordinary men, in such extremity of distresses, would gladly repair unto. Though his case did need a rock, as much as any man’s; yet he would not have it such a rock, which he might climb or reach: (for if he might, why not another man; his enemy: and how then be inaccessible, or safe?) but such a one, as neither he, nor any mortal man, could reach; and where he might reckon himself out of all danger. Such a rock he would have, and in this his extremity, so eagerly pursued, so destitute of help, he wanted.

**V. 3. ***{For thou hast been a shelter for me}* He urges God with his former experiences, and God’s constancy, 1 Sam. 17.37.

**V. 4. ***{I will abide in thy tabernacle for ever}* That is, Though I be now far from both, yet time will come when I shall be restored both to my palace, and your sanctuary, near it; there constantly to reside, where my greatest joy and comfort is; (your tabernacle, or sanctuary) of which see before upon Psal. 27.4,8. *Seek ye my face,* &c.

*{trust}* Or, *make my refuge.*

**V. 5. ***{For thou, O God, hast heard}* There is nothing that does more strengthen our faith, than the remembrance of God’s succor in times past.

*{my vows}* Delivered me, that I might perform my vows, verse 8.

**V. 6. ***{Thou wilt prolong the Kings life}* Heb. *thou shalt add days to the days of the King.* This chiefly is to be referred to Christ, who lives for ever, and is David’s son. See before upon Psal. 21.4. *He asked,* &c.

*{as many generations}* Heb. *as generation and generation.*

**V. 7. ***{O prepare mercy}* For the stability of my kingdom stands in your mercy and truth, Psal. 25.10. & 85.10. Prov. 20.28.

# PSALM LXII

**Vers. 1. ***{Truly}* Or, *Only.* Though Satan tempted him to murmur against God, yet he bridled his affections, and resting upon God’s promise, bears his cross patiently, Psal. 73.1.

*{waiteth}* Heb. *is silent.*

**V. 2. ***{He only is my rock}* It appears by the oft repetition of this word, that the Prophet abode manifold temptations; but by resting on God, and by patience, he overcame them all.

*{defence}* Heb. *high place.*

**V. 3. ***{against a man?}* He meaning himself, being the man whom God had appointed to be king, Psal. 4.3.

*{a bowing wall shall ye be}* Esay 30.13.

*{as a tottering fence}* Though you seem to be in honor, yet God will suddenly destroy you.

**V. 4. ***{They only consult to cast him down}* It is the scope of all their consultations.

*{from his excellency}* From his high dignity, the throne, or kingdom; if then king. Or if, as most conceive, but a courtier yet, and servant under Saul; then *from his excellency,* that is, from that degree of favor at court, and that eminency of credit and reputation abroad; which through God’s special favor he enjoys. So 1 Sam. 18.30. *so that his name was much set by;* or, *precious.* To which the vulgar Latin here might have some reference; *Verumtamen pretium meum cogitaverunt repellere:* though the Latin there, be, *& celebre factum est nomen ejus nimis:* and not *preciosum.*

*{they bless with their mouth, but they curse inwardly}* Psal. 28.3. & 58.21.

*{inwardly}* Heb. *in their inward parts.*

**V. 5. ***{wait thou only upon God}* David was greatly moved with these troubles, therefore he stirs up himself to trust in God.

**V. 7. ***{and my glory}* These vehement and often repetitions were necessary, to strengthen his faith against the horrible assaults of Satan.

**V. 8. ***{pour out your heart before him}* 1 Sam. 1.15. Lam. 2.19. He admonishes us of our wicked nature, who rather hide our sorrows, and bite on the bridle, than utter our grief to God, to obtain remedy, 1 Sam. 1.15.

**V. 9. ***{They are altogether lighter than vanity}* If all men were weighed together in one balance, they would be lighter than vanity, Esay 40.15,17.

*{altogether}* Or, *alike.*

**V. 10. ***{become not vain in robbery}* Some would have David to speak here to his soldiers, to the same purpose as S. John Baptist, to soldiers, Luke 3.14. Indeed, as the words run in this translation, and if we look no further, it is very probable. But if we observe the context, the drift of the Psalmist being to set out the vanity of man, even when in greatest lustre and glory, and the power of God; or rather by making a right estimation of man, even of the greatest, to ascribe all power unto God only; v. 9. and 11. why he should in the verse between, direct, or rather divert his speech to his soldiers, I can see no probable reason; and as little, why to such in general as make a profession, (be they soldiers or others) of *oppression and robbery.* Except a man can think, that by men of *high degree,* v. 9. to whom he opposes (v. 11.) the power of Almighty God; we can think, I say, that he should intend especially (for so the context will require; if we allow of any coherence) such as trust in *oppression and robbery;* the basest of men, and most contemptible, if we take the word *robbery,* literally, of common thieves and robbers. But the words will admit of different interpretation; that is sure enough. Some translate: *Ne fidatis calumniae & rapinae; & ne evanescatis.* So Vatablus. Some: *Ne confidatis in oppressione, & praeda: ne evanescatis:* So Calvin. Others again, *Ne confidatis in rapinis, & in praeda ne evanescatis.* I omit others. This last I like best: that is, Trust not in rapines (or oppression:) become not vain in matter of booty, or prey. My opinion is, that David by these words intended, not ordinary soldiers, either of his, or of Saul’s army, such as S. Stephen spoke to, as before was alleged; much less common thieves and robbers: but greatest warriors and conquerors, to whom, as most properly (among men) honor and glory (the consequents of power) does belong; so do we find, that their glory (nay all glory, the greatest that can be among men) is sometimes set out by these or the like terms, of spoils, and booty, and prey. *Thou art more glorious and excellent, than the mountains of prey:* Psal. 76.4. See the notes there. And so we believe, that the word *robbery* should be taken, Philip. 2.6. being spoken of Christ, that being *in the form of God, he thought it no robbery to be equal with God:* that is, he made it not a matter of brag and triumph: *but,* &c. for so the coherence will be much better: as has been showed more at large in another work.

*{set not your heart upon them}* Psal. 24.4. Jer. 9.23. Ezek. 28.5. By joying overmuch, or trusting in them.

**V. 11. ***{God hath spoken once, twice have I heard this, &c.}* Heb. *God hath spoken one, two have I heard this:* that is, *one time; two times.* Or, which comes all to one, *one thing, two things:* but rather as before: and so elsewhere, one, two, once, &c. The sense is, That both by the word and express testimony of God himself, and the acknowledgment of men, (it being a common speech in all wise men’s mouths) it is most sure and certain; and undeniably, unquestionable truth, That all power, and strength, (let men take never so much upon themselves, and domineer for a while, who in very truth are no better than *vanity,* &c. ver. 9.) and dominion, does properly belong unto God alone. Such confessions and acknowledgments of God’s power, that God is all in all, &c. the writings of ancient wise heathens of all kinds, afford plentifully, if a man would collect them. And as for the word or law of God, besides the generality of it, which tends to that end, to teach man to put his confidence in God, and to depend of him in all things; there be divers particular places, more express to that purpose, to which these words of the Psalmist might have reference. The vulgar Latin has it; *Semel locutus est Deus: duo haec audivi;* that is, *God hath once spoken; these two things have I heard:* which some late interpreters of high merit and credit, both wittily (if they be the first authors of it) and probably expound (taking both the verses; this, and the 12. following, together; *Also unto thee, O Lord, belongeth mercy,* &c. to this purpose: God has once spoken in Sinai, or from Mount Sinai, (when he delivered the Ten Commandments) and from what he then spoke, I have learned these two things; 1. That God is most powerful, to avenge himself of his enemies: and 2. most merciful, to requite them that serve him, and put their trust in him. *For I the Lord am a jealous God,* &c. *And showing mercy,* &c. Exod. 20.5,6. This is plausible; yet if any man like better to understand it of God’s word in general, confirmed unto David by many prophets and prophecies, I am not against it. As for the words and manner of expression here used, that in Job comes nearest; *Once have I spoken, but I will not answer: yea twice, but I will proceed no further,* Job 40.5. This power of God the Psalmist does here the more emphatically set out, because it was the only comfort and support he had in all his adversities and greatest extremities, that though for a while it might seem otherwise, whilst the wicked and ungodly had the better of it, &c. Yet he knew there was a just and righteous God in Heaven, who in due time would reward all men according to their deeds. See before upon Psal. 9.6. *O thou enemy,* &c.

*{power}* Or, *strength.*

**V. 12. ***{for thou renderest to every man according to his work}* Job 34.11. Prov. 24.12. Jer. 32.19. Ezek. 7.27. Matth. 16.27. Rom. 2.6. 2 Cor. 5.10. Eph. 6.8. Col. 3.25. 1 Pet. 1.17. Rev. 22.12. For the wicked feel your power, and the godly your mercy.

# PSALM LXIII

## TITLE

*{when he was in the wilderness of Judah}* 1 Sam. 22.5.

**Vers. 1. ***{My soul thirsteth for thee}* Psal. 42.2.

*{my flesh longeth}* Psal. 84.2.

*{thirsty}* Heb. *weary.*

*{thirsty land}* Though he was both hungry, and in great distress, yet he made God his sufficiency, and above all meat and drink.

*{where no water is}* Heb. *without water.*

**V. 2. ***{To see thy power}* In this misery I long more for the pleasures of the sanctuary, than for bodily relief. See before upon Psa. 27.8. *Seek ye my face,* &c.

*{see thy power and thy glory}* It is well observed by some here, that David by these words does allude (if not intend it, directly) unto the Ark, so styled, *his glory,* 1 Sam. 4.21. *his strength,* 1 Chron. 16.11. Psal. 132.8. *his strength and his glory,* Psal. 78.61. That was the place (the Ark) of God’s presence and wisdom, among men; and from whence (whilst it stood) he was conceived to grant requests especially; see upon Psal. 14.7. *out of Sion.*

**V. 3. ***{thy lovingkindness is better than life}* Psal. 30.5.

**V. 4. ***{Thus will I bless thee}* When I am restored to the sanctuary, I will praise you as long as I live.

**V. 5. ***{marrow}* Heb. *fatness.*

*{and fatness}* The remembrance of your favor is more sweet unto me, than all the pleasures and dainties of the world, Psal. 4.7.

**V. 6. ***{When}* Heb. *If,* Amos 7.2.

**V. 7. ***{Because thou hast been my help}* See upon Psal. 22.21. *For thou hast heard me from the horns of the Unicorn.*

**V. 8. ***{My soul followeth}* Heb. *cleaveth;* Sticks so close to you in piety and confidence, that no trouble can remove me.

**V. 9. ***{shall go into the lower parts of the earth}* They shall be slain, and made fit for burial, though they shall not have the honor to be buried, but be left on the earth for foxes to devour, ver. 10. In the Hebrew, the words (here translated, *the lower parts of the earth*) are, תחתיות הארץ, by which some understand *hell,* or eternal death. But it appears by comparing with the like, Ezek. 31.14. & 18. (תחתית ארץ there; in both places: translated, *the nether parts of the earth:* all the difference being that the word is singular here in Ezekiel: and plural, in David) that the grave only (not a formal grave, but any place or part of the earth, that receives the carcasses of the dead, and slain) is meant; that is, with relation to the body, and not to the soul. For *they are delivered unto death, to the nether parts of the earth,* &c. And so Psalm 86.13. משאול תחתיה, there translated, *the lowest hell,* or, *grave;* is meant, present, certain death: and by death, any great extremity of affliction metaphorically intimated. However, these words, תחתיות הארץ are used in another sense, Psa. 139.15. the womb there by *the lowest parts of the earth* being intended. Which sense nevertheless, has relation unto this former, and is grounded upon it, because the womb is to the body therein enclosed, as it were a grave: as by many it has been styled. And to this signification, or notion of the words the Apostle is thought by some to allude, Eph. 4.9. speaking there of Christ his incarnation; though the more received interpretation is, that by *the earth,* the world in general (as it is sometimes) to be understood: and so Christ said to have descended *into the lower parts of the earth,* when he came into the world, as man.

**V. 10. ***{They shall fall by the sword}* Heb. *they shall make him run out like water by the hands of the sword.*

**V. 11. ***{every one that sweareth by him}* That worships God aright, Esay 65.16. Jer. 4.1,2. Deut. 6.12,13. See more upon the next words, *the mouth of them,* &c.

*{shall glory}* Hebrew יתהלל that is, shall have occasion to glory, and for the occasion of their glorying, to praise and glorify God. He means, all things shall go well with them, according to their hearts’ desire.

*{the mouth of them that speak lies}* As swearing, such as is here understood, deliberate and conscientious swearing, upon weighty occasions, to bear witness to the truth, &c. is taken for the whole worship of God, because it presupposes a belief, first, of a God; and secondly, of a God that is privy to all the actions and intentions of men; which two things are the very foundation of all religion: so, *to speak lies,* here opposed to *swearing,* may as properly imply absolute ungodliness and infidelity, because it is to be opposed, that he that makes a practice of lying, which is a sin more immediately against God, because in his sight only; has said in his heart, that there is no God: and upon this ground, as he makes no conscience to tell an untruth with all security; so he may as freely indulge himself a liberty of sinning in any other kind, when he sees his opportunity.

*{the mouth of them that speak lies, shall be stopped}* That is, They who did lately boast of their hearts’ desire, shall now be silent, being full of shame and confusion, now that all things are fallen to them to the contrary. In brief, his meaning is, that there shall be an alteration, and that the prosperity of the wicked shall not continue for ever.

# PSALM LXIV

**Vers. 1. ***{Hear my voice}* Prayer may be either mental; as that of Moses, Exod. 14.15. *Wherefore cryest thou?* according to the exposition of all, or most ancient fathers: or vocal, such as this of David’s, probably; if we take the word *voice,* properly. But that need not. For *the voice of prayer,* may be taken for *prayer,* barely: as the voice of words, for words; as Deut. 1.34. *And the Lord heard the voice of their words,* &c. Yet it is possible there may be somewhat more than ordinary in that word both here in David’s words, and in that place out of Deuteronomy: intimating that in both, there was somewhat very penetrative, and likely to move God, whether anger, (as in Deuteronomy) or compassion, as in David’s words. So the blood of Abel is said to have a voice, Gen. 4.10. *the voice of thy brother’s blood.* Which I note the rather, because the Hebrew word, here translated *prayer,* has also the signification of *meditation:* so that the words may as truly be translated, *in my meditation:* and *voice* so taken, as we have showed, may as properly belong to *meditation,* as to loud prayer. He might also say, *the voice of my meditation,* to intimate, that thoughts and meditations of the heart are as easily heard by God, as words are by men that are uttered with a loud voice.

*{from fear of the enemy}* That I may not fear the enemy.

**V. 2. ***{from the secret counsel}* That is, from their secret malice.

*{from the insurrection}* To wit, their outward violence.

**V. 3. ***{Who whet their tongue}* Psal. 11.2.

*{bend their bows}* Psal. 58.7. See there.

*{even bitter words}* False reports and slanders.

**V. 4. ***{shoot in secret at the perfect}* Psal. 11.2.

*{and fear not}* Without any fear of God, or reverence of man.

**V. 5. ***{They encourage themselves in an evil matter}* The more that the wicked see God’s children in misery, the more bold and impudent are they in oppressing them: a great aggravation of their wickedness and cruelty. See upon Prov. 24.17. *Rejoice not,* &c. If there be danger in too much rejoicing at the fall of a wicked enemy; what may they expect at the hands of God one day, who the more they prosper in their unjust oppression, the more does their rage and malice against the oppressed, increase?

*{matter}* Or, *speech.*

*{commune of laying snares privily}* Prov. 1.11.

*{laying snares}* Heb. *to hide snares.*

*{who shall see them}* See upon Psal. 59.7. *For who, (say they,) doth hear?*

**V. 6. ***{They search out iniquities}* This is ambiguously translated. For God is said properly to search out iniquities, when he severely punishes, and calls a man to an account for old unrepented sins and iniquities: as Job 10.6. *Thou inquirest after mine iniquity, and searchest after my sin.* The meaning here is, They devise, by long search and study, mischievous things: they plot mischief. It might have been therefore more clearly translated, *They search out evils:* or, *They search for iniquities.*

*{they accomplish a diligent search}* Or, *we are consumed by that which they have thoroughly searched.* The word here translated in the text, *They accomplish,* is תמנו which being found in three or four places besides this, (as Numb. 17.13. Lam. 3.22. and elsewhere) is always translated in the first person plural, *we consume,* or, *are consumed.* The word indeed is of an irregular form; which made some Rabbins to take the liberty to make a third person plural of it in this place. But since we find it nowhere else so, it is safest to keep to that which we find. Neither will the sense be worse so; as it is expressed in the margin. But learned Grotius, very acutely, thus orders and supplies the whole verse, with that which immediately follows; *Investigarunt illi iniquitates,* &c. That is, *They have sought out iniquities;* (that is, says he, ways how to annoy us) *We have been consumed. But the most perfect search of all,* (is God’s; as he would have it supplied, in reference to the next verse) *which* (reaches, to wit) *to the inmost part of man, and to the very bottom of the heart. Therefore God hath shooted at them with an arrow; suddenly were they wounded.*

*{a diligent search}* Heb. *a search searched.*

*{both the inward thoughts of every one of them, and the heart is deep}* There is no way so secret and so subtle to do hurt, which they invented not for his destruction.

**V. 7. ***{shall they be wounded}* Heb. *their wound shall be.*

**V. 8. ***{So shall they make their own tongue to fall upon themselves}* That is, Those mischiefs which their hearts had devised, and their tongues with great insolency and confidence, had uttered and threatened against others. Or, as Psal. 52.2. *Their tongue deviseth mischief: like a sharp razor, working deceitfully.* And Prov. 12.13. *The wicked is snared by the transgression of his lips,* &c. It is ordinary with the Scripture, to ascribe unto the lips, mouth, or tongue; those things that belong unto the heart properly, because of their near dependency.

*{all that see them shall flee away}* יתנודדו which implies, that in their flight they shall wander through their astonishment and grief of mind. It must therefore be understood of the wicked, their favorers and abetters. So that here is a double aggravation of their misery, from the astonishment of their friends, and their own forlorn condition at that time, when those, who before did encourage and countenance, shall not be in case to relieve them, nor so much as abide with them, to comfort and to condole.

**V. 9. ***{and shall declare the work of God}* See before upon Psal. 28.4. *Because they regard not the works of the Lord,* &c.

*{for they shall wisely consider}* See before upon Psal. 41.1. *Blessed is he that considereth the poor.* To which add Mal. 3.18. *Then shall ye return and discern between the righteous and the wicked; between him that serveth God, and him that serveth him not.*

**V. 10. ***{The righteous shall be glad in the Lord}* Psal. 32.11. To see God’s heavy judgments against them, and how he has caught them in their own snares.

*{and shall trust in him}* When they shall consider that he will be favorable to them, as he was to his servant David.

# PSALM LXV

## TITLE

*{A Psalm and Song}* See the Notes on the title of Psal. 30.

**Vers. 1. ***{Praise waiteth for thee, O God, in Sion}* Heb. לך דומיה, *is silent unto thee:* or more properly, *silentium tibi: is silence unto thee.* For it is properly a noun substantive; and according to the propriety of the word, some expound it, The silence of others, (in other parts of the world, where you are not known) is in Sion your praise; that is, You are daily praised and glorified in your Church by the godly, for your manifold mercies, both spiritual and temporal, which others take no notice of, and bury in an unthankful silence, or oblivion. Others, *Tibi silentium laus est;* that is, say they, Your praises are so many, so incomprehensible, so inexpressible, that silence (or silent admiration) does become us, and them, better than words. Or, as some others, ἀναντίρρητος, ἀναποφώνητος, that is, which no man can speak against you, or deny; not to be contradicted by any, and consequently by all men acknowledged. See upon, *God hath spoken once, twice have I heard this,* &c. Psal. 62.11. Lastly, *Tibi silentium, laus: id est, sileant alia omnia, ubi de te laudando agitur: Favete linguis.* So the learned Grotius: That is, says he, Let all other things be silent, when you are to be praised. Let all men hold their peace, and be attentive. The words of the Prophet Habakkuk come very near; *The Lord is in his holy Temple: let all the earth keep silence before him,* Hab. 2.20. These indeed keep best to the propriety of the word; but they go somewhat far for the sense. Others therefore taking דומיה for דומה; that is, for a present particle, translate πρέπει, *convenit; belongs unto thee:* or as it is here, *quiescit, expectat; manet; waiteth.* And so we had before already, Psal. 62.1. *Truly my soul waiteth upon God:* it is the same word as is here. Now for the argument of the Psalm, it is conceived by many, that it was intended for a form of public thanksgiving, for a gracious welcome plenty, after a long dearth and famine; such as we read of 2 Sam. 21.1. & 14.

**V. 2. ***{unto thee shall all flesh come}* This is not literally meant of that coming, or gathering of the Gentiles unto God, by the preaching of the Gospel: (though mystically applicable to that too) but of the prayers and addresses to God, of all men of all nations, in their extraordinary needs and necessities; it being the propriety of pressing dangers and extremities, (as by many has been observed) to bring them to a thought of God, who otherwise are most godless and profane. See Psal. 107. where this is set out at large, and proved, or illustrated by many instances. Neither must the words be precisely taken of that right, either legal, by certain rites, oblations and sacrifices; (as then:) or evangelical, in Christ only, (Eph. 3.12. & Joh. 14.6. *No man cometh unto the Father, but by me*) but of a more general coming to God, when men in their distresses, having an apprehension of a deity, both merciful and powerful, apply themselves to it, such a way as they think best and most acceptable, for help and relief. So Saint Paul, who elsewhere sets out all the Gentiles as *godless,* because they had not the knowledge of the true God; in a more general sense, witnesses of the Athenians, (the greatest idolaters of the world) that they did, though *ignorantly,* (alluding to their inscription, *To the unknown God*) worship that God which he preached unto them, Acts 17.23.

**V. 3. ***{Iniquities}* Heb. *words, or, matters of iniquities.*

*{Iniquities prevail against me, &c.}* Rather, if the argument of the Psalm be not mistaken (of which see before) in the preterit, *Iniquities had prevailed,* &c. and, *thou dost purge,* or, *hast purged;* as Junius and some others. Except we should make the argument to be more general, that the Psalmist purposing to set out the goodness of God towards mankind in general, in affording them the fruits of the earth so plentifully, he does first begin with his more special love and favor to his Church, where having made himself known more particularly by his word, he has ordained the means of eternal salvation, by forgiveness of sins, &c. the choicest and chiefest of God’s blessings. See before upon Psal. 24.1. *The earth is the Lord’s,* &c.

**V. 4. ***{thou chosest}* Psal. 36.8.

*{satisfied with the goodness of thy house}* Psal. 160.5.

**V. 5. ***{By terrible things in righteousness wilt thou answer us}* By *terrible things* here, (נוראות in the original) most interpreters understand great and gracious deliverances of the Church, wherein the power and goodness of God, in its extreme perils and dangers, is most conspicuous. But how this agrees with that which follows, *who art the confidence of all the ends of the earth,* &c. does not appear. Except we should say with some, that therefore, *the ends of the earth,* &c. that is, of all men in general, because such great and miraculous deliverances which God affords unto his Church at such times, and upon such occasions, are a means to bring others to the knowledge of God, and of the increase of the Church all the world over. But this (though elsewhere, Psal. 67.2. *That thy way may be known,* &c. we have somewhat to this purpose) is here rather witty, than probable: especially, when there is no need at all of going so far. By *terrible things* then *in righteousness,* (taking *righteousness* here for mercy and goodness, as often already has been observed, and is here again noted by interpreters) we understand that goodness of God, in general, and those wonderful works of his, extending to all, as is here showed; and for which the Psalmist does so earnestly exhort all men of the world to praise and glorify God, Psal. 107. *O that men would praise the Lord for his goodness, and for his wonderful works to the children of men,* ver. 8,15,21,31. נוראות and נפלאות are words of great affinity, and sometimes put together; as Psalm 106.22. *Wondrous works in the land of Ham; and terrible things by the Red-sea.* This will be the best and most natural coherence certainly. And, *salvation,* of temporal deliverance, is ordinary enough. But if it be said, as it is by others, that at these words, *O God of our salvation:* the Psalmist ends what he intended to say of God’s goodness to his Church in particular; and so passes to the consideration, or commemoration of his general goodness towards all; I think this also may be said with probability enough; and therefore I leave it to the choice of the reader. But if so, if not a new verse, yet a full point might have done well; *Thou art the confidence,* &c.

*{and of them that are afar off upon the Sea}* Some supply the words, *& insularum maris distantium;* that is, *and of the Islands of the Sea, that are afar off.* Junius without any supplement, *fiducia omnium extremitatum terrae & maris longinquarum;* that is, *Thou art the hope of all the ends, both of the Land and Sea, afar off.* According to this translation, *and of them,* &c. the words might be interpreted by Psal. 107.23,24, &c. *They that go down to the Sea in ships,* &c. *These see the works of the Lord, and his wonders in the deep.* But it may do well, if both be understood; both they that are upon the seas; which may fit the literal meaning better perchance; and the isles of the sea, or inhabitants of far islands, which is more suitable to the mystical sense, of the conversion of isles to the Christian faith: according to that of Esay; *He shall not fail, nor be discouraged; till he have set judgment in the earth; and the Isles shall wait for his Law,* Esay 42.4.

**V. 6. ***{girded with power}* Psal. 93.1.

**V. 7. ***{Which stilleth the noise of the Seas}* Psal. 107.29. *He maketh the storms,* &c. and of Christ, Matth. 8.26. Of the consideration of nature, and of God’s power and providence over all created things; to what end often insisted upon by David, see upon Psal. 33.6. *By the word of the Lord,* &c.

*{the tumult of the people}* Esay 17.12,13. *Wo to the multitude of many people,* &c.

**V. 8. ***{thou makest the outgoings of the morning and evening to rejoice}* This expression, according to the subject, being altogether poetical, the sense is the more obscure; and of divers different expositions, it is hard to resolve which is the truest, because all, within compass of the liberty of a poetical expression; and of many, not any, that we can deem proper, or literal. Some would have him to say by these words, that God by his universal goodness gives the whole world occasion of rejoicing, even from East to West; that is, from one end of the world to the other: to which purpose he says elsewhere, that the whole earth is full of his goodness. Others, by the *out-goings of the morning,* understand men going with the morning light to labor, according to their several employments: and by the *out-goings of the evening,* wild beasts, that wait for the darkness, and then come abroad for prey: Which they, very probably, ground upon the words of the Psalmist, Psal. 104.20,21, &c. *Thou makest darkness, and it was night; wherein all the beasts of the earth do creep forth: The young Lions,* &c. *The Sun ariseth,* &c. *Man goeth forth unto his work, and to his labour until the evening.* Here plainly we have the *out-goings,* both of *morning,* and *evening.* These I take to be the chiefest expositions. If neither of these satisfy, I doubt Junius his interpretation will less; yet for the authority of the man, I will not omit it. He translates the whole verse thus; *Qui facis ut canant exortus matutinos & vespertinos incolae extremitatum reverentes à signis tuis.* It were not easy to say what he meant by this translation, did not he explain it in his notes: and yet not easy neither, to apply the note to the translation; *Qui totos dies à principio ad finem consumis in docendis hominibus laudem tuam;* So he explains it: let the reader judge. After all these, there is yet another interpretation, not less probable than any; which expounds these *out-goings,* of the vicissitude of day and night, and that admirable equipage of Sun and Moon with other stars, that belongs unto them: wherein God’s great power and providence to men is seen as much as in anything: as if he would say, You give men occasion to praise and glorify you, when they see these admirable works of yours, and find the benefit and comfort of both; both of day and night. Which is made the more probable, by comparing with Jeremy 31.35. *Thus saith the Lord, which giveth the Sun for a light by day, and the ordinances of the Moon and of the Stars for a light by night: which divideth the Sea, when the waves thereof roar,* (*qui disrumpit mare, & fremunt fluctus ejus,* Junius) *the Lord of hosts is his Name.* Here we see the Sea also is mentioned, as in the former verse: which, as we said, makes the matter so much the more probable. But why might not, which would make the expression more poetical, and yet the sense more literal, the Morning, and the Evening themselves, be said to rejoice? Of the Morning, or morning Sun, either of his *out-going;* or *rejoicing,* there would be little question, seeing we have both Psal. 19.5. *which is as a bridegroom coming out of his chamber; and rejoiceth as a strong man to run a race. His going forth,* &c. But then of the Evening, how shall that be said *to go out?* Yes, very well, according to poets, who use to describe the coming of the Evening, as a thing that had no dependence from the going down of the Sun: but had its proper *out-going,* as well as the Morning. *Jam vesper fusco roscidus exit equo,* Ovid for one, as I take it. But lastly, what hinders but that the words might be translated, *thou makest the out-goings of the Morning, and the Evening* (*vesperam,* not *vespera*) *to rejoice.* After all this, we may not omit a passage of the Prophet Micah, where this expression is used concerning Christ: somewhat may be made of it in confirmation of some of the interpretations that have been insisted upon. His words are: *But thou, Bethlehem Ephrata, though thou be little among the many thousands of Judah, yet out of thee shall he come forth unto me, that is to be ruler in Israel, whose goings forth* (it is the same word in the original, as is here,) *have been from of old, from everlasting,* (or, *the days of evermore.*)

*{to rejoice}* Or, *to sing.*

**V. 9. ***{Thou visitest the earth}* To wit, with rain.

*{waterest it}* Or, *after thou hast made it to desire rain.*

*{with the river of God which is full of water}* Most interpreters would have these words to be intended of the Holy Land particularly, and think that allusion is made to Deut. 11.10,11. where the promised land is said to be watered, not as Egypt, with multiplicity of rivers, whether natural, or artificial; which in Hebrew are properly called פלגים: which is the word here also used; but *with the rain of heaven;* that here therefore the rain of heaven is called *a river,* because it supplies the use of a river; but, *the river of God,* as immediately coming from Heaven, and of a different origin from ordinary rivers. Some again apply the words, to the river Jordan, or Siloe. But I see no necessity of restraining the words to the Holy Land. The rain may be called *the river of God,* with allusion to ordinary rivers, without any particular reference to those of Egypt, or to that place that speaks of them. The coherence as I conceive, will be much better so. The Psalmist therefore does here set out the benefit of the rain, which is as it were the great river of God, full of water, and sufficient to water all the earth;

*{thou preparest them corn, when thou hast so provided for it}* The meaning is, that when God by rain and otherwise, has fitted, or prepared (which is the word of the original) the earth, then is the earth in case to bring forth, corn for the use of men. That this is the meaning, is not doubted by any: and the Apostle says in effect as much, 1 Cor. 3.6. *I have planted, Apollo watered, but God gave the increase:* and Chap. 15. of the same Epistle, verses 35,36. *And that which thou sowest,* &c. but if the words were translated, as they may as well, *It* (that is, the earth) *prepareth them corn, when* (or, *because*) *thou hast so prepared it:* they would run with more acuteness and concinnity, and less savor of tautology. The third person future feminine, and the second masculine in the Hebrew, are all one. So תשלג is doubtfully translated, by some in the third, by others in the second person, Psal. 68.14. See there, upon, *it was white as snow in Salmon.*

**V. 10. ***{Thou waterest the ridges thereof abundantly}* By this description he shows, that all the order of nature is a testimony of God’s love towards us, who causes all creatures to serve our necessity.

*{thou settlest the furrows thereof}* Or, *thou causest rain to descend into the furrows thereof.*

*{thou makest it soft}* Heb. *thou dissolvest it.*

**V. 11. ***{the year with thy goodness}* Heb. *the year of thy goodness.*

*{thy paths drop fatness}* The clouds, where your chariots go, Psal. 104.3.

**V. 12. ***{upon the pastures of the wilderness}* That there may be food for beasts.

*{the little hills rejoice}* Are filled with grass, and have cause to rejoice, if they could; and give cause to man to praise God with heart and tongue, Psal. 147.8.

*{rejoice}* Heb. *are girded with joy.*

# PSALM LXVI

## TITLE

*{A Song or Psalm}* See the Notes on the title of Psal. 48.

**Vers. 1. ***{All ye lands}* Heb. *all the earth.* He prophesies that all nations shall come to the knowledge of God, who then was only known in Judea.

**V. 3. ***{submit}* Or, *yield feigned obedience,* Psal. 18.44. Heb. *lie. The strangers shall submit.* See there. To which may be added, Zeph. 3.13. *The remnant of Israel shall not do iniquity, nor speak lies: Neither shall a deceitful tongue be found in their mouths: for they shall feed, and lie down, and none shall make them afraid.* Here plainly, *speaking of lies,* and *a deceitful* (or *flattering:* See Psal. 12.2. where they are joined: as elsewhere) *tongue;* opposed to perfect, or secure liberty. The ground of which opposition, some of the best expositors not understanding, would have this *for lies,* &c. either to stand for nothing; or give such reasons of the coherence, as have not much probability. See also Esay 57.11. *And of whom hast thou been afraid, or feared, that thou hast lied,* &c. To *lie* therefore, I say, in such places, does import such low submission properly, as does often, through fear, put men upon a necessity (to save themselves) to tell a lie: or not to dare to speak the truth freely: so, properly; but sometimes, *submission* in general, whether through fear, or otherwise. Such metonymies are very frequent in all languages; and the not understanding of them breeds great obscurity sometimes, where the matter otherwise of itself is plain enough. So *to kiss,* Psalm 2.12. for to submit: as is there noted. And a *deceitful tongue,* or *hand,* in the Proverbs, often; for an idle tongue, or hand: as all interpreters agree: and so translated too, commonly; not, *deceitful,* (as the word original, properly) but *idle,* Proverbs 10.4. and 12.27. and again, 19.15. upon this ground, because idleness is oftentimes the occasion of guile and deceitfulness: from whence nevertheless we may not absolutely conclude, that every one that is deceitful, is idle; or every one that is idle, deceitful: it is not without example, I hope, that a man should be idle, and slothful in his business; and yet upright and honest in his dealings; no more than before (for it is the same reason:) that every one that is said in this kind to lie, that is, to submit; should be understood feignedly to submit, through fear.

**V. 5. ***{Come and see the works of God}* Psal. 46.8. *Come behold,* &c. Men are apt enough to talk, and inquire by way of curiosity, of all strange chances and alterations that happen among them: but to consider of them wisely, as the operations of God’s hand, that God may be glorified, and themselves upon a strict and unpartial search of what is amiss in themselves amended: (the best and safest use that we can make of God’s judgments upon others: Luke 13.3. *but except ye repent,* &c.) is not the care of many: but though not of many, must be of all, that are truly godly. See upon Psalm 28.5. *Because they regard not,* &c. towards the end.

*{he is terrible}* All fear of God, is grounded chiefly upon this his mercy, or his terror. There is great need of both, according to the several tempers and dispositions of men, to maintain religion among men: David therefore does often inculcate both: Atheists, as carefully and peremptorily deny both. Of both these, and upon what grounds denied by Atheists and Epicures, see upon Psalm 90.11. *even according to thy fear,* &c. and Psalm 130.4. *For there is forgiveness* (in some translations, *mercies:*) *with thee, that thou mayest be feared.*

*{toward the children of men}* His providence is wonderful in maintaining their state, Psal. 111.2,3.

**V. 6. ***{He turned the sea into dry land}* By giving a passage through the Red-sea, he finished our deliverance out of Egypt; and by making a way through Jordan, he gave us an entrance into Canaan, Exod. 14.21. Josh. 3.14.

**V. 7. ***{his eyes behold the nations}* Psal. 11.4. *His eye-lids,* &c. and 33.13. *The Lord looketh.* See there.

**V. 9. ***{Which holdeth our soul in life}* He signifies a special benefit of God, in delivering them from dangers mentioned in the verses following.

*{holdeth}* Heb. *putteth.*

**V. 10. ***{silver is tried}* Esay 48.10. Job 23.10.

**V. 11. ***{Thou broughtest us into the net}* God’s people may be brought into troubles, made subject unto tyrants, (whether one, or many at once:) and enter into manifold dangers, as beasts are taken, loaded, ridden on.

**V. 12. ***{Thou hast caused men to ride over our heads}* That is, You have set men over our heads, or over us, who by their cruel and insolent government, did as it were ride our bodies. See Esay 51.23. *which have said to thy soul, Bow down,* &c.

*{wealthy}* Heb. *moist.*

**V. 13. ***{I will go into thy house}* Though truest thankfulness; nay, that only truly, which is from the heart, and brings forth fruits of holiness and righteousness in our lives and conversations: Yet formal solemn thanks, as going to God’s house, and the like, must not be omitted. We must approve our thankfulness not unto God only, (though unto him especially) but unto men also, that God may be glorified, and others edified by our example. This has been the practice of all the godly in all ages, as well Christians, as Jews. See also upon Psal. 100.4. *Enter into his gates.*

**V. 14. ***{uttered}* Heb. *opened.*

**V. 15. ***{fatlings}* Heb. *marrow.*

**V. 16. ***{Come and hear, all ye that fear God}* It is not enough to have received God’s benefits, and to be mindful thereof, but also we are bound to make others profit thereby and praise God, Psal. 40.9,10.

**V. 17. ***{and he was extolled with my tongue}* Heb. *under my tongue:* by which some understand the heart, as if he meant, that being confident God would hear his cry, he began to meditate and frame within himself the praises of God, by way of thanksgiving. Others supplying *sermonibus,* translate it, *& fui elatus pro sermonibus linguae meae;* that is, *and I was exalted* (that is, delivered;) *according to the words of my tongue.* This I should like better, if the word in the original רומם, (properly, *exaltatus est,* in the third person: except we make a participle of it,) will bear it. But *extolled with my tongue,* is more generally received; and in my judgment, to be preferred. There is no such incongruity in the matter, if words be said to proceed from under the tongue, that are framed with the manifold reflections and complications of the tongue. And so *poison,* elsewhere, is said to be *under* the tongue, which is thought to proceed from the tongue itself. Now God is very truly and really exalted by us, when we call upon him in time of distress. This is the greatest honor (if we may so speak) that we can do unto him: (Psal. 50.15. *And call upon me in the day of trouble,* &c.) which they that bereave him of, calling upon saints or angels in such cases, have much to answer for.

**V. 18. ***{If I regard iniquity in mine heart}* If I delight in wickedness, God will not hear me, Joh. 9.31. *Now we know,* &c. and Psal. 50. last words.

# PSALM LXVII

## TITLE

*{Neginoth}* See Notes on the title of Psal. 4.

*{A Psalm or Song}* See Notes on the title of Psal. 30.

**Vers. 1. ***{Cause his face to shine upon us}* Psal. 4.6. *the light of thy countenance.* See more there. All these forms and expressions (which may be added) being taken from that form of solemn blessing prescribed by God himself, Numbers 6.24,25,26. *The Lord bless thee, and keep thee,* &c.
