That form being of God’s own devising, (as we may say) holy men did not think they could please God better, than in using his own words, borrowed from that form, so far as was agreeable to their occasions; it being intended only for a form of blessing: as of all prayers, we have great reason to think the Lord’s Prayer, as we commonly call it, to be the most acceptable prayer unto God, that we can use: and though we may use others to that, as our several occasions may require; yet to think better of any other of our own devising, than of that: or so to be in love of our own forms, whether set, or sudden, as to omit that in our devotions, whether public, or private; cannot be less than blasphemy, and sacrilege, in a high degree. And then to say, that it was not intended by Christ for a form of prayer, to be used *verbatim,* as it is set down; but I know not for what; is so ridiculous, and contradictory to the text itself, and to the practice of all Christians of ancient times; that we may as well allow of Papists to say, That when Christ enjoined, *Drink ye all of this:* he meant the priests only, and take it for a sufficient excuse of their abominable sacrilege, in bereaving the people of the cup, in the administration of the sacrament. But to return to that form prescribed Numb. 6.24, &c. most of the phrases there, tend to this, (well worthy our observation) to teach us, that the happiness of man does consist in being admitted to the presence of God, and his beatifical vision; the fruits whereof reaping even in this life, (where we see but as through a glass) temporal blessings, and spiritual; what may we expect, when we shall be admitted to behold him *face to face?*

*{upon us}* Heb. *with us.*

**V. 2. ***{That thy way may be known upon earth}* That the Gentiles may be converted unto God, Tit. 2.11. Luke 2.32. See before upon Psal. 65.5. *By terrible things,* &c. and upon Psal. 94.15. *But judgment shall return unto righteousness,* &c.

**V. 4. ***{O let the nations be glad}* By these oft repetitions he shows, that the people can never rejoice sufficiently, and give thanks for the great benefits that they shall receive under the kingdom of Christ.

*{righteously}* Psal. 96.13.

*{and govern the nations upon earth}* Heb. *lead gently,* as a shepherd leads his sheep.

*{govern}* Heb. *lead.*

**V. 6. ***{Then shall the earth yield her increase}* This Psalm, as is generally granted, containing a prophecy concerning Christ, and his kingdom, and the happiness of men under it; there is no question but the blessings here spoken of, though set out under the type of things temporal, according to the manner of the prophets, and the condition of those times, are to be understood spiritually, chiefly. For we would not altogether exclude the literal sense, because the Apostle says, that *Godliness hath the promises,* &c. but spiritually, chiefly. The *increase of the earth* is also mentioned by Ezek. (34.27.) and Zachar. (8.12.) upon this occasion. Now by the *earth,* some understand spiritually, the hearts and minds of men, cultivated by the knowledge of Christ, and his Gospel, and by the spirit of regeneration, to bring forth fruits plentifully, Luke 8.8,15. 2 Cor. 9.8. Heb. 6.7,8. Their interpretation therefore is not altogether improbable, who understand those words, Psal. 85.11. *Truth shall spring out of the earth.* To the same effect as these here of the *increase of the earth:* as if he had said, The earth shall be fruitful in yielding true increase. *Truth* indeed, taken in the ordinary sense, and other graces with it, to be intended, spiritually, there too: but the type, to be the same.

**V. 7. ***{all the ends of the earth shall fear him}* When they feel his great benefits, both spiritual and corporal toward them, then they shall serve God aright.

# PSALM LXVIII

## TITLE

*{A Psalm or Song}* See Notes on the title of Psal. 30.

**Vers. 1. ***{Let God arise, let his enemies be scattered: let them also, &c.}* Num. 10.35. See before upon Psalm 47.5. *God is gone up with a shout.* Most interpreters agree, that the occasion of this Psalm, was that solemn fetching of the Ark to Jerusalem, and placing of it in the tabernacle David had prepared for it: whereof we read, 2 Sam. 6.12, &c. Some, because many things here have reference, or at least, are applicable to the coming out of Egypt, and what then happened, they make that to be the argument. But I do not conceive that to be a sufficient reason. For the coming out of Egypt, was a subject to the Jews of a very large capacity in that kind, and which upon all occasions, of public praises and thanksgivings especially, might come in very well. Besides, the Ark, we know, was not only a consequent of their egress, in regard of the time: but in all their progress, their constant *palladium* and security; so that in that respect, their coming out of Egypt was no heterogeneous or impertinent, but very fit and proper subject, upon such an occasion. As for them that would have no other literal meaning throughout the whole Psalm, than Christ, because applicable to him; or, as they urge, applied to him by the Apostle; though that be true of a good part of it, yet we deny the necessity of the consequence. See before upon Psal. 40.6. *Mine ears hast thou opened;* and Psal. 45. upon the title of it.

*{before him}* Heb. *from his face.*

**V. 3. ***{but let the righteous be glad}* All those particulars concerning the wicked and ungodly from the beginning of the Psalm, (as these also, that concern the righteous in this verse) might have been translated in the form of prophetical denunciations as well, as in the form of imprecations: which though frequent in the Psalms, (of which see upon Psal. 34.4. *Let them be confounded,* &c.) yet the more frequent, the less need there is of multiplying them without cause. It is true, that Numb. 10.35,36. from whence part of the words are taken, the precatory, or imprecatory form is most proper. However, it does not follow, but that here, the original words being indifferent to either, it may be otherwise. So Vatablus, Junius, Calvin: *dispergentur: fugient; peribunt: laetabuntur:* or to that purpose: not, *dispergantur, fugiant,* &c. However, I except not against this translation, as faulty; but only show the difference. I rather follow this sense, because it will fit the day of judgment better, if the words be read by way of absolute affirmation, (*they shall be scattered,* &c.) than by way of bare imprecation. Truly that is the time, the only time, of which we can positively say, That *Surgente Domino:* when God shall arise (to judgment) that then *at his presence* all the wicked and ungodly shall be confounded, and perish: and the righteous on the other side, shall rejoice; yea, *exceedingly rejoice.* God’s judgments in this world, we see not oftentimes: and those that we see, so doubtful and obscure sometimes, because we see not the hearts of men, as God does; and cannot so peremptorily determine, who are wicked, or godly, before God, and who not; that it is not always very safe for us to judge. Were it otherwise, that God did always judge and reward in this world, there would be no need of faith, or hope: or at least, faith and hope would be of another value, than we are made to believe: not of things future, and invisible; but of things present and seen. So that the very foundation of our religion, would be altered: there would be more religion, and fear of God in the world, questionless, (had God appointed it so from the beginning) than there is; but it would have been guilty of another nature, and upon other grounds. See also upon Psal. 11.6. *Fire and brimstone.* And upon Proverbs 1.32. *For the turning away of the simple.*

*{let the righteous be glad}* Psal. 32.11.

*{be glad}* Esay 65.14.

*{exceedingly rejoice}* Heb. *rejoice with gladness.*

**V. 4. ***{extol him}* So divers others too, as Vatablus, Junius, Bucer, and Calvin: but not so well, as I conceive, as they, not fewer in number, nor of less authority, that translate, ὁδοποιήσατε, with the Septuagint; that is, *prepare the way,* or *make ready:* or, *raise the way,* or, *make an high way:* which agrees better with the propriety of the word סלל in the original; and makes the words the more applicable to Christ, according to that of Esay 40.3. *The voice of him that crieth in the wilderness, Prepare ye the way of the Lord: make straight in the desert a high way for our God:* and Malach. 3.1. *Behold I will send my messenger, and he shall prepare the way before me:* applied to John the Baptist, Christ his forerunner and harbinger, by Zacharias his father, Luke 1.76. yea, by Christ himself, Luke 7.27.

*{that rideth upon the heavens}* So Vatablus, and Calvin, *in nubibus; vel in coelis:* and Aben Ezra confirms it from verse 33. *To him that rideth upon the heavens of heavens;* the Chald. *qui sedet super solium gloria,* as Genebrard expresses it: Junius, *qui insidet amoenitatibus.* Others, *in desertis, in the deserts:* which signification is as proper to the word as any other, and though it be not Esay’s word, (במדבר is his word) yet may the sense very probably, (as upon the former word has already been observed) be thought the same in both places, Esay 40.3. *The voice of him that cryeth in the wilderness,* &c. The Septuagint also render it, ἐπὶ δυσμῶν: which although by the old Latin it be translated *super occasum;* according to the more proper and usual notion of the Greek word δυσμός: yet by comparing with divers other places, of their translation, it may appear that by δυσμός, they intend sometimes *the desert:* as by some here is well observed. And if there be any weight in Aben Ezra’s observation from the 33 verse; for the former opinion and interpretation, as much may be said for this from those words of the seventh verse, *when thou didst march through the wilderness.* According to this interpretation, *that rideth,* (the Ark being meant, as generally throughout the Psalm: which was also called *God:* whereof see more upon Psal. 27.8. *Seek ye my face,* &c.) is as much as, that is carried: and by *deserts,* we must literally understand, either some deserts, or desert places, properly so called, through which the Ark had formerly, or was now to pass: or more generally, any rough unequal ways, for the honor and reverence of him that was to pass through, (as for a great prince, upon some solemn occasion, either of triumph, or the like, uses sometimes) now to be levelled and made plain. And to that purpose are these words of Esay also, which some bring to explain these: *Every valley shall be exalted, and every mountain and hill shall be made low: and the crooked shall be made straight, and the rough places plain,* Esay 40.4. Now for the mystical and spiritual meaning, as the words are applied to Christ, coming in the flesh, and to the operation of his Word and Spirit upon the souls of men, &c. though by some insisted upon here also; as among the rest, by Diodat, (whose notes having relation to his translation, different from ours here, should not have been exhibited without it, neither here, nor anywhere else, where different; as too often it is in the late English translations; to the great puzzling of any diligent rational reader) yet I do not conceive it so proper and necessary, in these annotations. Indeed, where the spiritual meaning proves to be (as sometimes; of which upon Psalm 22. and elsewhere) the most proper and immediate; that is, literal; the case is otherwise: but that is not our case here.

*{by his Name JAH}* Though to speak properly, there is but one God, that is the true God; yet because there be many that are called and accounted gods, besides the true; as the Apostle tells us in a place: The Psalmist here would not have us to praise God in general only, but more particularly, the true God; the God of Israel: (*That all people of the earth may know thy Name, and fear thee as doth thy people Israel,* 2 Chron. 6.33.) known and distinct from other imaginary gods, by his Name JAH. Wherein I do not conceive that the Psalmist had a respect so much to the derivation of the word, wherein is an intimation of true being, or existence, proper unto God only, in opposition to whatsoever else is said to have a being, whether men or angels; whose being is but an imperfect and improper being, if compared to God’s: and again, proper unto the true God, in opposition to idols, and imaginary gods: all which, though most true, yet is not so obvious to ordinary capacities: not so much to the derivation, I say, as to the common use and notion of the word JAH, as it was used both by Jews and Gentiles. For even among the Gentiles, the God of Israel was known anciently by that name: but that they pronounced it JAO, instead of JAH. So Diod. Siculus, an ancient Greek historian, (one of the best and most accurate, but that we have but little of him remaining) does clearly testify, and he calls him *Moses his God;* or, the God of Moses. And besides him, Macrobius, an ancient Latin author, though some ages after Christ, yet a man wholly addicted to the old Gentilism, and very well versed in ancient mythology, or theology; he records an ancient oracle, consisting of five Greek verses, concerning the name JAO, or as there written in Greek, ΙΑΩ. And moreover he bears witness, that one Cornelius Labeo, an ancient Roman, did write a book concerning that oracle, inscribed, *De Oraculo Apollinis Clarii.* Now this name JAH being but an abbreviation of JEHOVAH, and the same in effect; that there is in it somewhat extraordinary, may appear by God’s own words, Exod. 16.2,3. *by the Name of El Shaddai (or, God Almighty) was I known unto them; but by my Name JEHOVAH was I not known unto them:* and by Esay 42.8. *I am JEHOVAH; that is my Name; and my glory will I not give to another, neither my praise to graven images.* But what that is, and what is the genuine meaning of those places, would be a long question: for which I must refer the reader to the learned expositors there. But another question will be, not impertinent to this place, whether the ancient Romans, when they transferred this name JAH, or JEHOVAH, to their *Jovis,* or *Jove,* may be said to have communicated it to their idols. Not to make a long business of it which would not be very pertinent here: I say briefly, that for the manner of the worship, they may be said to have made an idol of their Jupiter indeed; but for their opinions, that which they say of him, the more sober of them, especially in their prayers and supplications, is, for the most part, very agreeable to what the Scripture says. Whence it is, that the Apostle, Act. 17.23. makes no scruple to apply a passage of an ancient heathen poet, concerning Jove, (Διός was the word in the poet) as they did call him, to the true God, JEHOVAH.

**V. 5. ***{A father of the fatherless}* Psal. 10.14,18.

*{in his habitation}* Some understand the sanctuary, as Psal. 26.8. *Lord I have loved the habitation of thy house,* &c. but in relation to former times, it may, as probably, be taken of Heaven.

**V. 6. ***{God setteth the solitary in families}* He gives children to them that be childless, and increases their families, 1 Sam. 22.5.

*{in families}* Heb. *in a house.*

*{in a dry land}* Which is barren of God’s blessings, which before they had abused, Psal. 107.41.

**V. 7. ***{O God, when thou wentest forth before thy people}* This and the next verse are taken out of Deborah’s song, Judg. 5.4,5. but fitted by David, with some little alteration, for his purpose. All tends, in a poetical strain and style, to set out the glory and majesty of God, which although in himself, at all times equally great and glorious, yet to man he does appear then most, when he does some great work among them; as was the deliverance of the Israelites out of Egypt. Now because no man can comprehend the greatness of God, essentially, and naturally; (this very word of nature, being but improperly spoken of God:) and that even what the wiser sort of men can apprehend of God, is above the reach of the vulgar; he is therefore, more suitably to vulgar capacities metaphorically, and allegorically set out, under types of things incurrent to the senses, and which are most apt to move the fancy.

**V. 9. ***{Thou, O God, didst send a plentiful rain}* Because we do not find that these words have reference to any particular time, their translation therefore is to be preferred, (for to either the words in the original are indifferent) who render it by the present, *Thou, O God, dost send,* &c. *whereby thou dost confirm,* &c. *when it is weary:* which may be understood of every year’s rain. And in very deed, this of the rain was a thing so considerable to that country, that as it was promised as a special blessing, Deut. 11.13,14,15. *And it shall come to pass,* &c. so it is remembered by some of the prophets, as a special motive to thankfulness; and an aggravation of their unthankfulness, that did not make that use of it; as Jer. 5.24. *Neither say they in their hearts, Let us now seek the Lord,* &c.

*{send}* Heb. *shake out.*

*{confirm}* Heb. *confirm it.*

*{inheritance}* Deut. 11.11,12.

**V. 10. ***{Thy Congregation hath dwelt therein}* The Hebrew word חיה signifies also beasts, whether wild or tame. Some therefore here translate it, *Pecus tuum;* by which nevertheless, they understand men, the Israelites: which is in effect the same as here, *Thy Congregation.* But others render it, *Oves,* or *Armenta;* and expound it of cattle, both sheep and other; it being one of the chief commendations of the land of Canaan, that it abounded with cattle. But the following verb, importing *dwelling,* is more proper of a congregation of men, as the word is often used; than of sheep, or any other cattle. Somewhat to this purpose, but more general, we have Psal. 107.36,37. *He turneth the wilderness into a standing water; and dry grounds into water-springs: And there he maketh the hungry to dwell, that they may prepare a city for habitation.*

*{of thy goodness}* God blessed the land of Canaan, because he had chosen that place for his Church: or, in reference to the foregoing passages, it may be understood of God’s goodness in providing for them, and refreshing them in the wilderness.

**V. 11. ***{The Lord gave the word, great was the company of those that published it}* The sense of these words, though not so obvious at first sight, at least as here translated; yet is certain enough, and which is not ordinary in obscure places, among expositors, generally agreed upon. It must be first conceived, that blessings and mercies of several kinds, some past, and some present, are here remembered. Having therefore spoken of God’s blessings upon the land itself, he passes to the remembrance and commemoration of some notable victories and deliverances. Some think that David in these words had a respect to his own times, and those many victories he had obtained upon his enemies by God’s special favor: which is like enough. But however, the words are so general, that they may relate to former times also: neither is any order of time observed in this song, but several things promiscuously set down, as fit matter for such a solemn and general thanksgiving, being digested and put together, with more regard to the verse and poem, than history. Matters of great importance, that are achieved abroad, are often set out and personated in the Scripture, under the *prosopopoeia* of posts and messengers; which is very poetical, and of great force to affect the sense with a conceit, partly of the suddenness of the thing; and partly of the suspense expectation of men. Whether it be upon a dismal or joyful occasion, we have examples of it in both kinds. *One post shall run to meet another, and one messenger to meet another, to tell the king of Babylon, that his city is taken at one end,* Jer. 51.31. This was a sad occasion: but of a joyful, Esay 52.7. *How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that bringeth good tidings, that publisheth peace,* &c. Our Psalmist says, *The Lord gave the word;* that is, he gave the occasion; it was his doing: good reason therefore he should have the praise of it. As if he would have said, which is to the same purpose, but more fully expressed, *This is the Lord’s doing, it is marvellous in our eyes. This is the day the Lord hath made; we will rejoice and be glad in it.*

*{company}* Heb. *army.*

*{great was the company of those that published it}* All interpreters I have seen, but one excepted, take this *company,* of maids and women entertaining these good news with songs and melody; as the fashion was in those days rather to make use of women to that purpose, than of men. For which we are referred to Exod. 15.20. Judg. 11.34. 1 Sam. 18.6. To which some add, Jud. 5.11. To this also might those words in Esay’s message have reference. *The virgin, the daughter of Zion hath despised thee,* &c. Esa. 37.21. That which has moved them to think of women here (for as the words run in English, men might be understood as well; neither can it be well otherwise, except the word *women* or *maids,* had been added to the text) is because the word in the original Heb. מבשרות is a feminine. But on the other side it is objected, that the Scripture nowhere calls such, מבשרות, *Publishers of good tidings.* For so indeed the word might have been expressed, as the Greek renders it, τοῖς εὐαγγελιζομένοις: (in the dative there, because of Δώσει, to which it is joined) that is, *publishers or messengers of good tidings,* and so the word is translated in that forequoted passage of Esay, *How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him* מבשר *that bringeth good tidings:* and מבשר טוב *that bringeth good tidings of good:* in the same place, and if so here too, I think it had not been amiss. The sense had been the plainer. Neither is it without example, that a word feminine should be put for a masculine. So קהלת commonly translated *Ecclesiastes,* or, *the preacher,* is a feminine too; though it be intended of a man, as appears, Eccl. 1.1. *The words of the Preacher, the son of David, King of Jerusalem,* and divers other places, where it is joined with a masculine. Besides, the Septuagint took it for a masculine, τοῖς εὐαγγελιζομένοις: as before: and so the Chaldee Paraphrase, (though quite to another purpose) in Genebrard, *Deus dedit verba Legis populo suo, ministerio Mosis & Aaronis, qui primi nuntiaverunt verbum Dei exercitui magno Israelis;* that is, (for that exposition, though much different from the common, is not altogether impertinent) God gave the words of his law to the people, by the ministry of Moses and Aaron, who first published the word of God to the great congregation of Israel. This I confess, though I find but one to join with me, I think most likely, that the words should be intended of men rather publishers, posts and messengers, or the like, as upon the former words has been showed, than of the women-singers. Yet I am not against the common interpretation; neither is the difference of moment, as to the sense and scope of the words: and in the mystical sense, which some make their chief business here, I would rather say with some, that therefore women are here mentioned, because in very deed the best news that ever were brought and published unto the world (to wit, the news of Christ his resurrection, of which ours depends) were first brought by women; which was not without a mystery, as learned expositors tell you: than as others, that therefore the feminine gender is used, not that women should preach, say they, (a good and necessary caveat) but to show the weakness of the means (fishermen, and the like) that God for the greater manifestation of his power in those days of miracles, was pleased to use in this great work.

**V. 12. ***{did flee apace}* Heb. *did flee, did flee.*

*{she that tarried at home}* The prey was so great, that not only the soldiers, but women also had part thereof.

**V. 13. ***{among the pots}* Though God suffer his Church for a time to lie in darkness, like a black scullion among the pots, yet he will restore it, and make it most shining and bright.

**V. 14. ***{in it}* Or, *for her, she was.*

*{in it}* In the land of Canaan, where his Church was called his inheritance, ver. 9. or in the wilderness, Num. 21.24,35. Psal. 135.10,11.

*{it was white as snow in Salmon}* Heb. תשלג בצלמון which because of the ambiguity of the verb תשלג either a second masculine, or a third person future feminine, may differently be translated. Some therefore render it, *dealbabas eam; thou didst make her white:* Others, as it is here. But it comes all to one. Mount Salmon, they say, is always covered with snow, which is the case of many high hills. Always white then. Now whiteness, among all men, for divers respects, is an emblem of prosperity, and purity. The meaning then will be, That although the land, inheritance, or people, before spoken of, had been clouded and darkened with persecution, and other sundry calamities; yet now it was again recovered through those manifold and miraculous defeats of their enemies to an estate of joyful peace and tranquillity. And because צלמון *Salmon,* in the Hebrew, does also signify darkness, which indeed is the proper signification, if it be not taken for a proper name; Some, with more acuteness of sense, and no less probability, translate, *albesces in caligine;* that is, *In darkness, thou shalt be white,* or shine. Salmon taken for a proper name, was the name of a hill, near Sichem, in the heart of the country. Whence there be, who would not have any similitude here in the words, but Salmon, by a *synecdoche* of part for the whole, to be mentioned here, for the whole land: as if he intended only, That there shall be great joy throughout the whole land. These different interpretations, and expositions, I mention the rather, because there is no great certainty of Mount Salmon’s constant whiteness through snow, upon which the first is grounded. I find no mention of any such elsewhere. The mount itself is mentioned, Judg. 9.47,48. but of the height of it, or of snow that uses to lie upon it, nothing there.

**V. 15. ***{The hill of God is as the hill of Bashan}* How Zion, a little sterile, could absolutely be compared to Bashan, a great high mountain, and yet not so great or high, as fertile, as all that country thereabouts, famous unto a proverb, for fertility (whereof see upon Psal. 22.12. *bulls of Bashan,* &c.) I do not see. I rather follow them therefore, that make Zion here to be opposed to Bashan; the smallness and sterility of the one, to the greatness and sterility of the other: but yet Mount Zion in another respect, compared and equalled; to wit, for its spiritual use and prerogative; because of God’s presence there in his Sanctuary: that is, in effect, spiritual blessings and benefits, set out by temporal. See upon Psal. 4.7. *Thou hast put gladness in my heart,* &c.

**V. 16. ***{Why leap ye, ye high hills?}* Why boast you of your strength and beauty, against this mountain of God?

*{hill which God desireth to dwell in}* Psal. 87.1,2. & 132.23.

*{yea the Lord will dwell in it for ever}* *For ever,* is ordinarily taken in the Scripture, for a long time. God dwelled in Zion; there stood his Temple (the only true Temple of God in all the world) a long time. But that Temple was a type of the Church; of which, and to which Christ has said, *Lo I am with you always, even unto the end of the world,* Matth. 28. last verse, which must be understood of the catholic or universal Church, against which, *the gates of hell* (as is promised, Matth. 16.17.) *shall not prevail.* For of particular Churches, what they are subject to, former ages have seen in Asia and Africa; and we see at this day to our grief.

**V. 17. ***{twenty thousand}* Dan. 7.10.

*{even thousands}* Or, *even many thousands.*

**V. 18. ***{Thou hast ascended on high}* Some take these words as an acclamation of the people to David, passing by in triumph upon a lofty chariot, as the manner was of triumphant conquerors, among the Romans. And they think the Romans might have it from the Hebrews. But there is not much probability that it should be so. And David, or whoever else is meant, might poetically be represented as triumphing, in allusion to the customs of other nations, although there was never any such triumph really, or any such custom among the Jews. But the greater number make them to be David’s own words to God, piously and thankfully ascribing his victories, and the victory of former ages, unto him. And then indeed according to the Scripture phrase, may God be said to be exalted, or to *ascend on high,* when he does execute judgment upon his enemies, and bless his people with temporal successes. See before upon Psal. 7.7. *for their sakes therefore return thou on high.* Some would have this *ascending of God,* to be meant of his passing from Egypt, a low country, to Mount Sinai, and thence to Sion, I do not conceive this so likely.

*{thou hast led captivity captive}* Heb. *thou hast captived a captivity:* Which is an ordinary Hebraism, used in divers places; for thou hast taken many captives.

*{thou hast received gifts for men}* That is, Thou hast enriched thy people with the spoils of their enemies, and hast made them tributary unto them. That is the literal meaning. Of the mystical, as the words are applied to Christ by the Apostle, Eph. 4.8. See the commentators.

*{hast received}* Or, *having received, gavest*.

*{for men}* Heb. *in the man, or, to men.*

*{yea for the rebellious also}* Literally, referred to David’s victories, or any other victories of the Jews in former ages, the meaning must be, that not ordinary enemies only, which soon yielded themselves; but even the most averse and rebellious, have been subdued, and led in triumph, and made part (many of them) of God’s people. But the mystical meaning, of Christ, his conquest, and the Gentiles coming in, by the preaching of his ministers, Apostles, and others, is more proper, and in a manner, more literal here; and in all this verse: as it is applied by the Apostle, Eph. 4.8. wherefore that is here *received,* we find *given; and gave gifts unto men:* which may seem different. But certain it is, as may be proved by many examples, that the word לקח signifies often to give, as well as to receive; that is, when a thing is received, or taken (which is the proper signification of the word) to be given. And so in English, amongst many, *take me,* for give me. Which confirms their interpretation, who make God himself to be spoken to, not David, or any other; of which see upon the former words, *Thou hast ascended on high.* For though the word לקח may bear both interpretations, both of giving, and receiving: yet that of giving would not fit here, if the words were intended of any other, than God himself.

**V. 20. ***{He that is our God, is the God of our salvation}* Or, *God is to us a God of salvation.*

*{salvation}* Heb. *salvations;* That is, all manner of salvations, or manifold deliverances.

*{unto God the Lord belong the issues from death}* In most extreme dangers God has infinite ways to deliver his.

**V. 22. ***{from Bashan}* As he delivered his Church once from Og of Bashan and other tyrants, and from the dangers of the Red-sea, so will he still do, as often as necessity requires.

**V. 23. ***{dipped}* Or, *red.*

*{in the same}* That is, in the blood of a great slaughter, where dogs shall lap blood: not unlike that, Psal. 58.10. See more there.

**V. 24. ***{thy goings}* It is like he means, when David carried the Ark into the Sanctuary; or, among his people, when he marched through the wilderness before them, ver. 7.

*{in}* Or, *into.*

**V. 25. ***{among them were the damsels}* Heb. בתוך, properly, *in medio,* that is, *in the midst;* which is ambiguous, whether he mean that the singers, &c. were *in the midst*, or among the damsels; as Junius understands it, who expresses it, *inter puellas,* &c. or that the damsels (supplying the word *were,* as here) were *in the midst,* or, *among the singers,* &c. However, because where we read of damsels, maids and women upon such solemn occasions playing with timbrels, &c. (as for example, 1 Sam. 18.6,7. Judg. 11.34. no mention is there made of any other singers and musicians;) we either conceive, that there was somewhat extraordinary in this solemnity here described, the bringing of the Ark to Jerusalem, of which we have spoken upon the first verse; or that the women were the most considerable part, and therefore, though some singers and others might be mixed among them, only mentioned in those places.

**V. 26. ***{from the fountain of Israel}* Or, *ye that are of the fountain of Israel:* Ye numerous issue coming from Jacob, as waters out of a fountain, Gen. 35.11. Esay 48.1. Heb. 11.12. See before upon Psal. 24.6. *thy face, O Jacob.*

**V. 27. ***{little Benjamin}* Benjamin is called *little,* because he was the youngest son of Jacob, & his tribe was almost destroyed, Judg. 20.

*{their ruler}* Who was some chief ruler of the tribe.

*{the Princes of Judah and their counsel}* The word in the Original רגמתם, here translated *their counsel,* and in the margin, *their company,* because nowhere else to be found in the Scripture, is somewhat obscure, and by divers differently translated. The greater part take it to signify *a company,* because it comes of a root that signifies *lapidibus obruere,* that is, to stone to death; which is done commonly by casting multitude of stones. And because אבן properly signifying a stone, seems to be taken for a ruler in those words, *the shepherd, the stone of Israel,* Gen. 49.24. some conceive that רגמתם, may bear that signification too. So some please. However, that is not it, that made Septuagint, and after them, the vulgar Latin, to render it *duces eorum,* that is, their captains, or leaders: for that was upon another consideration: either because they took רגם for רקם, as it seems S. Jerome did; who translates it, *in purpura sua;* in their purple: or, which is more likely; to be the very same, as ארגמן, which in very deed signifies purple: and so Genebrard renders it, *Principes Judae, purpurati eorum,* &c. But it would trouble a man more to give a satisfactory reason, why these 4. tribes, and no others are mentioned; whereas no man makes any question, but that all the tribes performed their part, and had an equal interest in this solemnity. I think they speak best to the purpose, that bid us not to be over curious. It is not possible, where the story is so compendious, to give an account of all circumstances; without the perfect knowledge whereof, many places must of necessity, in such allusions upon the by, be obscure, and unknown. It may be the heads of those tribes were employed in the managing of that business, more than others. Some must; all could not. But this is but a guess, and many other things may be guessed with as much ease, and probability. What is said that may seem to have some ground is, That those tribes, some of them, stood out against David longest, and therefore of purpose mentioned, that we might know he was now in full and quiet possession. And moreover, that two of these tribes, Benjamin and Judah, were nearest of all to Jerusalem; Zebulun and Naphtali the furthest off: that therefore by naming these, all the rest are comprehended. Now for the mystical sense, how the words are applicable to the Apostles; the ancients, who most insist upon it, if anything can be said to that purpose, very satisfactory, will best satisfy. For of late interpreters, some I see, that follow most the mystical sense, are glad here to stick to the literal, and pass the other over in silence.

**V. 28. ***{Thy God hath commanded thy strength}* So Deut. 28.8. and elsewhere, God is said to *command the blessing:* Psalm 42.8. *command his loving kindness;* Psalm 42.4. *to command deliverances,* Psal. 71.3. *to give commandment to save,* and the like. The expression is very proper, because God’s Word is his deed. He need but to speak, and the matter, whatever it be, is done. So Psal. 33.9. *For he spake, and it was done: he commanded, and it stood fast.* Longinus, a heathen, but one of the most judicious masters of rhetoric that ever was, admired the sublimity of this expression in Moses: and had Epicurus, that arch-atheist, well understood this, he would have forborne his ridiculous objections, (one of his chiefest grounds for denying of a providence) concerning God’s perpetual toil and inquietude in the creation, and administration of the world. But the centurion in the Gospel, he understood it, (for which he was commended and admired by Christ) when he said to Christ, *Lord, I am not worthy that thou shouldst come under my roof, but speak the word only, and my servant shall be healed. For I am a man under authority,* &c. Matth. 8.8,9.

**V. 29. ***{Because of thy Temple at Jerusalem}* This is differently translated. Most interpreters join these words, in their explications, though parted in the Text, as here, with the former words and verse; *Strengthen, O Lord,* &c. *Out of thy Temple* (*è templo tuo,* or as others, *à templo tuo*) *at Jerusalem:* which the Original will bear very well. מהיכל may be translated *è,* or, *propter,* out of, or, *because of*. So Psalm 20.2. *Send thee help from the Sanctuary, and strengthen thee out of Sion.* As God was said to dwell there (whereof has been spoken before) so also hear prayers and to send help from thence. And so God himself speaks sometimes. And some that translate *propter,* or *because of,* as it is here; yet join the words with the former nevertheless, as if he prayed, That God would be gracious and merciful unto them, for his Church’s sake. But on the other side, because it was an ordinary custom among all nations, where any temple, for the holiness of it, and for the credit of the deity to which it was consecrated, was of renown, thither to bring and send many presents; which was done by great men and princes especially: and of gifts and presents sent from foreign princes to the Temple at Jerusalem, we read in the second of Maccabees, Chap. 3.2. particularly, and elsewhere: besides what we find in Esay 18.7. *In that time shall the present be brought unto the Lord,* &c. according to some: for these reasons, it may do as well, that the words be referred to that which follows; *Because of thy Temple at Jerusalem shall Kings,* &c. Either sense is warrantable enough: The reader may take his choice.

**V. 30. ***{Rebuke the company of spearmen, the multitude of the bulls, &c.}* The continuation of so many metaphors, (especially if the first words be translated word for word, *the beast of the reeds,* as in the margin) has made the verse to sound like an enigma, than ordinary language: the last words excepted, which contain as it were the solution, or explication of the riddle. So that all the obscurity is rather in the dress, than in the matter and substance itself: neither is there any disagreement among interpreters about the meaning, but what the ambiguity of one word, as we shall see when we come to it, has made. But that which I most wonder at, is, that none of the interpreters, as many as I have seen, take any notice at all of the coherence with the former words and verse: which I conceive very considerable. Thus therefore it is, as I take it; David, as himself declares, 1 Chron. 22.7, &c. had purpose to have built a House unto God. God would not have it so; *But the word of the Lord* (so David himself as his own words are recorded in that place) *came to me, saying, Thou hast shed blood abundantly, and hast made great wars: thou shalt not build an House unto my Name, because thou hast shed much blood upon the earth in my sight: behold, a son shall be born unto thee, who shall be a man of rest, and I will give him rest from all his enemies round about,* &c. David therefore having in the verse before mentioned the Temple, (whether by it he understand, by a prolepsis or anticipation, that very Temple that was to be built after him, as some; or that Tabernacle, which himself had built for the Ark; will not be much material: though it be more likely, because he prophesies of things future in the verse following, which has reference to this, that in this also he intended the future Temple;) having therefore, as I said, in the verse before, mentioned the Temple, and his thoughts being set upon it, with that earnestness that became his zeal, (whereof see upon Psalm 27.8. *Seek ye my face,* &c.) and knowing that the house he intended unto God, must be the fruits of a quiet estate and settled peace, he turns his speech, as was most proper and likely, in a serious detestation of war; which certainly though he were a great warrior, and a valiant man, yet as a good and religious man, he never did affect: but in his zeal to God’s House, knowing what he did, he must of necessity so much the more detest and abhor. Now we come to particular words; By *the beast of the reeds,* most understand men armed with spears or arrows: either of which, either spears or arrows, the reeds of those countries will make. In Latin we know, that *arundo,* (a reed) is commonly taken for an arrow. Yet there be, who think by *the beast of the reeds,* voluptuous, proud, insolent men, may be understood; such as live in fertile soils, and in great plenty of all things. That reeds grow, and are an argument of fertile soils, they refer us to Esay 35.7. *in the habitation of dragons, where each lay, shall be grass with reeds and rushes.* But the more commonly received interpretation agrees best with that which follows. By *bulls,* (which follows) are understood the leaders and chief commanders; by *calves,* the ordinary soldiers and followers. In the next words there is more difficulty, which therefore we will take by themselves.

*{till every one submit himself with pieces of silver}* Hebrew מתרפס ברצי כסף, by some translated, *calcantem in frustis argenti;* that is, treading (or, *walking*) in pieces of silver: as belonging to *company,* before. And by this, they understand men proudly arrayed, with silver in their shoes, or other clothes: which, then, or in those days, though afterwards, as many other things of that nature common and ordinary, might be an argument of great pride and excess. Or, as some others, *which lie in plates of silver;* which they understand of beds and ceilings; for which they refer us to Esth. 1.6. and Job 22.24. But this does not so well here, and the expression is without example. Yet against them that make this מתרפס an accusative, and translate it, *submittentem te,* or *fingentem fabricare,* as some do, Calvin’s objection is pertinent, that he need not pray against them, if they had already submitted: or mention them as already subjected, and yet pray against them. But as most Rabbins supply the words, *dum supplicet quisque;* that is, as we have it here, *until every one submit himself,* so, that objection is prevented, and the sense that is made of it is very proper and pertinent: To submit themselves with pieces of silver, that is, to pay tribute, or to seek peace by presents. See Psalm 72.15. *to him shall be given of the gold of Sheba,* &c.

*{scatter thou}* Or, *he scattereth.*

**V. 31. ***{out of Egypt}* He prophesies that the Gentiles shall come to the true knowledge and worship of God, Isa. 19.21. Zeph. 3.10.

*{Ethiopia shall soon stretch out her hands unto God}* It may be understood; as Psal. 44.20. (*or stretched out our hands to a strange god*) of the spreading of hands in prayer, as the custom was then, not among the Jews only, but in all, or most places of the world. And by the translation a man might think it were the same word in the Original, here, and Psalm 44.20. but it is not so. For there the word is פרש, more commonly translated, to spread forth; as 1 Kin. 80.22,38. and Esay 1.15. *when ye spread forth your hands, I will hide mine eyes: when ye make many prayers,* &c. and here it is, if we translate word for word, *Ethiopia shall make her hands to run to God.* However, though the expression be not, the meaning may be the same. Or, *to stretch out,* by offering of gifts and presents; by way of subjection and submission; as most expound it. But it comes all to one. This of Ethiopia was also prophesied by Esay, Esay 18.7. *In that time shall the present be brought,* &c. See the Italian notes there. But others, whom the English translation seems to follow, by translating *of a people;* not *by a people,* which alters the case; understand there presents of the spoils of the Ethiopians; as Junius, and others. However, certain it is, that Ethiopia, according to this unquestionable prophecy, was one of the first kingdoms that was converted to the Christian faith; the occasion and means whereof, we read of Act. 8.27,28, &c.

**V. 33. ***{he doth send out his voice}* By his terrible thunders, he will make himself known to be the God of all the world, Psal. 29.3, &c. Exod. 20.18. Job 32.2, &c.

*{send}* Heb. *give.*

**V. 34. ***{his Excellency is over Israel}* God’s glory is seen most in his Church and in heaven, Psal. 29.9.

*{clouds}* Or, *heavens.* Or he may understand the thunder, before spoken of, the proper place whereof is in the clouds.

**V. 35. ***{thou art terrible}* In showing fearful judgments upon your enemies.

*{out of thy holy places}* In the Tabernacle and in heaven, both mentioned, verse 34. Psalm 14.7. Or, as others, *out of thy Sanctuaries,* (ממקדשיך) because the Tabernacle, as the Temple afterwards, had several divisions. And so some understand, *let them bring me unto thy holy Hill, and to thy Tabernacles,* Psal. 43.3. משכנותיך is the word there: dwelling places, properly.

# PSALM LXIX

## TITLE

*{Shoshannim}* See notes on the title of Psal. 45.

**Verse 1. ***{For the waters are come in unto my soul}* David was in danger of death, as one ready to be drowned. See upon Psalm 32.6. *surely in the floods,* and Psalm 42.7. *deep after deep.*

**V. 2. ***{deep mire}* Heb. *the mire of depth.*

*{where there is no standing}* No firmness or stableness to settle my feet, Psal. 40.2.

*{deep waters}* Heb. *depth of waters.*

**V. 3. ***{mine eyes fail}* Though his senses failed him, yet his faith was constant, and encouraged him still to pray, Psal. 119.82,123.

**V. 4. ***{hate me without a cause}* John 15.25.

*{without a cause}* Condemning me guiltless, Psalm 7.4. and 35.19.

*{which I took not away}* Though I would not find fault without cause, much less condemn, yet I wish (which is as little as I can say) that our English translators here had kept more strictly to the propriety of the Hebrew word, which signifies to rob, properly: or to take away by force and violence, as prey and booty, and so I find it, not in the ancient Greek only, ἃ οὐχ ἥρπαζον, but in most Latin translations, whether old, or late: *quod* (or, *quae*) *non rapui:* see Vettullius, Junius, Calvin, Bucerus: and others I believe. Not that I would make a mystery of it, as some do, who apply this very word to Christ, who though *he thought it no robbery, to be equal with God,* Phil. 2.6. yet was by the Jews hated, persecuted, and delivered into the hands of infidels, for this crime especially, and chiefly, because he *made himself equal with God,* John 5.18. *Therefore the Jews sought the more to kill him,* &c. and 19.7. *We have a Law,* &c. Not but that even this may very well, as the words of St. Paul are commonly expounded, be allowed of too, since that all Expositors apply the words to Christ in general, who though he had committed no sin, &c. but because I am of another opinion myself concerning the meaning of these words whereof somewhat occasionally has been said upon Psalm ver. 62.10. *become not vain in robbery:* not for that reason, therefore, I say, or at least, not for that only: but also because in my judgment, it does obscure the coherence, with the former words; and obscure too the literal immediate sense of these very words; upon which, as I conceive the coherence does depend. The words before, in the Original Hebrew, and in most other translations, I have seen, run thus: *they that would destroy me are mighty:* (in the Hebrew words there is an allusion, and that is one reason too, why they should not have been parted) *being mine enemies without cause,* or wrongfully: *that which I had not robbed:* (or taken and carried away by force) *then did I restore.* Now I conceive it very probable, that the Psalmist having said that they were his enemies without cause, he intended what follows as a reason or further explication of what he had said: to this purpose: that although they had used him as a robber indeed, forcing him to restore, that is, seizing upon all that he had violently, pretending, &c. Yet certain it was that he had done no such thing, as should deserve such usage. In ancient times when men lived in greatest simplicity, in tents more, than in towns; and their wealth consisted in cattle especially: all differences almost, and controversies that did arise between men, and produced strifes, and force of arms, were about beasts, pretended to have been unjustly seized, and driven away. From those times sundry expressions were derived, frequently used in after ages, when the world was much altered. As for example, the Grecians in Homer’s time, and long after him, to say, that they had done a man no wrong, nor given him just provocation, used to say, that they had never driven away either ox, or ass of his. So we find it 1 Sam. 12.3. too, *Whose ox have I taken,* &c. but that divers others expressions, savoring of later times, than those formerly spoken of, are there added: but the Greek expression, literally first, and then afterwards proverbially used in later times, goes no further than Oxen and Asses. To restore therefore what he had not robbed, is used by David here, as a common speech of these times, both to express his own innocence in general; in further confirmation of what he said, that they were his enemies wrongfully, or without cause:) and what violence and injustice he suffered from others, whether in his person, goods, or otherwise.

**V. 5. ***{O God, thou knowest my foolishness, and my sins, &c.}* Some Ancients have been much troubled at these words, and devised ways of interpretations, how to make them applicable to Christ: grounding upon this, That because they knew certainly by the application Christ his Disciples and Apostles made of them, some words of the Psalm belonged unto Christ: therefore all must likewise. The necessity of which consequence, as we have already said upon the like occasion, (whereof see upon Psalm 40.6. *mine ears hast thou opened;* in the end there) I do not understand. If that were so, then it must be considered, whether it be not the same reason for chapters in the Prophets, as is here for particular Psalms; if Christ be spoken of in a part, that he must be the subject of the whole: and then it would much concern us to know both of chapters and Psalms, whether they be always rightly divided; the division of the one, as now divided, being in comparison, but of late invention; and that of the others, not always very certain; of which somewhat has been said upon the title of the first Psalm, upon those words, *Psalm first.* But however as for these words we are now upon, they might have spared their trouble, and we our care for a further answer, if the words be but rightly understood. For certainly, his purpose here was not to accuse, but to justify himself, as he does often (whereof see upon Psalm 18.20. *The Lord rewarded me according to my righteousness,* &c.) against his enemies; to which end, to make his protestation the more solemn and serious, he does appeal to God, the searcher of hearts, for his witness. So best interpreters here, Calvin, Mollerus, Junius, and others. And the very coherence besides might induce us to believe it so. For though David had his sins, there is no question, and he freely acknowledges it himself elsewhere, more than once; yet this was no very proper place for this acknowledgment, so abrupt and so short, without any apparent coherence or dependence on that which either goes before or follows. So then, *Thou knowest my foolishness,* &c. that is, You know whether I be guilty of any such thing, or no, &c. Of the word *foolishness,* and *fool,* how taken in Scripture, see before upon Psalm 14. *The fool,* &c.

*{sins}* Heb. *guiltiness.*

**V. 6. ***{for my sake}* Let not the enemies’ hard usage of me, be an occasion that the faithful fall from you, or faint, Eph. 3.13.

**V. 9. ***{For the zeal of thy house hath eaten me up}* How zealous for God’s house, literally, David was, see before upon Psalm 27.8. *When thou saidst, seek ye my face,* &c. and how literally these words were applied by Christ his Disciples, see John 2.17. *And his disciples remembered,* &c. and why therefore we should avoid the literal sense, except our zeal be contrary to David’s, and yet we would gladly pretend to David’s holiness, and piety; I see no reason.

*{and the reproaches of them that reproached me, are fallen upon me}* Rom. 15.3. Psalm 89.50.

**V. 10. ***{When I wept}* When my zeal moved me to lament, and pray for their salvation, Psalm 35.13.

*{that was to my reproach}* Heb. *reproaches,* that is, to my greatest reproach, as if I could not have done a worse act.

**V. 11. ***{I became a proverb to them}* The more he sought to win them to God, the more they were against him.

**V. 12. ***{They that sit in the gate}* Men of authority condemned me, (Deuter. 25. ver. 7.) and vile persons derided me, Job 30.9.

*{I was the song of the Drunkards}* Job 30.9. Heb. *songs,* that is, a continual object of their songs, upon all occasions of meeting.

*{drunkards}* Heb. *drinkers of strong drink.*

**V. 13. ***{in an acceptable time}* He hoped that this was the time that God has appointed for his deliverance, 2 Cor. 6.2. Psal. 32.6. Isa. 49.8. & 55.6.

**V. 14. ***{out of the deep waters}* He shows a lively faith, in that he yet depends upon God for his deliverance, although he were in exceeding great danger, and seemed past hope of recovery, verse 2.

**V. 15. ***{let not the pit shut her mouth upon me}* So that I cannot get out.

**V. 17. ***{And hide not thy face from thy servant}* Psalm 27.9. Not that he feared that God would not hear him, but that care made him think that God deferred long.

*{hear me speedily}* Heb. *make haste to hear me.*

**V. 19. ***{mine adversaries are all before thee}* You see how I am beset with mine adversaries, Psal. 3.1. & 118.11,12.

**V. 20. ***{I looked for some to take pity}* He shows that it is in vain to put our trust in men, in our great necessities, but that our comfort only depends upon God: for man rather increases our sorrows than diminishes them, Psal. 142.4. Job 16.2.

*{to take pity}* Heb. *to lament with me.*

**V. 21. ***{They gave me also gall for my meat, and in my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink}* That is, my affliction through their persecution, was so great, that I had no comfort of my life; no part of it free from bitterness: my meat tasted as gall to me, and my drink was as vinegar. That it was a common proverb among the Hebrews (as indeed in other languages there be that come near to it) to feed one with gall, &c. appears by the Prophet Jeremy, who uses it with little alteration (for gall and wormwood have great affinity, and sometimes joined together in the Scripture; vinegar and water of gall, though in themselves much different, yet in the proverb they have the same use;) more than once. *Therefore, thus saith the Lord,* &c. *Behold, I will feed them, even this people, with wormwood, and give them water of gall to drink,* Jer. 9.15. & 23.15. Now this which the Psalmist, proverbially, and metaphorically, speaks of himself; in Christ it was verified literally, Matt. 27.34. the like whereof see Psal. 22.18. *They part my garments among them,* &c.

**V. 22. ***{which should have been for their welfare, but it became a trap}* Although the Apostles in quoting the Scriptures of the Old Testament did not always think themselves bound to the precise letter of the original Hebrew, and therefore make use often of that vulgar Greek translation, called the Septuagint, then in use; and sometimes go from that too, and yet vary from the Hebrew; so that their quotations are no rule, or law, to translators, absolutely: yet where their translation does agree well enough, or as well as any other, with the Hebrew, I think it should then be preferred before any other. Which is our case here, as I take it, concerning the translation of these words. The word שלומים, we grant, may signify welfare; (or, as it is rendered by others, *pacifica*) and, according to that signification, the whole passage probably be supplied, as it is here, *and that which should have been,* &c. But since it is certain that the Apostle, Rom. 11.9. citing these words, though he cite them not word for word, but with some addition, and inversion of the order, no ways material to the sense: but certain that he expresses this שלומים, by the Greek word ἀνταπόδομα, which signifies (as there translated) *recompense;* and as certain, that that sense is as proper to the word, and will fit this place as well as any other, I think it should have been preferred. But now if we follow that notion or sense of the word, there may be a further question concerning the supplement, or explication of the whole passage. Some would have them thus supplied: *and for a full recompense of that which they have done to me,* (expressed in the foregoing verse, *They gave me also gall,* &c.) *let their table be a trap unto them.* To this purpose I find them supplied by more than one; and it may do well so; I am not against it. Yet seeing that the Hebrew שלם, as the Greek ἀνταποδιδόναι, are taken *tam in bonam, quam malam partem,* that is, to recompense either good, or evil; and so ἀνταπόδομα accordingly: there may be another sense too; as proper and probable, if not more, Luke 14.12. *When thou makest a dinner or supper,* says our Savior, *call not thy friends, nor thy brethren, neither thy kinsmen, nor thy rich neighbours, lest they also bid thee again, καὶ γένηταί σοι ἀνταπόδομα, and a recompence be made to thee.* In the 14 verse the word is repeated again twice, (ἀνταποδοῦναι ἀνταποδοθήσεται) to the same purpose. Why then should we not keep that signification here also, since it will fit the place so well? Let their table become a snare before them; *and for a recompense* (of their invitation and entertainment) *let them find* (or, have) *a trap.*

**V. 23. ***{Let their eyes be darkened}* A proverbial expression of great affliction and misery; as Lam. 5.17. *For this, our heart is faint; for these things, our eyes are dim.* See also upon Psal. 146.8. *The Lord openeth.* As for this and other like imprecations either here following, or in any other Psalm, see upon Psalm 35.4. *Let them be confounded and put to shame,* &c. and Psal. 41.10. *that I may requite them.*

**V. 25. ***{Let their habitations be desolate}* Acts 1.20. Give them no posterity; or, banish them out of their fathers’ houses.

*{their habitation}* Heb. *their palace.*

*{let none dwell}* Heb. *let there not be a dweller.*

**V. 26. ***{to}* Or, *of.*

*{those whom thou hast wounded}* Heb. *thy wounded.*

**V. 27. ***{Add iniquity to their iniquity}* Give them over to a reprobate sense, as a punishment to their sin, that so they may go on in evil till they perish, Rom. 1.24. 1 Thess. 2.16. 2 Thess. 2.11,12.

*{iniquity}* Or, *punishment of iniquity.*

**V. 28. ***{out of the Book of the living}* Though these expressions in this, and the precedent verse, being taken spiritually, or mystically, import no less than eternal damnation; yet as generally used and understood amongst the Jews in those days, when eternal life was a mystery as yet but very sparingly dispensed (for which reason Christ is said *to have brought life and immortality to light,* 2 Tim. 1.2. of which see more upon Psal. 37.1. *Fret not thyself,* &c. in the second column) they import no more, than absolute, or total destruction, in a most exemplary manner, in this present world: See also upon Psalm 59.5. *be not merciful.*

*{the book of the living}* Phil. 4.3. Rev. 3.5.

*{written with the righteous}* Luke 10.20. Heb. 12.23.

**V. 31. ***{better than an Ox}* There is no sacrifice which God more esteems, than thanksgiving for his benefits, Psalm 50.23.

**V. 32. ***{humble}* Or, *meek.*

**V. 33. ***{despiseth not his prisoners}* His servants that suffer for his cause, Eph. 3.1. or, all his that are afflicted: for affliction is God’s prison, Psal. 142.7.

**V. 34. ***{moveth}* Heb. *creepeth.*

**V. 36. ***{The seed also of his servants shall inherit it}* Under the temporal promises of the Land of Canaan, he comprehends the promises of life everlasting to the faithful and their posterity, Psal. 102.28. See more upon Psalm 37.2. *For they shall soon be cut down,* &c.

# PSALM LXX

## TITLE

*{to bring to remembrance}* See notes on the title of Psalm 38.

**Verse 1. ***{Make haste}* This Psalm agrees with Psalm 40. from verse 13. to the end of it. Of such other Psalms, twice to be found in this book, and of small differences to be found upon comparing; see upon Psalm 53. ver. 1. *the fool hath said.* As for this Psalm, the most considerable difference is in the last verse, *But I am poor,* &c. of which see there. In all the rest, there is no difference to be observed, but that some words (none very material) of Psalm 40. are here left out. As for example, in this first verse, the very first word, רצה *be pleased,* Psal. 40.13. *Be pleased, O Lord, to deliver me:* for which we find here in our English *make haste,* supplied from the words next following. But it being apparent that this Psalm is the same with Psalm 40. from verse 13. if anything at all be to be supplied, to make the sense perfect: (which it may be, needs not: the order of the word in the Original Hebr. is: *O God, to deliver me; to help me, O Lord, make haste:* which most interpreters, without any further supplement, content themselves with:) but however I say, If anything be supplied, I should think nothing more proper, or warrantable, than what we find Psalm 40.13. which verse there does answer to this. However this here, more warrantable, than either Greek, or vulgar Latin, where somewhat else is supplied, taken from neither place: but not so, as to make any considerable diversity of sense: much less than what is here supplied. Which nevertheless was to be noted, because in matter of text, there is no diversity so inconsiderable in itself, but may be considerable to some respects and purposes.

*{make haste to help}* A godly man in extremity of distress may call upon God earnestly to make haste to his deliverance; there is no hurt in that: nay God is well pleased with our earnestness, and sometimes we reap the fruits of it (for absolutely and generally it is never without fruit) present help and deliverance, according to our own desire, and the intention of our prayers. But provided still, that in our earnestness, there be a resolution of patience, and submission to God; with a certain belief and assurance, that God’s time, be it sooner, or later, is, and will be our best time, though present sense, (because *no affliction, or, chastening for the present seemeth to be joyous,* Heb. 12.11.) and weak flesh suggest the contrary. So may we reconcile this of David here, with that of the Prophet Esay, *He that believeth, shall not make haste,* 28.16. which though some by a kind of *metalepsis*, or *metonymy*, translate otherwise, *shall not be forlorn,* or *shall not go astray:* yet I am (with best interpreters) for the literal both translation and interpretation of the words: *shall not make haste,* that is, shall notwithstanding any earnestness of prayers, with patience wait God’s time and leisure: as David himself elsewhere professes of himself, (that we may reconcile David with himself also) that he *waited patiently:* Psalm 40.1. As for the Apostles’ (whether St. Paul, Rom. 9.32. or St. Peter, 1 Pet. 2.6.) quotation of these words, *He* (or, *whosoever*) *believeth in him, shall not be confounded or ashamed:* in case the difference were greater than it is; yet the sense fitting his present purpose, and being warranted by divers other places of Scripture, it is not to be wondered at, if he followed the Greek translation of the Septuagint, being most current among the Jews of those days: and indeed the only Scripture divers of them, which understand not the Hebrew tongue, living in foreign countries, read and understood. But I do not conceive the difference to be so great as to conclude with some very learned men, that the said Seventy read יבוש for יחיש (who also would have that to be the true reading) there being so much affinity in the nature of the things, between making haste, and being ashamed, or confounded, as that the putting of the one for the other, does not, of necessity, import a difference of sense: especially as the words may be used in some language though they be not so in all, ἀσχημοσύνη (to which εὐσχημοσύνη is opposed) is used by the Grecians for impatience, frequently (ἀσχημονεῖν, εὐσχημονεῖν, &c. likewise) though the word properly import no more than undecent behavior. See also upon Psalm 34. last ver. *that trust in him:* &c. But to return to the sense of the words of the Prophet, which we said we intended to reconcile with those of David’s, that we are now upon: which may be thought needless, and impertinent, if the words (as some would have it) were intended by the Prophet in another sense; I will produce a very warrantable (both for his antiquity, and learning) interpreter of the Prophet’s words, to wit, the author of the book commonly called Ecclesiasticus; *My son,* says he, *if thou come to serve the Lord,* &c. *Set thy heart aright, and constantly endure, and make not haste* (καὶ μὴ σπεύσῃς ἐν καιρῷ ἐπαγωγῆς) *in time of trouble.* Either he had, as probably, the Prophet in his thoughts; or it was (from the Prophet, originally, perchance) become a common speech among the faithful; *Not to make haste in time of trouble:* Which also does well vindicate the true reading of the Hebrew Text we have, from that suspicion, we have spoken of.

*{O God, to deliver me}* Psalm 40.13, &c.

*{to help me}* Heb. *to my help.*

**V. 2. ***{Let them be ashamed, &c.}* Psal. 35.4. & 71.13. He wishes that his enemies, who laid plots to take away his life, might be disappointed and shamed.

**V. 3. ***{For a reward of their shame}* He calls their shame, that which they had brought upon himself: theirs, because they had been the cause of it to him. Hereby we are taught not to mock at others in their misery, lest the same fall on our own necks, Prov. 24.17,18.

**V. 4. ***{Let all those that seek thee, rejoice}* Let those that worship you aright, see my deliverance, and be glad of it, and praise you for it.

**V. 5. ***{make haste unto me, O God}* Psalm 40.17. (which otherwise from verse 13. agrees with this, as is noted upon the first verse) we had it, *But I am poor and needy: yet the Lord thinketh upon me: thou art my help, and my deliverance,* &c. by comparing of which places may further appear, (besides what has already been said to that purpose upon verse first, *make haste to help*) that David when he so earnestly desired God to make haste, was confident nevertheless, that even then, whilst God did put him off, and deferred the accomplishment of what he so earnestly prayed for; God, I say, did think of him; and took good notice both of his prayers, and what he did suffer; upon which confidence he might well ground, that God would put off his deliverance no longer, than might be for his own good: which persuasion cannot but breed great inward comfort in the midst of greatest extremities, and dispose a man to wait patiently: Psal. 40.1. and to argue against himself, and present sense, that though the time seem long, yet it will not be long, Hab. 2.3. *though it tarry,* &c. So let a man be never so earnest in his prayers, yet it may truly be said, to be still, Psal. 4.4. and to possess his soul in patience, Luke 21. verse 19. See also upon Psalm 31. ver. 7. *thou hast known my soul.*

# PSALM LXXI

**Verse 1. ***{In thee, O Lord, do I put my trust}* Psal. 31.1. See upon Psalm 16.1. *In thee do I put my trust.* He prays to God with full assurance of faith, that he will deliver him from his adversaries.

*{be put to confusion}* See upon Psalm 34. last verse. *That trust in him.*

**V. 2. ***{Deliver me in thy righteousness}* Keeping your promise to me, and therein approving yourself just and righteous. So some here, and elsewhere very often, as often almost, as David does appeal by way of humble supplication to God’s righteousness; or does applaud himself, and praise him for his experiential knowledge of it upon particular occasions. Now, though God’s truth be one of his most glorious and essential attributes, wherein keeping of promise is included, and sometimes particularly specified: and again though it be granted, that we may sometimes, David might at least, put God in mind of his promise, and call upon him, with humility for performance: yet that David would tell God of his righteousness so often in this sense, that is, of his engagement and obligation, with a tacit intimation, or inference, that had not God, or should not God deal thus and thus with him, he were not righteous; so often to tell him of it, as common interpreters make him; I do not know whether it did stand with David’s modesty and civility so well: and much less, with his discretion and safety. For though we may be sure that God will keep his promise to us: yet all God’s promises (as his threats) being conditional, as we are taught very particularly, Ezek. 33.13. *When I shall say to the righteous, that he shall surely live,* &c. who can so far presume upon his own performances, as to dare (except it be done sparingly, and with much humility) to challenge God, as bound in justice? But I will leave men to their liberties, where the coherence will bear it. I am sure it will not in all places. As for example, besides the very place the subject of this observation; and ver. 15,16,19,24. following; Psalm 51.14. *and my tongue shall sing aloud of thy righteousness:* (it was besides David’s purpose there, to put God in mind of his promises) and Psalm 143.1,2. *and in thy righteousness. But enter not into judgment,* &c. We conclude therefore, that since righteousness, or rather the Hebrew word, translated righteousness, does often signify goodness, equity, yea mercy, pure mercy: I know no reason why David, when he does ascribe righteousness unto God so often, should not rather be understood in this general sense, more applicable to all men, and by consequence more comfortable; than with reference to particular promises. That the Hebrew word, which properly signifies justice, or righteousness, is taken not for goodness and equity only, as in some other languages, words answerable to that; but for mercy also, in the precisest sense; see upon Psalm 22.31. *and shall declare his righteousness.* For further proof whereof, may be added, that Matth. 6.1. *Take heed that ye do not your alms.* where some, or most later copies have it ἐλεημοσύνην: ancient both copies and translations had δικαιοσύνην; which by Beza, and other chief commentators is approved, as the genuine reading.

*{Be thou my strong habitation}* So Vatablus too; and others, *in robustum habitaculum:* which might be doubtful, whether he read it מעון or מעוז: *habitationis,* or, *fortitudinis:* because the bare word rock, or *rupes,* may imply strength, without any further expression. But when he gives us his translation *ad verbum rupem fortitudinis;* and not *rupem habitationis;* he makes it clear, that he read מעון and not מעוז: which nevertheless (this latter I mean) is the reading in this place of all the printed Hebrew Bibles I have seen. And so Calvin too, in the very Text, *rupem fortitudinis,* and not according to the original *habitationis:* by Junius, and some others, of our side, well expressed: and by Genebrard the Papist asserted: not without some reprehension of those he calls *recentiores,* for preferring that other reading. A man might suspect that their eyes, the difference being so small, might deceive them; and the rather, because they give us no reason. But the matter is this. Psalm 31. at the beginning, we have three verses, that agree with the three first of this Psalm, almost word for word. Now there it is מעוז *fortitudinis,* in all copies; and not מעון, and because it is there so, and that the verses seem to be the same, and that all the difference in the original words is but the drawing out of one letter a little more, or less: (an easy mistake, if men be not very wary:) it is therefore conceived, that, here also the same word should be. For my part, although I make no great matter, which of the two we read where the difference is so little, either in the writing, or sense itself (in this place, at least:) yet I shall neither join with them, that keep to the proper; or at least, more generally received reading. Neither is that a warrantable ground, that because those three verses are almost the same, therefore they must agree in every word; for that we know they do not; there be, even here other differences besides this, which no man does question. But of this argument in general, See more upon Psalm 59.9. *Because of his strength:* towards the end of the Note, *Now if any shall upon this ground,* &c. and Psalm 34. last v. *that trust in him.* As for the English here, though the words be doubtful in the Text, yet in the margin they agree plainly with the original. *Be thou to me for a rock of habitation.*

*{whereunto I may continually resort}* Yet Psal. 61.2. his words were *lead me to the rock that is higher than I;* which may seem contrary to what is here desired. But however the words may seem to differ, yet well understood, they will come to one sense. A rock there, higher than man’s reach, was a good periphrasis of God, in opposition to ordinary rocks, and fences: so here, a rock, unto which I may continually resort, not for the easiness of access so much; (though that be somewhat) as for the safety and security of it; as if he had said: such a rock, so invincible, so inaccessible, as that I may need no other, at any time (as many times they that are eagerly pursued, are fain to fly from one fort, to another:) but at all times, and against all kinds of dangers, and enemies, find it a secure safeguard and protection, and what rock can that be, but God himself? See there upon that Psalm, Psalm 91.2. more of rock in general.

*{thou hast given commandment}* Or, *give commandment.*

*{thou hast given commandment to save me}* Psal. 6.28. *Thy God hath commanded,* &c. See there. But it is the opinion, I see of many Expositors, that by this and the like expressions, Angels are intended. That God indeed does very commonly use the ministry of Angels to accomplish his purposes towards men, whether to punish, or to save, or whatever else it be, that he will have done; besides frequent examples throughout the whole Scripture: we have the clear assertion of the Apostle, or rather of the Psalmist; avouched and confirmed by the Apostle, Heb. 1. v. 7. and 14. Nevertheless, In such expressions as these, where Angels are not expressed, I think with best (if not most) Expositors, that it is more proper, and warrantable to understand the words, as is there (upon Psalm 68.28.) showed, and explained. Of Angels in general and their ministry, see more upon Psalm 33.13. *The Lord looketh from Heaven.* Psal. 6.28. You have infinite means, and all creatures are at your commandment, therefore show some sign that I shall be delivered. See more there.

**V. 4. ***{out of the hand of the wicked}* He means some principal adversary.

**V. 5. ***{thou art my trust from my youth}* He strengthens his faith by the experience of God’s benefits, who did not only preserve him in his mother’s belly, but took him thence, and ever since has preserved him.

**V. 6. ***{from the womb}* Psal. 22.9,10. Esay 46.3. David does often insist upon this particular of God’s tender care and providence over infants, wherein not his goodness, only; but his divine power, to the great admiration of most understanding naturalists, does abundantly manifest itself. And though it be a general providence, wherein all mankind (yea and brute beasts, in some degree, for man’s sake:) have an equal interest; yet the commonness of it does not take away our obligation of particular thanks and acknowledgments: and David’s thankful remembrance in this kind, does condemn their unthankfulness, that never praise God for general benefits, for which God does expect to be glorified as much as for particular, if not more. However, these words may have another interpretation too. See upon Psal. 22.9. *that took me out of the womb.*

**V. 7. ***{I am a wonder unto many}* All the world wonders at me, because of my miseries; as well they in authority, as the common people: yet being assured of your favor, I remain steadfast.

**V. 9. ***{in}* Or, *to.*

*{in the time of old age}* You that helped me in my youth, when I was full of strength, help me till I be old: or, now I am old and weak, help me so much the more.

**V. 10. ***{against}* Or, *of.*

*{lay wait for}* Heb. *watch, or observe.*

**V. 11. ***{God hath forsaken him}* Thus the wicked both blaspheme God, and triumph against his Saints, as though he had forsaken them, if he suffer them to fall into their hands.

**V. 12. ***{O my God}* In calling him his God, he puts back the false reports of the adversaries, that said, *God hath forsaken him,* Psalm 22.1.

*{make haste for my help}* Psal. 70.1.

**V. 13. ***{confounded and consumed that are adversaries to my soul}* Psalm 35.4,26.

**V. 15. ***{for}* Or, *though,* as Psalm 4.8.

*{for I know not the numbers thereof}* Because your benefits toward me are innumerable, I cannot but continually meditate and rehearse them. Psalm 40.5. *Many, O Lord, my God,* &c. But the original Hebrew word, here translated numbers, does also signify learning, as for example, Isa. 29.12. *And the book is declared to him that is not learned:* Heb. *to him that knoweth not a book,* or letters, or numbers: And though it be a singular here, and a plural in David; nay a plural of another form; yet there being many examples of like irregularities, they may be accounted one and the same word nevertheless. It is generally conceived, that both the ancient Greek and Latin aimed at this sense in their translation of these words; the one, οὐκ ἔγνων γραμματείας: the other expressing them, *Non novi literaturam:* as understanding David to this purpose: That he was not a scholar, nor yet ambitious of much learning: his only business, which he proposed to himself, instead of all learning, being this, to praise God, and to set forth, or magnify his power, his righteousness, &c. Calvin is very angry with this translation, and tells us that Anabaptists, and Enthusiasts (he calls them fanatics, men of distempered brains) have made advantage of it, to cry down all good learning, and to inveigh against the use of liberal sciences.
