And well may he call this use, or sense of the words Anabaptistical, or more truly, Diabolical, from that spirit that works in men of that profession, to the undermining, of all true religion; and promoting of atheism (if once they become absolute masters, as at Munster in Germany, and other places) or at the best of Turkism, which barely stands upon these two pillars, force of arms and banishment of human literature. But I do not conceive the translation itself deserves so rigid a censure, or can justly be charged to have been the ground, upon a rational account of such pretences. For what if David had said that he had no other learning; or desired no other, or greater than to be able to praise God? Must we make that construction of it, that therefore he despised learning: or rather this, that the only end he proposed unto himself of all learning, and of all sciences, to what degree of perfection soever he should attain in them, whether greater, or less, whether himself, or anybody else that depended of him; was, and ever should be, to glorify God; That as he accounted it perfection of learning to do this as it should be done (towards which as there is no kind of good learning, but may help: so none so great, but may be thought, insufficient: 2 Cor. 2.16.) so all learning without this use, though never so great, or so complete otherwise, but ignorance. Sure I am, that some heathens (as we commonly call them) very learned themselves, and great favorers of learning in others, have said little less. Not to insist upon inferior authors, as Seneca, and others, that lived since Christ began to be preached and known in the world: Divine Plato (not unworthily so styled by all ages) in divers places reduces all human wisdom, and knowledge to the knowledge of God, and godliness. Among others, in his *Theaetetus* he (peremptorily) maintains that whatsoever can be commendable in man be it wisdom, knowledge, prudence, eloquence, yea courage, fortitude, and the like is comprehended in the true knowledge of a just God: and that without this knowledge, no virtue is virtue; no commendation, solid and true. Περὶ τοῦτο γὰρ ἡ ὡς ἀληθῶς δεινότης ἀνδρὸς, καὶ οὐδενία τε καὶ ἀνανδρία, ἡ μὲν γὰρ τούτου γνῶσις (where some Latin interpreters do Plato wrong, translating *horum,* for *hujus:* as clearly appears by the context) σοφία, &c. It is the opinion of most ancient Fathers and other Christian writers, that Plato was acquainted with the writings of Moses, and the Prophets. Whether it be so or no, I shall not here dispute. But it cannot be denied, but that in that passage, and others like unto it, he may seem to have imitated the prophet Jer. *Thus saith the Lord let not the wise man glory in his wisdom,* &c. Jer. 9.23,24. I said I would, not mention later heathen authors, yet upon this subject I cannot altogether omit to make mention of one, both for his high degree, having been a great Emperor in his time; and for the many passages he has to this purpose, (in learning also not inferior to any of the wisest of philosophers that had been before him:) very considerable. But I shall content myself here with one of his shortest passages of this subject. Τί ἀρκεῖ, says he in a place: What will, or may satisfy? What but to worship and praise the Gods: (that was the phrase in those days, though he often uses the singular also:) and to do good unto men? Anton. Book v.27. Now if heathens though learned, and favorers of learning, could speak so, without offence: why should not we construe David as favorably? Neither did St. Paul certainly, where he professed of himself, that he would from henceforth know nothing, but Christ and him crucified, 1 Cor. 2.2. intend by this (or so understood by any sober man) a renunciation to learning either human (whereof he had made so good use for the propagation of the Gospel, as appears by his Epistle, and what is recorded of him in Scripture History:) or other: but this, that what learning he had, power, facility, or ability in a kind, should all be employed and improved to that end and use, to advance the knowledge of Christ crucified. I think such an argument should not be passed over slightly; and Calvin’s earnestness has made me the more earnest.

But now I must acquaint the reader with my suspicion, being grounded, as I conceive, upon no little probability. I doubt very much, whether the Greek interpreters, or Latin either, intended any such sense, as is commonly apprehended. For I would know first what kind of word γραμματείας is, or by whom ever used, (if at all used) for learning, or literature? That which may be said for it, as I conceive, how it should come to signify learning, is this. סופר a Scribe, is in Scripture Greek γραμματεύς. Now because these Scribes are generally conceived, to have been very exact (by their profession at least) in the knowledge of the Law; it may be, that this word γραμματεία was formed with reference to γραμματεύς, to express such learning properly, as might belong to a Scribe, or learned interpreter of the Law. However, Suidas, a man well acquainted with old Greek words, whether sacred, or profane, understood it far otherwise, interpreting it, ⟦ἐξαριθμῆσαι; reading provisional⟧U4; and gives a sense of the words, quite different. It may be read in him, if any be so curious: for it is very remote from that which is commonly received for true. But besides this; that reading γραμματείας, is uncertain in some copies, it is πραγματείας, and accordingly in some Latin copies, *Negotiationes.* Somewhat more might be said of that too: but I have said enough, for this place.

*{numbers thereof}* Psal. 40.5.

**V. 16. ***{I will go in the strength of the Lord}* I will remain steadfast, being upheld by the power of God.

**V. 17. ***{thou hast taught me from my youth}* By instruction and experience.

**V. 18. ***{Now also}* He desires, that as he has begun, so he would continue his benefits, that his liberality may have perfect praise.

*{when I am old}* Heb. *unto old age, and gray hairs.* And by consequence, in more danger and need of help, ver. 9.

*{old}* Verse 9.

*{until I have shewed thy strength unto this generation}* Deliver me now, that I may praise you for this deliverance; both in word and writing.

*{thy strength}* Heb. *thine arm.*

**V. 19. ***{Thy righteousness also}* The just performance of your promise, v. 2.

*{O God}* His faith breaks through all temptations, and by this exclamation, he praises the power of God.

*{O God, who is like unto thee?}* Psal. 89.6,8.

**V. 20. ***{shewed me}* Heb. *made us to see:* That is, to endure, as Psal. 34.12. & 89.49. & 27.4. See upon Psal. 60.3. *Thou hast showed.*

*{and sore troubles}* As he confesses, that God is the only author of his deliverance: so he acknowledges, that these evils were sent unto him by God’s providence.

**V. 22. ***{With the Psaltery}* Heb. *with the instrument of psaltery.*

*{even thy truth, O my God}* He confesses, that his long tarriance would be well recompensed, when God should perform his promise.

**V. 23. ***{my soul which thou hast redeemed}* For there is no true praising of God, except it come from the heart.

**V. 24. ***{My tongue also shall talk of thy righteousness}* The Psalmist does insist upon the same thing, with variety of words, which shows the fullness of his heart: Matth. 12.34. and though God do not regard the praise of the lips or tongue, or rather hate it, where there is not a concurrence of the heart, or soul: yet naturally there is such dependence between these, that where the tongue is silent; it is to be feared, that the heart is sterile. They therefore (great talkers especially) that seldom speak of God, or of things tending to godliness, in their ordinary talk have great reason to suspect themselves, that the fear of God, and the spirit of godliness, does not abide in them: though they may flatter and deceive themselves by goodly pretences; as that God knows their hearts; and that they hate hypocrites, which make a fair show outwardly, because they would be thought religious, and the like.

# PSALM LXXII

## TITLE

*{A Psalm for Solomon}* *Of, for, or, concerning Solomon*: all these ways the Hebrew לשלמה may be expressed; and accordingly opinions do vary; concerning both the author and the argument of the Psalm. First for the author; there be that ascribe it to Solomon, grounding especially upon the inscription, because as in the inscription of many other Psalms, לדוד is interpreted David’s; (of David) so they think it should be here. But that rather shows, that notwithstanding the inscription be לשלמה which by the article should rather be a dative, than a genitive; it may be Solomon’s nevertheless, if there be other good grounds for it; than that it must be his, or probably is his, if nothing else induce to that belief. That Solomon himself would so speak of himself, as this Psalmist speaks of him; or that God would make him the instrument of his own praises, in such an hyperbolical style, is not very likely. So that the subject and tenor of the Psalm, is rather an argument against that opinion, than for it. There is one Psalm more, and but one, as I remember, so inscribed; and that is the 127. Psalm. That Psalm for the subject of it, as it might be Solomon’s, so anybody’s else: David’s, as probably, as any other, that has not his name prefixed. And it may be it was composed by him, upon occasion of Solomon’s birth, as a thankful acknowledgment of God’s blessing. By the subject of it, it should be some *Genethliacum,* or congratulatory Psalm for a newborn child, whoever were the author. Neither then from that inscription there, can any argument be drawn, to prove Solomon the author of this. Why David rather, somewhat may be gathered perchance from the inscription, in the best copies of the Greek translation, which we find to be this; Εἰς Σαλωμὼν ψαλμὸς τῷ Δαυίδ: that is, *A Psalm of David concerning Solomon*: but more from that which is at the end of this Psalm, v. 20. *The prayers of David the son of Jesse are ended.* So much for the author, or instrument rather, and penman: for of the principal author, the Spirit of God, there is no question; and so long, we need take the less care.

But the second *quaere,* concerning the subject, is a matter of more, if not difficulty, yet consequence to the right exposition of the whole Psalm. That Christ is the main, is a thing generally agreed upon among all Christians: but whether the only, is the question. There be that make it a matter of great impiety, to think that any part of it belongs unto Solomon. Genebrard is of that opinion; but not he only. Even among them that we account best interpreters, some by their way of interpretation, may seem to have thought little less. Although therefore somewhat has been said already to this purpose upon the 45. Psalm, partly upon the title, and partly upon several verses of that Psalm, which both for the subject, and for the sublimity of the style, answerable to the matter, has great affinity with this: Yet because I find so much opposition here, somewhat here also shall be added. The main objection is, that most things in this Psalm are too high, to belong to Solomon, or any mortal man. They put together whatsoever is of that nature, which indeed makes the greatest part of the Psalm, and then ask, whether this were literally true of Solomon. But they are much mistaken in their ground, as I conceive. For first, not to speak of the like figurative speeches in other parts of Scripture, as the Prophets, and others, which are many, against which the same may be objected, we say here, that the style being poetical, and the subject extraordinary, the inauguration, or welcoming of a King to his throne by auspicious acclamations, ample hyperboles might the better become it. How ordinary it is for Poets, even the most sober, and Orators, upon such an occasion to be hyperbolical, who knows not? Or should we say, that Virgil, that so much admired Poet by all ancients, the greatest wit of his age, (the best age for wits as ever the world knew) that he did not intend his Eclogue, *Sicelides Musae,* &c. truly and really to be a *Genethliacum carmen,* upon the birth of a noble Roman, much inferior to a King, because most of it fits the Son of God much better, and in divers of his expressions, agrees with the predictions of the Prophets, concerning the future happy state of the world under the Messias? That his pen in very deed, was, by a secret providence, so directed to such expressions, (whether he had them out of the Sibyl’s verses, or no) that should fit Christ so well, and none properly, and literally, but him, that so Christ his birthday (for he wrote much about that time) might by the greatest of wits among them, who gloried in nothing so much as wit, be celebrated; has been I know the opinion of very learned and judicious men, to whom I easily subscribe. Neither was this providence without a good effect; those very verses, as in all probability, and therefore much pressed by some ancients, having much contributed to the conversion of many, to Christ. It is no good argument therefore against the literal sense of this Psalm, that because the Holy Ghost had the managing of the whole business, therefore all must literally be true of Solomon; since that the Holy Ghost had a double intention in it, and as it was intended for a *carmen Genethliacum,* it was sufficient that it fitted then when it was written, according to the nature of the thing, and the present occasion; and was not to be fitted to the event of things in future ages; which would have altered the nature of the thing, and of a *Genethliacum,* have made it a bare prophecy.

But besides, was there not ground for these hyperbolical expressions from God’s own words and expressions concerning Solomon? *I will be his father, and he shall be my son,* &c. *and thine house and thy kingdom shall be established for ever,* 2 Sam. 7.14, &c. To say, that here also Christ was immediately and only intended, were very strange. I know the Apostle does apply some of these words, as well he might, since Solomon was his type, to Christ, Heb. 1.5. But that is no argument to me, that therefore Solomon was not immediately intended. And certainly those words there, *If he commit iniquity,* &c. cannot well be applied to Christ. But that in very deed, both here and there, Solomon was the immediate object of the words, may be here made good, by good and irrefragable authority; by the application that is made of all there, and of many, yea, most things here, to David and his posterity, Psal. 89. by the Holy Ghost, the right author, as here, so there. For there of necessity David, and his posterity, must literally be intended; or else there were no ground for the ensuing expostulation, *But thou hast cast off,* &c. Except there also men will take the liberty to turn all into allegories, to the destruction of the letter, and make the sufferings of Christ his Church, to be the immediate object of those complaints. But that no sober rational man will do, or be persuaded unto by others, in these days. If some of the ancients have done it, it may the better be borne with in them. It was the way then in request, when the literal sense, for want of that knowledge of tongues, which has been since in the world, was more mystical and hidden, than that we properly call mystical, to expound all things allegorically.

In the last place, for another respect too, I say it is no good consequence to argue from the event, against the literal sense. God had once passed his promise to Eli’s forefathers, that the Priesthood should continue in their family for ever; that is, as long as the Temple itself; and for so long as that hierarchy was to continue. It fell out otherwise. How happened that? God himself will tell us; *I said indeed that thy house, and the house of thy father should walk before me for ever: but now the Lord saith; Be it far from me. For they that honour me, I will honour,* &c. 1 Sam. 2.30. See also Ezek. 33.13. *When I shall say to the righteous, that he shall surely live,* &c. And was not this Solomon’s case? What could be done more by any man, to frustrate the promises of God, than was done by Solomon? That he repented, and found mercy in his own person, I will easily believe, though not obliged by any direct Scriptures that I know of: but if he did repent, it was after he had done enough and more than enough, to bring the curse of God upon all his posterity. David himself, notwithstanding God’s promises so often reiterated, and so absolutely passed unto him, proposed them in his last words to his son Solomon conditionally; *And keep the charge of the Lord thy God to walk in his ways, — that thou mayest prosper in all thou dost, — That the Lord may continue his word which he spake concerning me, saying, If thy children take heed to their ways, to walk before me in truth,* &c. 1 Kin. 4.3,4. And so Psal. 132.12. *If thy children,* &c. All these things well considered, the literal sense I hope will not be denied us; which needs not much other illustration, than the justifying of the hyperboles of it: which has been our chiefest aim and business hitherto. And as for the mystical sense, as the words are applicable to Christ, there is nothing of which any man can make any question: what of Solomon immediately and literally, but hyperbolically, was intended; it is exactly applicable to Christ, without any hyperbole: and in that respect more literally and truly, than to Solomon; but in a spiritual sense though, most of it; though that too, not without some literal and historical accomplishment, as may appear by particulars.

**Verse 1. ***{Give the King thy judgments}* Endue the King with the spirit of wisdom and justice, that he reign not, as do worldly tyrants.

*{unto the king’s son}* To wit, to his posterity.

**V. 3. ***{the mountains shall bring peace to the people}* Even the places most barren shall be enriched with your blessings, v. 16. comprised under the name of peace: or, the mountains, that is, great men, as Zach. 4.7. shall procure prosperity to the state by righteous judgment. Of Solomon’s days, the Scripture notes expressly, that all his time, *Judah and Israel dwelt safely,* (or confidently) *every man under his vine, and under his fig-tree, from Dan to Beersheba:* that is, all his kingdom over, 1 King. 4.25. Of Christ, first spiritually, the peace of conscience, the most valuable peace, must be understood: the proper fruit of his Gospel: *For the kingdom of God, is not meat and drink; but righteousness and peace, and joy of the Holy Ghost,* Rom. 14.17. Secondly, literally; when Christ was born, there was peace, an extraordinary peace, all the world over almost, under Augustus, a peaceable just Prince. The Romans then Governors and Masters of greatest part of the world, took such notice of it, that they shut the doors of their Temple Janus: a ceremony that was not used, but when they had a general peace: and happened not in all their time, above twice or thrice at most: *Sic ubique cuncta atque continua totius generi aut pax fuit, aut pactio: aususque tandem Caesar Augustus—Janum Geminum claudere,* &c. says the abridger of the Roman History, L. Florus, l. 4. c. 12. They that note an allusion in the word שלום (*Shalom*) Peace, to the name of Solomon: should if right in this, have taken notice of another in the word יושיע (verse following) *Joshiah* (*he shall save*) to the name Jesus. I think there is as much ground for the one, as for the other: the one belonging to the literal; and the other to the mystical sense: though as Christ is our Peace-maker, and the true Melchisedek, King of Salem, so the first allusion might belong to him also.

*{by righteousness}* Or, *in a just and full measure.* So the word righteousness is used sometimes: as for example, Joel 2.23. *for he hath given you the former rain moderately:* in the Hebrew, as there in the margin is noted, *according to righteousness.*

**V. 4. ***{He shall judge the poor of the people}* He shows wherefore the sword is committed to Kings: to wit, to defend the innocent, and suppress the wicked.

*{He shall judge the poor of the people, &c. and break in pieces the oppressor}* This is again repeated, and more largely prosecuted, v. 12,13,14. which indeed is the highest commendation, and truest character of a noble mind, of nearest relation and resemblance to God himself, to whom the same propriety is often both in the Scriptures, and in the writings of profane authors (as we call them) ascribed. Whence it is, that Virgil, that master-poet, when he extols his Romans (by that general name aiming at his Prince Augustus particularly) to the height of human perfection and magnanimity, after particular enumeration, by way of preamble (to make his speech the more solemn and considerable) of divers trades and professions, and faculties, wherein he willingly grants the Romans to be inferior to some other nations; he concludes his speech with these excellent verses, *Tu regere imperio populos Romane memento: Hae tibi erunt artes; pacique imponere morem, PARCERE subjectis* (which many Princes have made their motto; and happy it were, that all would make it their practice) *& debellare superbos.*

*{the}* Or, *for the,* Psal. 26.1. & 82.3.

**V. 5. ***{They shall fear thee as long as the sun and moon endure}* Whether thee, O God, named in the first v. or thee, O King, by an apostrophe, so called in rhetoric, is ambiguous: but either way it will do well. They shall fear thee, O God: true religion will be the fruit of a settled peace, and just government: first the fruit, then the security; for these two have great dependence one upon another. The fear of God is taken for the whole worship; that is ordinary. Again, they shall fear thee, O King; that is, they shall worship and reverence: *Thy Name shall endure for ever, as long as the sun,* &c. as it is in the 17. verse.

**V. 6. ***{He shall come down like rain upon the mown grass}* As this is true in all godly Kings, so it is chiefly verified in Christ, who with his heavenly dew makes his Church ever to flourish.

*{like rain upon the mown grass}* This similitude, with little alteration, is often used in the Scripture, upon several occasions, as Deut. 32.2. *My doctrine shall drop as the rain;* &c. & Job 29.22,23. *and my speech dropped upon them, and they waited for me, as for the rain,* &c. *The King’s wrath is as the roaring of a lion; but his favour is as dew upon the grass,* Prov. 19.12. and elsewhere: but upon this very occasion of God’s blessing upon David and his posterity, 2 Sam. 23.4. *And he shall be as the light of the morning when the sun riseth, even a morning without clouds: as the tender grass springing out of the earth by clear shining after rain.* The word in the Original גז here translated mown grass, does also signify a fleece of wool (properly, a shorn fleece) which some take to be the proper signification here, in allusion (as they conceive) to Gideon’s fleece, Judg. 6.37, &c. So the Septuagint, καταβήσεται ὡς ἐπὶ πόκον: *in vellus,* in the vulgar Latin. This interpretation is embraced by most of the ancients, and finely prosecuted by Chrysostom in relation to Gideon’s fleece, upon this place. Since the word will bear it, as well as that other sense, good reason we should take notice of it too. But yet I conceive the other to be the more proper signification here; as more agreeable to that which follows, *as showers that water the earth.* Genebrard also, though otherwise prone enough to justify the vulgar Latin, be the mistakes of it never so gross and palpable; yet here he makes no scruple to tell us, what he thinks best.

**V. 7. ***{so long as the moon endureth}* Heb. *till there be no moon.* He mentions here the moon only; as v. 17. the Sun only; having joined both in v. 5. As his fancy led him, and would fit the v. best: and it were but a fancy to make a further mystery of it. Till there be no moon; that is, to the world’s end; or for ever, as elsewhere.

**V. 8. ***{He shall have dominion also from Sea to Sea; and from the river, &c.}* Most interpreters by these words, would have the bounds of Judea, and the Israelites’ Kingdom according to Exod. 23.31. Numbers 34.3, &c. expressed: that is, from the Sea of Sodom, or the Red Sea, to the Mediterranean Sea (by some called *mare Syriacum*) East and West; and from the River, (that is, the River Euphrates, for the greatness and famousness, absolutely called, the River) to the great Desert, North and South. And this they conceive was accomplished in Solomon, of whom the story testifies in these words: *And Solomon reigned over all Kingdoms from the river, unto the land of the Philistines; and unto the border of Egypt, they brought presents and served Solomon all the days of his life,* 1 Kin. 4.24. and again, v. 24. of the same chapter, *For he had dominion over all the region of this side,* &c. For my part, as I believe, that the words had immediate relation to Solomon’s person; so to those quoted places too of the Law concerning the bounds of the Jewish Kingdom, that they have some reference, I will easily grant: especially to Exod. 23.31. which words come nearest to these. Yet when I consider that the words must have reference to Christ too, as all the Psalm has; nay that they are directly so applied, and repeated of Christ, without any alteration at all: (*And I will cut off the chariot,* &c. *and he shall speak peace unto the heathen, and his dominion shall be from Sea even to sea; and from the river, even to the ends of the earth,* Zach. 9.10.) whose dominion (spiritual) to confine within these narrow bounds, would not be so proper, nor so agreeable, either to the predictions of other prophets, or to the event itself: And again, that the words, Exod. 23.31. are not so like, but that some considerable difference may be observed: it being there, *from the red sea, even unto the sea of the Philistines, and from the desert unto the river;* and here more absolutely, *from sea to Sea,* &c. upon these grounds I conceive it more probable, that an universal dominion over all the world is here comprehended and promised. And it may be, *from sea to sea,* is meant, from South to North (for ים is sometimes taken from the South; as Psalm 107.3.) and from the River (Euphrates, which was East of Judea; though according to the opinion of some, we expounded it before, from the River to the desert, to be, North and South) to the ends of the earth (or as the Septuagint, ἕως περάτων τῆς οἰκουμένης: in the vulgar Latin, *usque ad terminos orbis terrarum*) is meant, East and West. Now if it be opposed, that this was never true of Solomon; we answer as before, upon the title; first, that the expression, as is usual in these cases, is hyperbolical: secondly, that the question is not properly, what Solomon did actually enjoy; but what either by God might be promised conditionally; (neither did the Israelitish Kingdom ever extend so far, as those bounds assigned to it by God, in the Law, *propter desidiam ac peccata;* as St. Jerome in a place) or by the Poet, or poetical author (the immediate we mean) might be wished unto him without trespassing against the laws of that *decorum,* or beseemingness, (always grounding upon some apparent possibility, if not probability) to which all Poets and Orators are equally bound.

**V. 9. ***{his enemies shall lick the dust}* Bow to the ground in sign of reverence, Isa. 49.23.

**V. 10. ***{The Kings of Tarshish}* Of Cilicia, and of all other countries beyond the Sea, which he means by the Isles, Psal. 48.

*{shall bring presents}* Psal. 68.29, &c.

*{the Kings of Sheba and Seba}* Seba and Sheba, the one, the son; the other, the nephew of Cush, the son of Cham, Gen. 10.6,7. From these, and their posterity, these places had their denomination; but what places are intended by these two names, is not agreed upon among Expositors. Some refer both to some regions of Arabia. Others understand Arabia and Ethiopia, which I think more likely: some parts, at least. Now concerning Solomon, of the Queen of Sheba, what she did in reverence and admiration of his wisdom and high renown, we read particularly, 1 King. 10. chap. There also, v. 15. of the Kings of Arabia it is recorded, in these words, *Besides that he had of the merchant men—and of all the Kings of Arabia,* &c.

**V. 11. ***{Yea all Kings shall fall down before him; all nations shall serve him}* An hyperbolical and poetical amplification: of which kind has been spoken already sufficiently. Neither need the words be pressed too rigidly. By all Kings, may be understood many, which were either absolute subjects, or tributary. To which purpose we read, 1 Kin. 4.21. *And Solomon reigned over all kingdoms,* &c. *they brought presents and served Solomon all the days of his life.* Besides, this falling down, may be understood more generally too, of extraordinary respects done unto him by sundry Kings and Princes, voluntarily; by their Legates and Ambassadors: as one speaks of the Romans under Augustus; *Illi quoque reliqui, qui immunes imperii erant, sentiebant tamen magnitudinem, & reverebantur.* All nations shall serve him: many did, over whom he had either absolute power and dominion; or conditional: by tributes, &c. of others, he did serve himself, by trade and commerce, transporting their wares and commodities by ships and otherwise, into his own Kingdom for his use: a great argument of a happy reign, and flourishing Kingdom. Now of Christ (of whom, as before we said, chiefly intended) all this has been verified without an hyperbole: yet not of him neither, so absolutely and precisely, without some figure; but such as is so ordinary, as that it may seem rather proper language, than figurative. All Kings, that is, some Kings of all Nations: and all nations shall serve him; which has already been fulfilled, of most: and we expect shall be verified of all in God’s appointed time, before the end of the world. By what we have already seen, we may easily believe that which is to be.

**V. 12. ***{For he shall deliver the needy when he crieth}* Job 29.12.

*{For he shall deliver the needy, &c.}* See before upon v. 4. *He shall judge the poor of the people.* Now this might seem (as in very deed it is objected by some) not to agree so well with that complaint of the people, concerning Solomon, 1 Kings 12.4. *Thy father hath made our yoke grievous:* but that is there accorded, not as a complaint of the people only: which whether it proceeded from too much happiness under Solomon (for that will do it, as well as the other; such is the nature of man) or from any real suffering, is left doubtful. In the History I am sure we find ample testimony concerning their great happiness under him: and among other things, this particularly, which comprehends a great deal; *That of the children of Israel Solomon did make no bondmen,* 1 Kings 9.22. But of their sufferings by him, nothing do I find. It may be that in this also Solomon was a type of Christ, who though he say of himself, that *his yoke is easy and his burden light,* Matthew 11.30, yet there be many that think far otherwise of it: else wickedness would not abound so much among Christians.

**V. 14. ***{He shall redeem their soul from deceit and violence}* Fraud and violence, the two noted engines of all mischief, the effects whereof none commonly feel more, than they that are best able to bear it, the poor and needy. It is the case of many great and flourishing kingdoms: where the better sort are most at ease, the poorer people are most oppressed. *Go to now ye rich men,* &c. *Behold the hire of the labourers,* &c. *Ye have condemned and killed the just,* &c. James 5.1, 4, 6. As it is therefore the commendation of a good King, not to oppress, himself: so of a King both good and wise, to prevent the oppression of others. The Hebrew word תוך is by the Septuagint, because of the affinity of the sound, (as in many other words) translated τόκος; which signifies usury; and the Old Latin out of the Greek, expresses it, *Ex usuris & iniquitate redimet animas eorum:* that is, say they, he shall redeem their souls from usury and oppression: for so is *iniquitas,* here to be taken. It is likely they thought the word τόκος had its beginning from the Hebrew תוך: which is not impossible, though from τίκτω be the more obvious derivation. And certainly there is a great deal of reason to think that איש תככים, Prov. 29.13, does signify an usurer: of which see more there. However, since fraud, and cruelty, and oppression are so incidental, and almost inseparable from that kind of life, the expression cannot be much amiss: and what a work the ancient Romans had in their time, to deliver their poor from the cruelty and tyranny of the *feneratores,* their Histories will show.

*{precious shall their blood be in his sight}* He will see that none of his subjects, not the meanest of them, shall lose his life by any wrong judgment at home: nor will he be prodigal of their blood, (as Kings and States many times are: a thing much to be lamented) in services abroad, not for the good, or safety (though commonly pretended) of the Commonwealth, but only to serve his own ambitious and covetous ends, or to revenge private quarrels. See also upon Psalm 116.15. *precious in the sight.*

**V. 15. ***{shall be given}* Heb. *one shall give*.

*{of the gold of Sheba}* God will prosper his life, and also cause people to bring rich presents to him.

**V. 16. ***{There shall be an handful of corn}* If a little corn be sown in the most barren places, it shall grow so plentifully, that it shall shake, as the trees of Lebanon do, with the wind.

*{the fruit thereof shall shake like Lebanon}* Under such a king shall be exceeding great plenty, both of fruit, and also of the increase of mankind, Jer. 31.27.

**V. 17. ***{his name shall endure for ever}* By a continual succession of children, for so the Hebrew word signifies.

*{shall endure}* Heb. *shall be*.

*{his name shall be continued as long as the sun}* Heb. *shall be as a son to continue his father's name for ever*.

*{and men shall be blessed in him}* They shall count themselves happy in such a king, and expect all comforts from him, Prov. 31.28. Fulfilled chiefly in Christ, Gen. 12.3.

**V. 18. ***{who only doth wondrous things}* Psal. 136.4. He confesses that except God miraculously preserve his people, neither the king, nor the kingdom can continue.

**V. 20. ***{The prayers of David the son of Jesse are ended}* This was his last prayer that he made for Solomon, a little before his death: or, the last of those Psalms that David set in order before his death, for the use of the Church. The rest were gathered together by others, as some of Solomon's Proverbs were, Proverbs 25.1. So 2 Samuel 23.1. *Now these be the last words of David:* not the last words that ever he spoke, (for he both spoke and did many things after that,) but the last that he spoke as a Prophet, to his son Solomon, as learned men expound it there: and some make those words there, an appendix as it were of this Psalm here. From these words most collect that David was the author of the Psalms: as it is very probable, if we take this note as referring, not to the foregoing Psalms in general; whereof this is the 72 in number, and (according to that subdivision we have spoken of upon the first Psalm, in the beginning: and upon the 41 in the end) ends the second book of Psalms: but to this particular Psalm, and therefore taken into the body of it, as part of it. Yet there be, who think David might utter the matter of it upon his deathbed, by way of solemn benediction, in imitation of Jacob, Gen. 49, and Moses, Deut. 32, 33. David, in prose, and Solomon afterwards put it into verse: and therefore both their names put to it, the one at the end, the other at the beginning. But if as others, the note refer to the whole collection of the foregoing Psalms (he called prayers, by a *synecdoche partis pro toto,* as they call it) the argument will not be of so much force. Some Rabbins have made this sense of the words, *Consummatae sunt preces David;* that is, say they: This is the sum of David's wishes, in the accomplishment whereof he shall have his heart's desire. I mention it, not for the probability, but because some of the ancient Expositors have somewhat to that purpose too.
